Sharp thresholds for Escobar and Gagliardo-Nirenberg functionals: the Escobar-Willmore mass, geometric selection, and compactness trichotomy
Mayukh Mukherjee and Utsab Sarkar

Abstract
. We develop a unified quantitative framework for sharp threshold phenomena in boundary–critical variational problems on compact Riemannian manifolds with boundary, covering both the conformally covariant Escobar quotient and a Dirichlet Gagliardo–Nirenberg family. Via a transfer–stability–reduction scheme (chart–scale near–isometries plus quantitative stability), we obtain attainment–versus–bubbling alternatives, H 1 H^{1} –compactness results in sign–preserving neighborhoods, and explicit finite–dimensional reductions under separation/balance regimes. A distinctive boundary feature is geometric selection governed not only by mean curvature H g H_{g} but also by a universal Willmore–type anisotropy channel from | II ̊ | 2 |\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2} . At the hemisphere threshold S ∗ = C Esc ∗ ​ ( 𝕊 + n ) S_{\ast}=C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(\mathbb{S}^{n}_{+}) and in dimensions n ≥ 5 n\geq 5 (on the refined stratum H g ≡ 0 H_{g}\equiv 0 where the ε 2 \varepsilon^{2} coefficient is intrinsic), we identify a renormalized second–order boundary mass ℜ g \mathfrak{R}_{g} with the decomposition ℜ g = κ 1 ​ ( n ) ​ Ric g ⁡ ( ν , ν ) + κ 2 ​ ( n ) ​ Scal g | ∂ M + κ 3 ​ ( n ) ​ | II ̊ | 2 , κ 3 ​ ( n ) < 0 . \mathfrak{R}_{g}=\kappa_{1}(n)\,\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu)+\kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}+\kappa_{3}(n)\,|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2},\qquad\kappa_{3}(n)<0. Together with the first and third coefficients ρ n conf ​ H g \rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g} and Θ g \Theta_{g} (for n ≥ 6 n\geq 6 ), this yields uniform one–bubble expansions and “energy–only” estimators for H g H_{g} and ℜ g \mathfrak{R}_{g} (and Θ g \Theta_{g} when available). We prove a near–threshold alternative: if the first nonvanishing coefficient among { ρ n conf ​ H g , ℜ g , Θ g } \{\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g},\mathfrak{R}_{g},\Theta_{g}\} is negative somewhere, then C Esc ∗ ​ ( M , g ) < S ∗ C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)\hskip-2.84526pt<\hskip-2.84526ptS_{\ast} and minimizing/Palais–Smale sequences are H 1 H^{1} –precompact. At C Esc ∗ ​ ( M , g ) = S ∗ C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast} , blow–up of positive constrained critical points is modeled by boundary bubbles and concentrates at critical points of H g H_{g} ; on H g ≡ 0 H_{g}\equiv 0 , stationarity forces ℜ g ​ ( p ) = 0 \mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0 and ∇ ∂ ℜ g ​ ( p ) = 0 \nabla_{\partial}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0 at limiting centers. In particular, if H g H_{g} is Morse and ℜ g > 0 \mathfrak{R}_{g}>0 at all critical points of H g H_{g} , then no threshold bubbling occurs. In the multi–bubble regime an explicit boundary interaction kernel appears; in n ≥ 5 n\geq 5 it is lower order at the ε 2 \varepsilon^{2} level on collision–free sets, so center dynamics decouple and are governed by 𝒲 k ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∑ i = 1 k ℜ g ​ ( x i ) \mathcal{W}_{k}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i}) , producing k k –bubble critical points at infinity at the quantized levels k 1 / ( n − 1 ) ​ S ∗ k^{1/(n-1)}S_{\ast} (while n = 4 n=4 requires logarithmic renormalization and coupled interactions). In the fully degenerate case where the organizing invariants vanish in a boundary collar and an extremal exists at S ∗ S_{\ast} , we obtain local conformal hemispherical rigidity under standard geometric rigidity hypotheses. The parallel Gagliardo–Nirenberg track yields analogous threshold dichotomies with first-order curvature selection and resolves a question of [ 18 ] (also see [ 7 ] ): the sharp GN constant on manifolds with small Dirichlet windows diverges at the optimal capacitary rate, quantitatively relating the threshold to spectral and isoperimetric invariants while precluding certain geometric degenerations near threshold. Applications include entropy production inequalities for fast diffusion with explicit geometric rate constants, curvature-driven selection of focusing NLS ground states, topological obstructions to attainment, and (in dimension n = 2 n=2 ) recovery of the Euler characteristic from GN measurements alone.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
53C21, 35B44, 35J25.

1.
Introduction

Sharp critical inequalities on manifolds with boundary exhibit two persistent and tightly coupled features:

(i)
a universal threshold dictated by the model geometry (the Euclidean half-space / round hemisphere), and

(ii)
a
geometric selection
mechanism (encoded in curvature and mean curvature) that governs
where concentration occurs and whether compactness can fail.
This paper develops a unified
transfer–quantization–reduction
framework that captures both phenomena
in a quantitative way, simultaneously for a Dirichlet Gagliardo–Nirenberg family (including sharp boundary trace
quotients) and for the
conformally covariant
Escobar functional.

On the Escobar side, our goal is a near-threshold
closure package
: a quantitative description of
(i)
what
bubbling profiles can occur (quantization / Struwe decomposition),
(ii)
where
bubbling must occur (selection laws for centers and scales),
and (iii)
how
the near-threshold variational landscape behaves (finite-dimensional reduction,
multiplicity, and dynamical control near bubbling).
A central theme is that, once one works
on the constraint slice
, in the positive sector the only obstruction is boundary bubbling modeled on
U_{+}
(see (
2.6
)); in general, bubbles are half-space critical points.

Slice viewpoint and the organizing coefficients.

The slice formulation isolates the genuinely noncompact directions: boundary bubbles and their modulation parameters.
Our quantitative package is organized by the first three coefficients in the one-bubble expansion on the slice,

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}\quad(\text{first order}),\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{g}\quad(\text{second order, }n\geq 5),\qquad\Theta_{g}\quad(\text{third order, }n\geq 6),

together with an explicit boundary interaction kernel
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
in the multi-bubble regime.
These objects drive both (a) obstruction/compactness at and below the hemisphere threshold, and (b) the
finite-dimensional reduction underlying multiplicity via critical points at infinity.

Escobar functional (covariant).

Let
(M^{n},g)
be a smooth compact Riemannian manifold with smooth boundary
\partial M
.
We consider the canonical conformal pair

(1.1)

L_{g}^{\circ}:=-\Delta_{g}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}\mathrm{Scal}_{g},\qquad B_{g}^{\circ}:=-\partial_{\nu}+\frac{n-2}{2}H_{g},\qquad q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2},

and the Escobar quotient

(1.2)

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u]:=\frac{\displaystyle\int_{M}u\,L_{g}^{\circ}u\,dV_{g}+\int_{\partial M}u\,B_{g}^{\circ}u\,d\sigma_{g}}{\Big(\displaystyle\int_{\partial M}|u|^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}\Big)^{2/q}},\qquad u\in H^{1}(M)\setminus\{0\}.

We write

(1.3)

C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g):=\inf_{u\not\equiv 0}\mathcal{J}_{g}[u],\qquad S_{\ast}:=C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(S^{n}_{+},g_{\mathrm{round}}).

On the constraint slice

\mathcal{S}:=\Big\{u\in H^{1}(M):\ \int_{\partial M}|u|^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}=1\Big\},

the quotient reduces to its numerator:

\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}}=\mathcal{E}_{g}
.
All reductions and differentiations below are carried out in sign-preserving neighborhoods of
positive functions in
\mathcal{S}
.

Remark 1.1

(Mean curvature convention)
.

We use the
inward
-pointing unit normal
\nu
on
\partial M
and the convention

\mathrm{II}(X,Y)=-\langle\nabla_{X}\nu,Y\rangle_{g},\qquad H_{g}=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\mathrm{II}.

With this choice, the Euclidean unit ball has
H_{g}=n-1>0
on its boundary.

Standing analytic hypotheses.

We assume a strict coercivity condition (Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
) (so the Escobar graph form controls
\|u\|_{H^{1}}
)
and bounded-geometry hypotheses (BG
m+
) at the order required by the expansions and reductions below.

1.1.
Main results for the Escobar problem

Theorem A

(Global compactness at Escobar multiples; equal-mass quantization)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
2+
)
and fix
n\geq 3
.
Let
(u_{\ell})\subset\mathcal{S}
be a positive Palais–Smale sequence for
\mathcal{E}_{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
with

\mathcal{E}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\ \longrightarrow\ k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\,S_{\ast}\;=\;k^{\frac{1}{n-1}}\,S_{\ast}\qquad(\ell\to\infty)

for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, up to a subsequence,
u_{\ell}
admits a Struwe-type decomposition into
a (possibly trivial) Escobar solution
u_{\infty}
, finitely many boundary bubbles with the usual Struwe separation/orthogonality (in the isolated-simple regime one can choose pure separation; i.e. no towers),
and a remainder
w_{\ell}\to 0
in
H^{1}(M)
.
Moreover, the boundary
L^{q}
–masses of the bubbles are
quantized and equal
:
each bubble carries mass
1/k
, and therefore the number of bubbles satisfies
J\leq k
, with
J=k
whenever
u_{\infty}\equiv 0
.

Theorem B

(Hemisphere threshold
S_{\ast}
: classification, selection, and compactness)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
.
Let
(u_{\ell})
be smooth positive solutions of the Escobar Euler–Lagrange equation, normalized by
\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
,
with
\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)
.
If
(u_{\ell})
blows up, then necessarily
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
, and after extraction there is a single-bubble
decomposition

u_{\ell}=\mathcal{U}_{x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell}}+w_{\ell},\qquad\|w_{\ell}\|_{H^{1}(M)}\to 0,\qquad\varepsilon_{\ell}\downarrow 0.

The bubble center satisfies the leading-order selection law

\|\nabla H_{g}(x_{\ell})\|\ \lesssim\ \varepsilon_{\ell},\qquad\text{hence }x_{\ell}\to p\in\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g}),

and, moreover, bubbling forces
H_{g}(p)=0
.

Assume in addition
n\geq 5
and the refined second-order regime holds in a fixed collar around the blow-up set
(so that the cutoff-independent
\varepsilon^{2}
coefficient
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is defined there).
If
H_{g}\equiv 0
on the collar containing the blow-up set, then the next-order selection becomes

\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nabla\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0,

so truly threshold bubbling must concentrate at points where both the first- and second-order obstructions vanish.

Finally, if
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
,
n\geq 5
,
H_{g}
is Morse, and

\min_{p\in\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})}\ \mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)\ >\ 0,

then no bubbling can occur at the hemisphere threshold: every sequence of constrained positive critical points with

\mathcal{E}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to S_{\ast}
is precompact in
H^{1}(M)
.

Theorem C

(Reduction at higher levels: interaction kernel and center-only potential)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
and work in the standard separation regime.
The Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction produces a finite-dimensional reduced functional

\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
whose pairwise interaction is governed by an explicit
boundary kernel
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
(a renormalized boundary-to-boundary Green kernel for the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map).
The scaling of this interaction yields a sharp dimension split at next order:

•

in
n=4
, the interaction term is of the same order as the
\varepsilon^{2}
self-information (modulo logarithmic renormalization), producing a coupled center–interaction potential;

•

in
n\geq 5
, the interaction contribution is lower order at the
\varepsilon^{2}
level on collision-free sets, so the
next-order center selection decouples and is governed by the renormalized mass
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
.

In dimensions
n\geq 5
, assuming
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
, nondegenerate positive critical points of the resulting center-only potential

\mathcal{W}_{k}(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})

produce
k
-bubble
critical points at infinity
(positive Palais–Smale sequences with vanishing scales). In the Morse setting this yields
multiplicity results based on the topology of the configuration space of the boundary.

Model-free observability from isotropic probes.

Beyond qualitative selection, our expansions lead to a quantitative inverse principle: the geometric coefficients

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}
,
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
, and (when available)
\Theta_{g}
can be recovered
numerically
from
quotient energies of canonical isotropic boundary bubbles. Concretely, writing

v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
and
E(x,\varepsilon):=\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]-S_{\ast}
,
one obtains an estimator

\widehat{H}_{g}(x)=\frac{E(x,\varepsilon)}{S_{\ast}\,\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,\varepsilon}

with uniform error
|\widehat{H}_{g}(x)-H_{g}(x)|\lesssim\varepsilon
on compact collars, and a three-scale de-biasing of the
energies
E(x,k\varepsilon)
recovers
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
(and
\Theta_{g}
in
n\geq 6
) with quantitative rates.
This converts the near-threshold asymptotics into a robust “energy-only” measurement rule.

New geometric coefficients and the Willmore channel.

The one-bubble expansion introduces three boundary invariants that organize the near-threshold landscape:

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}\quad(\text{first order}),\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{g}\quad(\text{second order, }n\geq 5),\qquad\Theta_{g}\quad(\text{third order, }n\geq 6).

A key structural identity is the Escobar–Willmore splitting of the second-order coefficient (valid for
n\geq 5
):

\mathfrak{R}_{g}=\kappa_{1}(n)\,\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu)\;+\;\kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}\;+\;\kappa_{3}(n)\,|\mathring{\II}|^{2},\qquad\kappa_{3}(n)<0.

The strict sign
\kappa_{3}(n)<0
shows that the anisotropy channel
|\mathring{\II}|^{2}
enters with a universal negative weight:
boundary shear lowers the energy at the first nontrivial order on the
H\equiv 0
stratum.
This yields, for example, a quantitative positive-mass barrier excluding one-bubble threshold blow-up in collars where

H_{g}\equiv 0
and
\inf\mathfrak{R}_{g}>0
, and a sharp subcriticality criterion when
\mathfrak{R}_{g}<0
somewhere.

Quantitative control near bubbling and dynamics.

We complement the reduction with two analytic tools on the constraint slice:
(i) a quantitative reduced-gradient principle near the normalized boundary bubble manifold,
which identifies the constrained first variation in terms of modulation parameters and defect coordinates,
and (ii) a Łojasiewicz–Simon inequality near any
positive
constrained critical point.
Together these yield convergence (and rates) for the constrained gradient flow in neighborhoods where the flow remains
sign-preserving.

Generic threshold compactness.

Finally, for
n\geq 5
we show that for a
C^{m}
-open dense set of metrics satisfying (Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
) and (BG
3+
),
the mean curvature is Morse and the renormalized mass is nonzero at every critical point of
H_{g}
.
If in addition
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}>0
at these critical points, then constrained critical points are compact at
the hemisphere threshold.

1.2.
Context and related work

The study of critical Neumann problems on manifolds with boundary was initiated by Cherrier
[
16
]
, who established sharp trace inequalities and early existence results for prescribed curvature problems with critical boundary nonlinearity. Escobar
[
26
,
27
]
reformulated the problem in conformally covariant terms, identified the hemisphere as the sharp model geometry, and proved existence in a broad range of cases via localized test bubbles and positive-mass inputs. The interpolation between interior Sobolev and boundary trace inequalities was further clarified by Maggi–Neumayer
[
42
]
. Subsequent work by Marques
[
43
]
, Almaraz
[
1
]
, Brendle–Chen
[
10
]
, and Mayer–Ndiaye
[
44
]
, among others, completed the existence theory in full generality.

On the compactness side, foundational results were obtained by Han–Li
[
33
]
. Almaraz
[
20
]
established compactness when the trace-free second fundamental form is nowhere vanishing, with extensions to low dimensions by Almaraz–de Queiroz–Wang
[
2
]
and Kim–Musso–Wei
[
37
]
; Disconzi–Khuri
[
23
]
gave a systematic treatment of compactness versus blow-up more broadly; the effect of linear perturbations of mean curvature was studied by Ghimenti–Micheletti–Pistoia
[
32
]
. The topological approach to multiplicity via critical points at infinity, pioneered by Bahri–Coron
[
6
]
, and the Lyapunov–Schmidt framework for critical equations
[
3
]
, have natural extensions to the boundary setting. However, a quantitative near-threshold description for the
covariant
Escobar quotient—including a Struwe decomposition
[
54
]
at the Escobar multiples (see also the pointwise refinements in
[
24
]
), explicit geometric selection laws for centers and scales, and a reduction package yielding multiplicity in dimensions
n\geq 5
—has remained substantially less developed. Our results provide such a package in a unified framework, organized by the explicit coefficients
(\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g},\mathfrak{R}_{g},\Theta_{g})
and the boundary interaction kernel
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
.

1.3.
Synopsis: GN (Dirichlet) side

Let

W_{M}(u)=\frac{\|u\|_{L^{p+1}(M)}^{p+1}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{\alpha}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{\beta}},\qquad\alpha=2-\frac{(n-2)(p-1)}{2},\quad\beta=\frac{n(p-1)}{2},

for
n\geq 3
and
1<p<\frac{n+2}{n-2}
, and set

C_{\ast}\;:=\;\sup_{\,u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|u\|_{L^{p+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p+1}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\alpha}\,\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\beta}}\;=\;\sup_{\,u\in H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|u\|_{L^{p+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}^{p+1}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}^{\alpha}\,\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}^{\beta}}.

Theorem D

(GN sharp threshold, quantitative stability, and first–order selection)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be smooth compact with nonempty boundary,
n\geq 3
, and
1<p<\frac{n+2}{n-2}
. For

W_{M}(u)=\frac{\|u\|_{L^{p+1}(M)}^{p+1}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{\alpha}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{\beta}},\qquad\alpha=2-\tfrac{(n-2)(p-1)}{2},\quad\beta=\tfrac{n(p-1)}{2},

set
C_{\ast}=\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}W_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}W_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}
. Then:

(i)

(Threshold/attainment vs. bubbling)

W_{M}^{\sup}\geq C_{\ast}
. If
W_{M}^{\sup}>C_{\ast}
the supremum is
attained
by some
u\in H^{1}_{0}(M)
. If
W_{M}^{\sup}=C_{\ast}
every maximizing sequence either converges (after modulation) or
bubbles off
a single Euclidean/half-space optimizer; on bounded
\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
there is non-attainment at level
C_{\ast}
.

(ii)

(Quantitative
H^{1}
-stability via transfer; absorbed error)
There exist
c_{n,p}>0
and
C_{\mathrm{geo}}(M,g)>0
such that for every
u\in H^{1}_{0}(M)
with
\|u\|_{2}=\|\nabla u\|_{2}=1
there is a best-fit intrinsic bubble
v^{\sharp}=a\,\Pi_{x,\varepsilon}Q
with

\|u-v^{\sharp}\|_{H^{1}(M)}^{2}\ \leq\ c_{n,p}^{-1}\Big(C_{\ast}-\|u\|_{p+1}^{p+1}\Big)+C_{\mathrm{geo}}\times\begin{cases}\varepsilon^{2},&x\in M^{\circ},\\
\varepsilon,&x\in\partial M.\end{cases}

At the best scale the geometric error is absorbed; in particular

\mathrm{dist}_{H^{1}(M)}(u,\text{\emph{one bubble}})\ \lesssim\ \big(C_{\ast}-\|u\|_{p+1}^{p+1}\big)^{1/2}.

(iii)

(First–order geometric selection with explicit coefficients)
For the intrinsic bubble
u_{x,\varepsilon}=\Pi_{x,\varepsilon}Q
(interior) or
u_{x,\varepsilon}=\Pi_{x,\varepsilon}Q_{+}
(boundary) one has

W_{M}(u_{x,\varepsilon})=\ C_{\ast}\Big(1-\kappa^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,p}\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2})\Big)\quad(x\in M^{\circ}),

W_{M}(u_{x,\varepsilon})=\ C_{\ast}\Big(1+\kappa^{\mathrm{bdy}}_{n,p}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon)\Big)\quad(x\in\partial M),

with
explicit
moment formulas for
\kappa^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,p}
and
\kappa^{\mathrm{bdy}}_{n,p}
. Hence the signs of
\kappa^{\mathrm{int}}_{n,p}
and
\kappa^{\mathrm{bdy}}_{n,p}
select minima vs. maxima of
\mathrm{Scal}_{g}
/
H_{g}
as preferred concentration centers.

Theorem E

(GN near–iff at the threshold; spectral/isoperimetric control)
.

Let
Y_{\mathrm{GN}}=W_{M}^{\sup}/C_{\ast}=1+\delta
and
\beta=\tfrac{n(p-1)}{2}
. In the bounded–geometry regime there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending only on
(n,p)
and the geometric bounds of
(M,g)
such that, for
\delta
sufficiently small,

c_{1}\,\lambda_{1}(M)^{-\frac{2}{\beta}}\ \leq\ \delta\ \leq\ c_{2}\,\lambda_{1}(M)^{-\frac{2}{\beta}},\qquad c_{1}\,h(M)^{-\frac{1}{\beta}}\ \leq\ \delta\ \leq\ c_{2}\,h(M)^{-\frac{1}{\beta}}.

In particular, uniform spectral/isoperimetric lower bounds force
Y_{\mathrm{GN}}\downarrow 1
. Conversely, if
M=N\setminus\overline{D}
with a small Dirichlet window
D\Subset N
and
n\geq 3
, then
\lambda_{1}(M)\asymp\mathrm{Cap}_{g}(D)/\mathrm{Vol}(N)
and hence

W_{M}^{\sup}\ \gtrsim\ \lambda_{1}(M)^{-\beta/2}\ \asymp\ \mathrm{Cap}_{g}(D)^{-\beta/2}\ \longrightarrow\ \infty\quad\text{as}\ \mathrm{Cap}_{g}(D)\downarrow 0.

1.4.
Context and related work

In the Euclidean theory, the delicate interplay between domain geometry and critical Sobolev nonlinearities was revealed by Brezis–Nirenberg
[
12
]
, who showed that existence in bounded domains depends sensitively on dimension and lower-order terms. Sharp constants and optimizers for the Gagliardo-Nirenberg (GN) family go back to
Weinstein
[
58
]
and the Del Pino-Dolbeault program
[
21
]
.
Mass-transport and spectral methods were developed in
[
19
]
, while Bianchi-Egnell

[
8
]
established the seminal quadratic stability for the critical Sobolev inequality.
Quantitative GN/Sobolev stability was advanced in
[
14
,
48
,
9
,
28
,
22
]
. Profile decompositions at critical scales originate in
Lions’ concentration-compactness and were formalized in
[
50
,
31
,
53
,
57
]
.

On negatively curved backgrounds, Banica analyzed dispersive concentration and enhanced Strichartz estimates
for NLS on
\mathbb{H}^{n}
and raised the natural question whether curvature can change variational thresholds
(sharp GN/Weinstein functional) versus the Euclidean case
[
7
]
. In parallel,
Christianson-Metcalfe-Marzuola-Taylor studied ground states on
weakly homogeneous spaces
(including

\mathbb{H}^{n}
) and conjectured that the Weinstein functional on
\mathbb{H}^{n}
has the same sharp value as

\mathbb{R}^{n}
with no maximizer in
H^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{n})

[
18
]
. This conjecture was resolved by the
first author, proving equality of the (fractional) Weinstein sharp constants and non-attainment in

H^{1}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
; the argument extends to the fractional Laplacian
[
47
]
.

1.5.
Guide to the paper

Section
2
establishes the Escobar transfer, local stability and scale law; it proves
the
O(\varepsilon)
boundary selection and the
O(\varepsilon^{2})
one-bubble expansion with
renormalized mass
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
. Section
4
constructs the Lyapunov-Schmidt correction,
the multi-bubble expansion with the boundary interaction kernel
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
, eliminates scales,
and analyzes the center-only potential
\mathcal{W}_{k}
. The compactness, rigidity, and bubbling parts of
Theorem
3.29
are concluded there. Anchored/bi-modal stability and inverse selection
corollaries are recorded where they are used. Section
5
develops the GN track: transfer, stability, first-order selection,
and spectral/capacitary thresholds (with compactness/profiles deferred to appendices). Technical toolkits (charts, Jacobians, moment estimates,
and profile decomposition) are collected in appendices to keep the main line streamlined.

H_{g}
\mathrm{Scal}_{g}
O
P_{+}
P_{-}

Legend at
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S^{*}
(boundary channel)

H_{g}>0
:
compactness
(first-order suffices; scale killed).

H_{g}<0
:
bubbling admissible
(first-order suffices).

\bullet\ P_{+}

H_{g}=0,\ \mathrm{Scal}_{g}>0
:
compactness
(positive/renormalized mass).

\bullet\ P_{-}

H_{g}=0,\ \mathrm{Scal}_{g}<0
:
bubbling
(negative mass).

\bullet\ O\,\,\,

H_{g}=\mathrm{Scal}_{g}=0
(genuine degeneracy):
\Theta_{g}>0

\Rightarrow
compact,
\Theta_{g}<0

\Rightarrow
bubble;

\Theta_{g}=0
+ attainment
\Rightarrow
local hemispherical rigidity.

Balanced scales near
H_{g}\approx 0
: centers satisfy
\nabla\mathcal{W}_{k}=0
. Interior channel may win if its first-order driver is larger.

Figure 1.
Sign diagram with
sufficient
regimes only; all decisions refer to the boundary channel at the hemisphere threshold.

2.
Escobar I: Hemisphere stability, transfer to
(M,g)
and first-order law

Standing dimension.
Throughout this section we assume
n\geq 3
.

Dimension split.

All first–order statements (in particular the coefficient
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
in the
one–bubble expansion and the associated scale–deficit laws) are developed for every
n\geq 3
,
but the analytic mechanisms differ between low and high dimensions.
For
n\geq 4
we compute
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
explicitly from global weighted moments of the
half–space optimizer
U_{+}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
. In dimension
n=3
the corresponding weighted moments
diverge logarithmically, and we
do not
use them; instead we derive the first–order law
and the constant
\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}
on the spherical model
(S^{3}_{+},g_{\mathrm{round}})
,
via the Cayley transform, and then transfer the expansion to
(M,g)
by conformal covariance.
The second– and third–order coefficients
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
and
\Theta_{g}
(renormalized mass
and third–order term) will be used
systematically only for
n\geq 4
. In dimension
n=3

we do not rely on a full renormalized second/third–order Escobar expansion; the results in
Sections
3
–
4
that require
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
or

\Theta_{g}
are therefore stated and used there under the standing convention
n\geq 4
.

Standing assumption on the Escobar functional.

(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
Strict coercivity.

By Green’s identity for the canonical conformal pair (
2.6
), for every

u\in C^{\infty}(M)
one has

\int_{M}u\,L_{g}^{\circ}u\,dV_{g}+\int_{\partial M}u\,B_{g}^{\circ}u\,d\sigma_{g}=\int_{M}\Big(|\nabla u|_{g}^{2}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,u^{2}\Big)\,dV_{g}+\frac{n-2}{2}\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,u^{2}\,d\sigma_{g}.

We
define
the covariant Escobar graph form on
H^{1}(M)
by the right-hand side:

(2.1)

\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u):=\int_{M}\Big(|\nabla u|_{g}^{2}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,u^{2}\Big)\,dV_{g}+\frac{n-2}{2}\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,u^{2}\,d\sigma_{g},\qquad u\in H^{1}(M).

Our standing hypothesis is the strict coercivity

(2.2)

\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u)\ \geq\ c_{Y}\,\|u\|_{H^{1}(M)}^{2}\qquad\forall\,u\in H^{1}(M).

Under (BG
2+
) the coefficients
\mathrm{Scal}_{g},H_{g}
are bounded and the global trace

H^{1}(M)\to L^{2}(\partial M)
is continuous (Lemma
2.2
), hence there is
C<\infty
with

(2.3)

\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u)\ \leq\ C\,\|u\|_{H^{1}(M)}^{2}\qquad\forall\,u\in H^{1}(M).

Consequently
\|u\|_{E,g}:=\sqrt{\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u)}
is equivalent to
\|u\|_{H^{1}(M)}
.

Escobar sharp constant.

Define

(2.4)

C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g):=\inf_{u\in H^{1}(M)\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u)}{\|u\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\partial M)}^{2}},\qquad 2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}.

By (
2.2
) and the global trace inequality

\|u\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\partial M)}\leq C_{\mathrm{tr}}\|u\|_{H^{1}(M)}
(Lemma
2.2
),

(2.5)

C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)\ \geq\ c_{Y}\,C_{\mathrm{tr}}^{-2}\ >\ 0.

Such a condition seems standard in the Yamabe/Escobar community (see for example
[
26
,
27
,
43
,
33
]
) for guaranteeing the well-posedness needed for subsequent stability and blow-up analysis, and also in other areas of conformal geometry/minimal surfaces (for example, positivity of the first Steklov eigenvalue (
\sigma_{1}>0
) in the work of Fraser–Schoen
[
29
]
on minimal surfaces).

Remark 2.1

(Use of coercivity)
.

The coercivity assumption (Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
) is a standard hypothesis ensuring that the
Escobar graph form defines a genuine Hilbert structure equivalent to
H^{1}
.
More precisely, under (Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
) (together with the standing bounded–geometry
assumptions used to control the lower–order terms and the trace), one has the norm equivalence
(cf. Lemma
2.20
(c))

\|u\|_{E,g}^{2}\;\geq\;c_{Y}\,\|u\|_{H^{1}(M)}^{2}.

It is used crucially at the following junctures:

(i)

To establish the fundamental
single-bubble spectral gap
(intrinsic coercivity orthogonal to
kernel modes, Theorem
2.23
), which underpins local stability analyses.

(ii)

To derive the
quantitative stability estimate
relating the
H^{1}
-distance to the bubble manifold
to the energy deficit and scale (Theorem
2.24
).

(iii)

To prove the
scale-deficit laws
by absorbing error terms using quantitative stability
(e.g. Lemma
2.36
, Lemma
2.37
).

(iv)

To ensure
compactness
of Palais–Smale sequences whenever an energy barrier excludes bubbling
(e.g. under positive mass barriers at the hemisphere threshold:
Theorem
4.29
,
Corollary
3.19
,
Corollary
2.45
).

(v)

To justify the
Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction
framework for multi-bubbles, including the uniform
spectral gap on the orthogonal complement and control of the remainder term (Lemma
4.4
).

(vi)

As an ingredient in the
global compactness/Struwe decomposition
for PS sequences
(Theorem
4.19
) and in the dynamical/perturbative tools built on the constrained
gradient framework (e.g. Łojasiewicz–Simon estimates, Theorem
4.35
,
and Morse lifting, Theorem
4.37
).

By contrast, the computation of the
one-bubble expansion coefficients

(
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
for all
n\geq 3
, and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
,
\Theta_{g}
for
n\geq 4
) and their
geometric structure is essentially local (Fermi expansions and properties of the model optimizer),
rather than a global consequence of (Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
).
Similarly, the
local singular structure
of the interaction kernel
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
is local;
however, the
global normalization/existence
of the renormalized inverse kernel is tied to
the spectral assumptions (e.g. (Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
) or an explicit renormalization convention).

Standing assumption on the manifold.

We work on a smooth Riemannian manifold with smooth boundary
(M^{n},g)
of
bounded geometry up to order
k
, denoted (BG
k+
). This means there exist constants
r_{0}>0
and families of numbers
\{C_{j}\}_{0\leq j\leq k-2}
,
\{\widetilde{C}_{j}\}_{0\leq j\leq k-1}
such that:

(i)

(
Uniform radii of charts
) The interior injectivity radius satisfies
\operatorname{inj}(M,g)\geq r_{0}
, and the normal exponential map induces a Fermi collar

\exp^{\perp}:\ \{(x,t)\in T^{\perp}\partial M:\ 0\leq t<r_{0}\}\longrightarrow\{p\in M:\ 0\leq\mathrm{dist}_{g}(p,\partial M)<r_{0}\}

which is a diffeomorphism onto its image. Equivalently, one has geodesic normal charts on
B_{r_{0}}(x)\subset M^{\circ}
and Fermi charts on a boundary collar of width
r_{0}
.

(ii)

(
Uniform curvature bounds
) The Riemann curvature tensor and its covariant derivatives up to order
k\!-\!2
are uniformly bounded:

\sup_{M}\bigl|\nabla^{j}\operatorname{Rm}_{g}\bigr|\ \leq C_{j},\qquad 0\leq j\leq k-2.

(iii)

(
Uniform second-fundamental form bounds
) The second fundamental form
\II
of
\partial M
and its
tangential
covariant derivatives up to order
k\!-\!1
are uniformly bounded:

\sup_{\partial M}\bigl|\nabla^{j}_{\!{}^{\top}}\II\bigr|\ \leq\widetilde{C}_{j},\qquad 0\leq j\leq k-1,

where
\nabla_{\!{}^{\top}}
is the Levi-Civita connection of the induced metric on
\partial M
.

Lemma 2.2

(Global trace under a uniform collar)
.

Assume
(BG
2+
)
. Then there exists
C_{\mathrm{tr}}<\infty
, depending only on the
(BG
2+
)
data, such that for all
u\in H^{1}(M)
,

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\partial M)}\ \leq\ C_{\mathrm{tr}}\,\|u\|_{H^{1}(M)}.

Proof (standard).

Let
\mathrm{Tr}:H^{1}(M)\to H^{1/2}(\partial M)
be the trace operator. Under (BG
2+
) (uniform collar charts and
uniform
C^{2}
control of the metric), the usual localization-to-Fermi-charts argument and the Euclidean half-space trace
estimate yield a uniform constant
C
such that

\|\mathrm{Tr}\,u\|_{H^{1/2}(\partial M)}\leq C\,\|u\|_{H^{1}(M)}\qquad\forall\,u\in H^{1}(M).

Since
\dim(\partial M)=n-1
, the Sobolev embedding
H^{1/2}(\partial M)\hookrightarrow L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\partial M)
gives

\|u\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\partial M)}\leq C\,\|u\|_{H^{1/2}(\partial M)}.

Also
H^{1/2}(\partial M)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\partial M)
, hence
\|u\|_{L^{2}(\partial M)}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{1/2}(\partial M)}
.
Combining these inequalities yields the claim with
C_{\mathrm{tr}}=C^{2}
.
∎

Under (BG
k+
) the metric coefficients
g_{ab}
in any such normal/Fermi chart have all partial derivatives up to order
k
uniformly bounded (uniform
C^{k}
control of metric jets).

Lemma 2.3

(Uniform
C^{k}
Fermi jets from (BG
k+
))
.

Under
(BG
k+
)
, the metric coefficients in any Fermi chart on the collar
\{0\leq t<r_{0}\}
possess uniform
C^{k}
bounds (in tangential variables and in the normal variable
t
), with constants depending only on the
(BG
k+
)
data. In particular, all mixed normal/tangential derivatives of total order
\leq k
are uniformly bounded.

Proof sketch.

Use Gauss-Codazzi and the Riccati ODE
\partial_{t}\II=-\II^{2}-\mathrm{Rm}(\nu,\cdot,\nu,\cdot)
to control normal derivatives from curvature bounds in (ii), while tangential derivatives are controlled by (iii). A standard bootstrap yields the stated
C^{k}
bounds.
∎

Order bookkeeping.
The distinction between (BG
k+
) and (BG
(k+1)+
) is one of available derivatives: (BG
(k+1)+
) adds one more bounded curvature derivative (up to order
k\!-\!1
) and one more bounded tangential derivative of
\II
(up to order
k
), hence promotes uniform
C^{k}
to uniform
C^{k+1}
control of the metric jets in local coordinates. In particular, to produce an
O(\varepsilon^{m})
coefficient in the small-radius Fermi/normal expansion with a uniform
O(\varepsilon^{m+1})
remainder, one assumes (BG
(m+1)+
).

Thresholds used in this paper.

•

(BG
2+
) (uniform
C^{2}
control of
g
and a fixed Fermi collar) suffices for all

first-order
energy expansions (the
O(\varepsilon)
term with
O(\varepsilon^{2})
remainder)
in every dimension
n\geq 3
. For
n\geq 4
, the second-order coefficient
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is developed in §
3
;
to obtain an
O(\varepsilon^{2})
coefficient with a uniform
O(\varepsilon^{3})
remainder we work under
(BG
3+
) (and for the third-order refinement under (BG
4+
)).

•

(BG
4+
) (uniform
C^{4}
control) is needed, for
n\geq 4
, for the full higher–order
Escobar expansion (the
O(\varepsilon^{2})
coefficient
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
together with the

O(\varepsilon^{3})
term
\Theta_{g}
) with a uniform
O(\varepsilon^{4})
remainder, which is used in the
finer observability and multi–bubble selection arguments.

Remark 2.4

(Roadmap, scope, and normalization)
.

We work
exclusively
with the covariant functional (
1.2
).

Escobar I (§
2
).

This section develops the first-order theory: (i) Fermi chart toolkit and near-isometry, (ii) model stability on the hemisphere and transfer to the half-space, (iii) intrinsic coercivity/stability on
(M,g)
, (iv) first-order expansion and the scale-deficit law, (v) boundary-vs.-interior competition at first order, and (vi) compactness below the hemisphere. The
plain trace
addendum appears in §
2.9
.

Escobar II (§
3
).

This section contains
second-order
one-bubble material: the
O(\varepsilon^{2})
expansion and the renormalized mass
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
, explicit curvature decomposition, sampling/observability formulas, the Escobar-Willmore splitting and its consequences (closure/rigidity at the threshold), and the
O(\varepsilon^{3})
coefficient
\Theta_{g}
with its structure.

Escobar III (§
4
).

This section contains the multi-bubble analysis: Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction, the quantitative multi-bubble expansion and interaction kernel
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
, scale elimination and the center-only potential
\mathcal{W}_{k}
, and the threshold classification plus existence/multiplicity.

2.1.
Basic notation and conventions for §
2

Geometry, charts, and indices.

For
x\in\partial M
,
\Phi_{x}:\varepsilon B_{2R}^{+}\to M
denotes a Fermi chart centered at
x
with respect to the
inner
unit normal
\nu
. We fix
R>0
and only consider
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}:=\frac{r_{0}}{4R}
, so that
\varepsilon B_{2R}^{+}\subset\{0\leq t<r_{0}\}
and
\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon B_{2R}^{+})
lies inside the fixed Fermi collar from (BG
k+
).
Throughout, the inner normal is used, i.e.
t\geq 0
.
We write

y=(y^{\prime},y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty),\qquad B_{R}^{+}:=B_{R}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},

and use Einstein summation for tangential indices
a,b\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. The induced boundary metric is
\bar{g}=g|_{T\partial M}
, and we raise/lower tangential indices with
\bar{g}
and its inverse
\bar{g}^{-1}
.

Second fundamental form and mean curvature.

\mathrm{II}=\{\mathrm{II}_{ab}\}
is the second fundamental form of
\partial M
in
(M,g)
, with
\mathrm{II}^{ab}:=\bar{g}^{ac}\bar{g}^{bd}\mathrm{II}_{cd}
.
The (trace) mean curvature is

H_{g}:=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\mathrm{II}=\bar{g}^{ab}\mathrm{II}_{ab},

taken with respect to the
inner
normal. With this sign, strictly convex Euclidean domains (e.g. the Euclidean ball endowed with the inner unit normal) have
H_{g}>0
(Remark
2.14
). We write
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
for the traceless part:
\mathrm{II}^{ab}=\frac{H_{g}}{n-1}\,\bar{g}^{ab}+\mathring{\mathrm{II}}^{ab}
.

Quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
and the canonical pair.

We set

a_{n}:=\frac{4(n-1)}{n-2}.

For the
canonical
Escobar pair we fix

(2.6)

L_{g}^{\circ}:=-\Delta_{g}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g},\qquad B_{g}^{\circ}:=-\partial_{\nu}+\frac{n-2}{2}\,H_{g}.

The associated weak energy on
H^{1}(M)
is (cf. (
2.1
) upstream with
\alpha_{n}=1
,
\beta_{n}=\tfrac{n-2}{2}
)

\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u):=\int_{M}\Big(|\nabla u|_{g}^{2}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,u^{2}\Big)\,dV_{g}+\int_{\partial M}\frac{n-2}{2}H_{g}\,u^{2}\,d\sigma_{g}.

We then define

\mathcal{Q}_{g}(u):=a_{n}\int_{M}|\nabla u|_{g}^{2}\,dV_{g}+\int_{M}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,u^{2}\,dV_{g}+2(n-1)\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,u^{2}\,d\sigma_{g}.

For
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
, Green’s identity gives

(2.7)

\int_{M}u\,L_{g}^{\circ}u\,dV_{g}+\int_{\partial M}u\,B_{g}^{\circ}u\,d\sigma_{g}=\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u),\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{g}(u)=a_{n}\,\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u),

and by density these identities hold for all
u\in H^{1}(M)
interpreting the left-hand side via the right-hand side.

Escobar quotient and deficit.

The Escobar quotient is

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u]:=\frac{\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u)}{\|u(\cdot)\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\partial M)}^{2}},\qquad 2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2},

and the sharp constant
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g):=\inf_{u\neq 0}\mathcal{J}_{g}[u]
is strictly positive by (Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
) and the global trace. The (nonnegative) covariant Escobar deficit is

\mathrm{Def}^{\mathrm{Esc}}_{g}(u):=\mathcal{J}_{g}[u]-C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)\ \geq 0.

Half-space notation and tangential gradient.

On
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
we write
\nabla_{\tan}w:=(\partial_{y_{a}}w)_{a=1}^{n-1}
and
dy
for Lebesgue measure;
d\sigma
denotes the flat boundary measure on
\{y_{n}=0\}
.

Fermi pushforward at the critical trace scaling.

For
w
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
(compactly supported in
B_{2R}^{+}
, or as a limit of such truncations),

(\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}w)(z):=\varepsilon^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}\;w\Big(\frac{\Phi_{x}^{-1}(z)}{\varepsilon}\Big)\quad\text{for }z\in\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon B_{2R}^{+}),\qquad 0\ \text{outside}.

When
w
is not compactly supported we insert a fixed smooth cutoff
\chi_{R}
equal to
1
on
B_{R}^{+}
and define
\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R}w)
; all first-order (resp. second-order) formulas are independent of the choice of
\chi_{R}
under (BG
2+
) (resp. (BG
3+
) or (BG
4+
) depending on the remainder chosen earlier). We keep the shorthand
v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R}w)
, suppressing
R
.

Model profiles and cutoffs.

Fix a half-space optimizer
U_{+}
(cf.
[
25
, Eq. (2)]
) for the
covariant
Escobar problem on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
. Fix once and for all a representative
U_{+}
that is centered at the origin and tangentially radial, and normalize it by

\|\nabla U_{+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\;=\;1.

Equivalently (since
U_{+}
is an optimizer and
S_{\ast}
is the sharp constant on the model),

\|U_{+}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}=\frac{1}{S_{\ast}}.

All model constants (e.g.
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}
,
\Theta
) are defined with respect to this fixed, normalized
U_{+}
.
Similarly, fix a radially symmetric optimizer
U_{\mathrm{int}}
for the interior Yamabe problem on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, normalized by
\|\nabla U_{\mathrm{int}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=1
.

Remark 2.5

(Interior (Yamabe/Sobolev) bubbles: standard references)
.

In this paper, an
interior bubble
is obtained by transplanting to
(M,g)
, via geodesic normal
coordinates and the critical scaling
\varepsilon^{-(n-2)/2}
, the Euclidean extremals of the sharp
Sobolev inequality on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(the
Aubin–Talenti
family)

U_{\lambda,\xi}(y)=c_{n}\left(\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda^{2}|y-\xi|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{2}},\qquad\lambda>0,\ \xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

which are precisely the optimizers for the sharp Sobolev inequality; see
[
4
,
56
]
.
Equivalently, these are the positive entire solutions (up to symmetries) of the critical equation

-\Delta U=U^{\frac{n+2}{n-2}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, whose classification is due to
Caffarelli–Gidas–Spruck
[
13
]
.
In Yamabe/Escobar blow-up analysis on manifolds, these functions appear as the standard local interior
blow-up profiles; see, e.g., Schoen
[
51
]
.

Fix a smooth cutoff
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
0\leq\chi\leq 1
,
\chi(y)=1
for
|y|\leq 1
, and
\chi(y)=0
for
|y|\geq 2
. For
R\geq 1
, set
\chi_{R}(y):=\chi(y/R)
.

Definition 2.6

(Bubbles and Manifolds)
.

Let
(M,g)
satisfy
(BG
2+
)
with injectivity radius
r_{0}
. Fix a cutoff parameter
R\geq 1
.

(i)

Boundary bubbles.
For
x\in\partial M
and
0<\varepsilon<r_{0}/(2R)
, the boundary bubble is defined via the Fermi pushforward
\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}
(from §
2.1
):

U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\ :=\ \mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R}U_{+}).

(Explicitly:
U_{+,x,\varepsilon}(z)=\varepsilon^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}\,(\chi_{R}U_{+})\big(\Phi_{x}^{-1}(z)/\varepsilon\big)
inside the Fermi chart).

(ii)

Interior bubbles.
For
x\in M^{\circ}
and
0<\varepsilon<r_{0}/(2R)
(assuming
\mathrm{dist}_{g}(x,\partial M)>2R\varepsilon
), let
\Psi_{x}
be a geodesic normal chart at
x
. The interior bubble is

U^{\mathrm{int}}_{x,\varepsilon}(z)\ :=\ \varepsilon^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}\,(\chi_{R}U_{\mathrm{int}})\!\left(\frac{\Psi_{x}^{-1}(z)}{\varepsilon}\right).

(iii)

Bubble manifolds.
We define the submanifolds of
H^{1}(M)
(for a fixed
R
) by:

\mathcal{B}_{\partial}:=\{U_{+,x,\varepsilon}:x\in\partial M,\varepsilon>0\},\qquad\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{int}}:=\{U^{\mathrm{int}}_{x,\varepsilon}:x\in M^{\circ},\varepsilon>0\}.

Remark 2.7

(The two cutoff conventions)
.

The truncated bubbles defined above are not exact critical points of the Escobar functional. Although this differs from the classical definition, the truncated approach has been adopted in
[
45
,
15
]
and is adequate for the analysis carried out here. The choice of
R
dictates the structure of the error terms, leading to two specific conventions:

(a)

Limit coefficients (Diagonal Regime):
In Sections
2
–
3
(single-bubble theory), intrinsic coefficients like
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
are defined via the limit
R\to\infty
(or explicitly along a diagonal
R(\varepsilon)\to\infty
with
\varepsilon R(\varepsilon)\to 0
).

(b)

Fixed separation (Interaction Regime):
In Section
4
(multi-bubble theory), to ensure quantitative support separation for the Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction, we fix a large
R=R_{0}
once and for all.

Unless specified, the notation
U_{+,x,\varepsilon}
refers to the bubble constructed according to the context-appropriate regime.

Modulation directions.

For
v^{\sharp}=U_{+,x,\varepsilon}
, the tangent (neutral) directions are

\partial_{\varepsilon}U_{+,x,\varepsilon},\qquad\nabla_{x}U_{+,x,\varepsilon}=\big(\partial_{x^{\alpha}}U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\big)_{\alpha=1}^{n-1},

and we impose
graph-orthogonality

\langle u-v^{\sharp},\partial_{\varepsilon}U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\rangle_{E,g}=0
and

\langle u-v^{\sharp},\partial_{x^{\alpha}}U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\rangle_{E,g}=0
in the modulation lemmas.

Cayley transform and spherical optimizer.

The Cayley transform
\mathcal{C}:S^{n}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
is the standard conformal diffeomorphism; if
U_{+}
is fixed on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
, its Cayley pullback
F_{\ast}:=\mathcal{C}^{\ast}U_{+}
lies on the optimizer manifold
\mathcal{M}_{S}
on
(S^{n}_{+},g_{\mathrm{round}})
. We write
E:=T_{F_{\ast}}\mathcal{M}_{S}
for the tangent space to
\mathcal{M}_{S}
generated by boundary-preserving conformal motions.

First-order moments on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
.

For the (harmonic) optimizer
U_{+}
we set, in dimensions
n\geq 4
,

\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}:=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy},\qquad\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\tan}:=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\nabla_{\tan}U_{+}|^{2}\,dy}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy},\qquad\Theta:=\frac{\|U_{+}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}}{\|\nabla U_{+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}^{2}}.

These quantities are finite for
n\geq 4
and enter the explicit formula for the first-order
coefficient
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
in (
2.18
), cf. Lemmas
2.11
and
2.42
.

In dimension
n=3
we do
not
use the individual moments

\mathfrak{g}^{(1)},\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\tan},\Theta
, since the corresponding weighted
integrals on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
diverge logarithmically.
Instead we regard
\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}
as the unique positive constant appearing in the
one-bubble expansion

\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]\;=\;S_{\ast}\bigl(1+\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon\bigr)\;+\;o(\varepsilon),\qquad\varepsilon\downarrow 0,

whose existence and positivity are proved by passing to the spherical model

(S^{3}_{+},g_{\mathrm{round}})
via the Cayley transform, establishing the expansion there
(where all relevant integrals are finite), and then transferring it back to
(M,g)
by
conformal covariance (see Lemma
2.42
).
We will not attempt to express
\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}
in terms of global moments of
U_{+}

on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
.

Big-O with chart radius.

The symbol
O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})
means a bound
\leq C(R)\,\varepsilon^{2}
with
C(R)
independent of the center
x
(by (BG)) and acting on the appropriate quadratic norm of
w
(e.g.
\|w\|_{H^{1}(B_{2R}^{+})}^{2}
). We use the same convention for
O_{R}(\varepsilon)
. Under (BG
2+
) the constants
C(R)
are uniform in
x
thanks to the uniform
C^{2}
bounds of the Fermi jets; for higher-order remainders we use (BG
3+
) or (BG
4+
) accordingly.

Tangential radiality.

A function
w
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
is
tangentially radial
if
w(y^{\prime},y_{n})=W(|y^{\prime}|,y_{n})
; then
|\nabla_{\tan}w|^{2}=W_{r}^{2}
and the traceless part
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
averages out in first-order corrections (cf. (
2.9
)).

2.2.
Toolkit in Fermi charts (first order)

Lemma 2.8

(Uniform Fermi atlas and Jacobian control)
.

Assume
(BG
2+
)
. Then there exists an atlas of interior normal and boundary Fermi charts with uniform
C^{2}
bounds and bilipschitz constants (independent of the center and chart), so that the Jacobians and their first derivatives are uniformly controlled on fixed chart radii. In particular, Euclidean capacity and trace estimates transfer to
(M,g)
with constants depending only on the
(BG
2+
)
data (cf. Lemma
2.2
).

Lemma 2.9

(Near-isometry of the energy with first-order geometric correction)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
satisfy
(BG
2+
)
near a boundary point
x\in\partial M
. Fix a Fermi chart
\Phi_{x}:B_{r_{0}}^{+}\to M
adapted to the
inner
unit normal, and for
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)
use its restriction to
\varepsilon B_{2R}^{+}
.
For
w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
compactly supported in
B^{+}_{2R}
and
u_{\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}w
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{g}(u_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=a_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}}|\nabla w|^{2}\,dy+a_{n}\,\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}}y_{n}\Big(2\,\mathrm{II}^{ab}(x)\,\partial_{y_{a}}w\,\partial_{y_{b}}w-H_{g}(x)\,|\nabla w|^{2}\Big)dy

(2.8)

\displaystyle\quad+2(n-1)\,\varepsilon\,H_{g}(x)\,\|w(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})\,\|w\|_{H^{1}(B^{+}_{2R})}^{2}.

Here
H_{g}=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\mathrm{II}
is the (trace) mean curvature with respect to the inner normal. If, in addition,
w
is tangentially radial, then the traceless part of
\mathrm{II}
averages out and

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{g}(u_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=a_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}}|\nabla w|^{2}\,dy

\displaystyle\quad+a_{n}\,\varepsilon\,H_{g}(x)\left[\frac{2}{n-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}}y_{n}\,|\nabla_{\tan}w|^{2}\,dy-\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}}y_{n}\,|\nabla w|^{2}\,dy\right]

(2.9)

\displaystyle\quad+2(n-1)\,\varepsilon\,H_{g}(x)\,\|w(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})\,\|w\|_{H^{1}(B^{+}_{2R})}^{2}.

Proof.

Fix
R<\infty
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}(R))
so that
\varepsilon B^{+}_{2R}\subset B^{+}_{r_{0}}
for the fixed Fermi collar in (BG).
Write
y=(y^{\prime},y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)
and
z=\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon y)\in M
in Fermi coordinates at
x\in\partial M
, adapted to the
inner
unit normal. Define

u_{\varepsilon}(z)=\varepsilon^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}\,w(y),\qquad\supp w\subset B^{+}_{2R}.

If
w
is not compactly supported we insert a fixed smooth cutoff
\chi_{R}\equiv 1
on
B_{R}^{+}
and apply the argument below to
\chi_{R}w
. In dimensions
n\geq 4
we may then pass to the limit
R\to\infty
by dominated convergence when needed (all relevant weighted moments are finite). In dimension
n=3
we will only use this lemma for compactly supported truncations (such as
\chi_{R}U_{+}
); the global coefficients (e.g.
\rho^{\mathrm{conf}}_{3}
) are defined by comparison with the spherical model rather than by explicit flat moment formulas.

Step 1 (Fermi expansions).

We take the tangential coordinates
y^{\prime}
on
\partial M
to be geodesic normal for
\bar{g}
at
x
, and extend them by Fermi transport along the inner normal.
By (BG
2+
) and standard Fermi theory (indices
a,b\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
):

(2.10)

\displaystyle g_{ab}(y^{\prime},y_{n})

\displaystyle=\bar{g}_{ab}(y^{\prime},0)-2y_{n}\,\mathrm{II}_{ab}(x)+O(|y^{\prime}|^{2}+y_{n}^{2}),

(2.11)

\displaystyle g^{ab}(y^{\prime},y_{n})

\displaystyle=\bar{g}^{ab}(y^{\prime},0)+2y_{n}\,\mathrm{II}^{ab}(x)+O(|y^{\prime}|^{2}+y_{n}^{2}),

(2.12)

\displaystyle\sqrt{|g|}(y^{\prime},y_{n})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{|\bar{g}(y^{\prime},0)|}\,\big(1-H_{g}(x)\,y_{n}\big)+O(|y^{\prime}|^{2}+y_{n}^{2}),

(2.13)

\displaystyle g_{an}

\displaystyle=0,\quad g_{nn}=1,\quad g^{an}=0,\quad g^{nn}=1+O(|y^{\prime}|^{2}+y_{n}^{2}),

with
\bar{g}_{ab}(y^{\prime},0)=\delta_{ab}+O(|y^{\prime}|^{2})
and
\sqrt{|\bar{g}(y^{\prime},0)|}=1+O(|y^{\prime}|^{2})
. Here
H_{g}=\sum_{a=1}^{n-1}\mathrm{II}^{aa}
is the mean curvature with respect to the boundary metric
\bar{g}
and the inner normal. These expansions in boundary Fermi coordinates are standard; see, e.g.,
[
27
, §2]
and
[
5
, Ch. 2, App. B]
.

Step 2 (Dirichlet energy: change of variables and scale cancellation).

Using
z=\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon y)
and
\partial_{z_{i}}=\varepsilon^{-1}\partial_{y_{i}}
,

\int_{M}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\,dV_{g}=\int_{B^{+}_{2R}}g^{ij}(\varepsilon y)\,\partial_{y_{i}}w(y)\,\partial_{y_{j}}w(y)\,\sqrt{|g(\varepsilon y)|}\,dy.

Step 3 (First-order expansion of the integrand).

From (
2.11
)–(
2.13
) and
\sqrt{|\bar{g}(y^{\prime},0)|}=1+O(|y^{\prime}|^{2})
,

\displaystyle g^{ab}(\varepsilon y)\sqrt{|g(\varepsilon y)|}

\displaystyle=\delta^{ab}+\varepsilon y_{n}\Big(2\,\mathrm{II}^{ab}(x)-H_{g}(x)\,\delta^{ab}\Big)+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2}),

\displaystyle g^{nn}(\varepsilon y)\sqrt{|g(\varepsilon y)|}

\displaystyle=1-\varepsilon H_{g}(x)\,y_{n}+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad g^{an}\sqrt{|g|}=O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Hence, writing
\nabla_{\tan}w=(\partial_{y_{a}}w)_{a=1}^{n-1}
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\,dV_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{B^{+}_{2R}}|\nabla w|^{2}\,dy+\varepsilon\int_{B^{+}_{2R}}y_{n}\Big(2\,\mathrm{II}^{ab}(x)\,\partial_{y_{a}}w\,\partial_{y_{b}}w-H_{g}(x)\,|\nabla w|^{2}\Big)\,dy

(2.14)

\displaystyle\quad+\,O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})\,\|w\|_{H^{1}(B^{+}_{2R})}^{2}.

Step 4 (Scalar curvature term).

Scaling as above,

\int_{M}\!\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,u_{\varepsilon}^{2}\,dV_{g}=\varepsilon^{2}\!\!\int_{B^{+}_{2R}}\!\!\!\mathrm{Scal}_{g}(\varepsilon y)|w(y)|^{2}\sqrt{|g(\varepsilon y)|}\,dy=O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})\|w\|_{L^{2}(B^{+}_{2R})}^{2}\!\leq O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})\|w\|_{H^{1}(B^{+}_{2R})}^{2}.

Step 5 (Boundary term).

On
y_{n}=0
we parametrize
\partial M
by
z^{\prime}=\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon y^{\prime},0)
; then

u_{\varepsilon}(z^{\prime},0)=\varepsilon^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}\,w(y^{\prime},0),\qquad dS_{g}=\sqrt{\det g_{ab}(\varepsilon y^{\prime},0)}\,\varepsilon^{n-1}dy^{\prime}=(1+O(\varepsilon^{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2}))\,\varepsilon^{n-1}dy^{\prime}.

Moreover
H_{g}(\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon y^{\prime},0))=H_{g}(x)+O(\varepsilon|y^{\prime}|)
. Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,u_{\varepsilon}^{2}\,dS_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\big(H_{g}(x)+O(\varepsilon|y^{\prime}|)\big)\,\varepsilon^{-(n-2)}|w(y^{\prime},0)|^{2}\,(1+O(\varepsilon^{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2}))\,\varepsilon^{n-1}dy^{\prime}

(2.15)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\,H_{g}(x)\,\|w(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}\ +\ O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})\,\|w\|_{H^{1}(B^{+}_{2R})}^{2},

using the trace inequality on
B^{+}_{2R}
for the error term.

Step 6 (Assemble
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
).

By definition,

\mathcal{Q}_{g}(u_{\varepsilon})=a_{n}\int_{M}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\,dV_{g}+\int_{M}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,u_{\varepsilon}^{2}\,dV_{g}+2(n-1)\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,u_{\varepsilon}^{2}\,dS_{g}.

With the canonical normalization of the weak Escobar energy (gradient coefficient
1
and zeroth-order coefficients
\mathrm{Scal}_{g}/a_{n}
in the interior and
2(n-1)H_{g}/a_{n}
on the boundary), we have the bridge identity
\mathcal{Q}_{g}(u)=a_{n}\,\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u)
for all
u\in H^{1}(M)
(see (
2.7
)). Combining (
2.14
)–(
2.15
) gives (
2.8
).

Step 7 (Isotropic reduction for tangentially radial
w
).

If
w(y^{\prime},y_{n})=W(r,y_{n})
with
r=|y^{\prime}|
, then
\partial_{y_{a}}w=W_{r}(r,y_{n})\,\omega_{a}
for
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{n-2}
and
|\nabla_{\tan}w|^{2}=W_{r}^{2}
. Decompose covariantly

\mathrm{II}^{ab}=\frac{H_{g}}{n-1}\,\bar{g}^{ab}+\mathring{\mathrm{II}}^{ab},\qquad\bar{g}_{ab}\,\mathring{\mathrm{II}}^{ab}=0.

Using
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-2}}\omega_{a}\omega_{b}\,d\omega=\frac{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{n-2})}{n-1}\,\bar{g}_{ab}(x)
,
we obtain

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,\mathrm{II}^{ab}\,\partial_{y_{a}}w\,\partial_{y_{b}}w=\frac{H_{g}(x)}{n-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\nabla_{\tan}w|^{2},

since the
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
-contribution integrates to zero by tracelessness. Substituting into (
2.8
) gives (
2.9
).

All
O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})
terms are uniform in
x
by (BG
2+
) and the support restriction
|y|\leq 2R
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.10

(Boundary trace at critical scaling has no
O(\varepsilon)
correction)
.

Let
n\geq 3
and let
w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
be supported in
B_{2R}^{+}
with

w(\cdot,0)\in L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
, where
2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
.
Assume
(BG
2+
)
. Then, for each fixed
R<\infty
there exists

\varepsilon_{0}(R)>0
such that for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(R)
,

\big\|\big(\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}w\big)\big|_{\partial M}\big\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\partial M)}^{2^{*}_{\partial}}\ =\ \big(1+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})\big)\,\|w(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2^{*}_{\partial}}

uniformly in
x\in\partial M
, where
O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})
denotes a quantity bounded by

C(R)\varepsilon^{2}
with
C(R)
depending only on
R
and the
(BG
2+
)
data.

Proof.

Work in a boundary Fermi chart
\Phi_{x}
centered at
x\in\partial M
, adapted to the inner
unit normal, and write
z=\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon y)
with
y=(y^{\prime},y_{n})
and
y_{n}\geq 0
.
By definition,

(\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}w)(z)=\varepsilon^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}\,w(y),\qquad z\in\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon B^{+}_{2R}),

and
(\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}w)(z)=0
outside this chart image. On the boundary slice

\{y_{n}=0\}
we choose
y^{\prime}
to be geodesic normal coordinates for
\bar{g}
at
x
. Then,
by (BG
2+
) and the standard boundary normal expansion,

g_{ab}(\varepsilon y^{\prime},0)=\delta_{ab}+O(\varepsilon^{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2}),\hskip 5.69054ptd\sigma_{g}\big|_{y_{n}=0}=\sqrt{\det(g_{ab}(\varepsilon y^{\prime},0))}\,\varepsilon^{n-1}dy^{\prime}=\varepsilon^{n-1}\big(1+O(\varepsilon^{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2})\big)\,dy^{\prime}.

Since
(\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}w)|_{\partial M}
is supported in

\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon B_{2R}\cap\{y_{n}=0\})
, we can write

\displaystyle\int_{\partial M}\big|(\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}w)(z)\big|^{2^{*}_{\partial}}\,d\sigma_{g}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}\varepsilon^{-\frac{(n-2)2^{*}_{\partial}}{2}}\,|w(y^{\prime},0)|^{2^{*}_{\partial}}\cdot\varepsilon^{n-1}\big(1+O(\varepsilon^{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2})\big)\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|w(y^{\prime},0)|^{2^{*}_{\partial}}\,\big(1+O(\varepsilon^{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2})\big)\,dy^{\prime},

because
\frac{(n-2)2^{*}_{\partial}}{2}=n-1
. Since
w(\cdot,0)
is supported in
B_{2R}
,
we have
|y^{\prime}|\leq 2R
on the support, so
O(\varepsilon^{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2})=O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})
, and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|w(y^{\prime},0)|^{2^{*}_{\partial}}\,O(\varepsilon^{2}|y^{\prime}|^{2})\,dy^{\prime}=O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})\,\|w(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2^{*}_{\partial}}.

This yields the desired estimate. Uniformity in
x
follows from the uniformity of the Fermi
expansions under (BG
2+
).
∎

Lemma 2.11

(Weighted harmonic moment identities on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
)
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
, and let
U
be the Poisson extension of
f
to

\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
, so that
U
is harmonic in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
and
U(\cdot,0)=f
in the trace sense. Then

(2.16)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\nabla U|^{2}\,dy\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2},\,\,\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\nabla_{\tan}U|^{2}\,dy\;=\;\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\partial_{y_{n}}U|^{2}\,dy\;=\;\frac{1}{4}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}.

In particular, if in addition
\nabla U\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
so that the dimensionless moments
from §
2.1
are finite (for example, for the Escobar optimizer
U_{+}
when

n\geq 4
), then

(2.17)

\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\,\Theta,\qquad\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\tan}\;=\;\frac{1}{4}\,\Theta.

Proof.

Taking the tangential Fourier transform in
y^{\prime}
yields

\widehat{U}(k,y_{n})=\hat{f}(k)e^{-|k|y_{n}},\qquad k\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1},\ y_{n}>0,

for the Poisson extension. In Fourier variables,

|\nabla_{\tan}U|^{2}\longleftrightarrow|k|^{2}|\hat{f}(k)|^{2}e^{-2|k|y_{n}},\qquad|\partial_{y_{n}}U|^{2}\longleftrightarrow|k|^{2}|\hat{f}(k)|^{2}e^{-2|k|y_{n}}.

For
k\neq 0
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}y_{n}|k|^{2}e^{-2|k|y_{n}}\,dy_{n}=|k|^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}y_{n}e^{-2|k|y_{n}}\,dy_{n}=\frac{1}{4},

while for
k=0
the integrand is identically zero, so the inner integral vanishes.
Define

J(k):=\int_{0}^{\infty}y_{n}|k|^{2}|\hat{f}(k)|^{2}e^{-2|k|y_{n}}\,dy_{n}.

Then
J(k)=\tfrac{1}{4}|\hat{f}(k)|^{2}
for all
k\neq 0
, and
J(0)=0
, so

0\leq J(k)\leq\tfrac{1}{4}|\hat{f}(k)|^{2}\quad\text{for all }k\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}.

Using Tonelli and Plancherel,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}|\nabla_{\tan}U|^{2}\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}y_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|k|^{2}|\hat{f}(k)|^{2}e^{-2|k|y_{n}}\,dk\,dy_{n}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}J(k)\,dk=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|\hat{f}(k)|^{2}\,dk=\frac{1}{4}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2},

where the last equality holds because the set
\{k=0\}
has Lebesgue measure zero.
The same computation with
|\partial_{y_{n}}U|^{2}
in place of
|\nabla_{\tan}U|^{2}
gives

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}|\partial_{y_{n}}U|^{2}\,dy=\frac{1}{4}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}.

Adding the two equalities yields

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}|\nabla U|^{2}\,dy=\frac{1}{2}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2}.

This proves (
2.16
). The relations (
2.17
)
follow immediately by dividing by
\|\nabla U\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}^{2}
whenever the dimensionless
quantities are defined.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

In dimension
n=3
the Escobar optimizer
U_{+}
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
satisfies

U_{+}(y^{\prime},0)\sim|y^{\prime}|^{-1}
as
|y^{\prime}|\to\infty
, so its
L^{2}
trace diverges
logarithmically. Consequently,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}y_{3}\,|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}y_{3}\,|\nabla_{\tan}U_{+}|^{2}\,dy

are infinite, and the ratios
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\tan}
and
\Theta

(as defined in §
2.1
) are not meaningful in
n=3
.

Whenever we need harmonic moment identities in
n=3
, we apply
Lemma
2.11
only to harmonic functions with finite
L^{2}
trace
(for instance, harmonic extensions of truncated boundary data), and we use the resulting
identities at the level of intermediate estimates. For the model optimizer
U_{+}
itself,
and for the first-order coefficient
\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}
in the one-bubble Escobar
expansion on
(M,g)
,

\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]=S_{\ast}\bigl(1+\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x)\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon)\bigr),

we
do not
rely on any renormalized bulk-moment formula on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
. Instead,

\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0
is defined via the spherical model (Cayley pullback to
S^{3}_{+}
)
and the corresponding first-order expansion on the hemisphere. By conformal invariance

C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(S^{3}_{+})=S_{\ast}
, so the same coefficient governs the flat model.
See Remark
3.9
in the Escobar II section. Thus, in
n=3
we treat

\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}
as a geometric constant determined by the model problem, without
asserting any explicit representation in terms of
(\mathfrak{g}^{(1)},\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\tan},\Theta)
.

Lemma 2.13

(Canonical numerator equals the
a_{n}
-scaled quadratic form)
.

Let
n\geq 3
and set
a_{n}=\frac{4(n-1)}{n-2}
. Define

\mathcal{Q}_{g}(u):=a_{n}\int_{M}|\nabla u|_{g}^{2}\,dV_{g}+\int_{M}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,u^{2}\,dV_{g}+2(n-1)\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,u^{2}\,d\sigma_{g}.

Then, for
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

\int_{M}uL_{g}^{\circ}u\,dV_{g}+\int_{\partial M}uB_{g}^{\circ}u\,d\sigma_{g}=\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u),\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{g}(u)=a_{n}\,\mathcal{N}^{\circ}(u).

By density, both identities extend to all
u\in H^{1}(M)
, interpreting the left-hand side
via the right-hand side (weak Green identity).

Combining Lemmas
2.9
,
2.10

and
2.13
with a
tangentially radial
profile

w\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
supported in
B_{2R}^{+}
(and
w(\cdot,0)\not\equiv 0
) yields,
for
u_{\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}w
, a first-order expansion of the Escobar
quotient of the form

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\varepsilon}]=\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{flat}}[w]+\varepsilon\,H_{g}(x)\,\Lambda[w]+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2}),

where
\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{flat}}
is the Escobar quotient on the half-space and

\Lambda[w]
is a scalar depending only on
w
and
n
. The traceless part of
\II
does
not contribute at first order precisely because
w
is tangentially radial
(cf. (
2.9
)), so the linear term is proportional to
H_{g}(x)
.

In particular, taking
w=\chi_{R}U_{+}
with
\chi_{R}
a fixed smooth
tangentially radial
cutoff
supported in
B_{2R}^{+}
and equal to
1
on
B_{R}^{+}
(so that
\chi_{R}U_{+}
remains tangentially radial), we obtain

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\varepsilon,R}]=\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{flat}}[\chi_{R}U_{+}]+\varepsilon\,H_{g}(x)\,\Lambda[\chi_{R}U_{+}]+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad u_{\varepsilon,R}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R}U_{+}).

Since
\|\nabla(\chi_{R}U_{+})-\nabla U_{+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}\to 0
and
\chi_{R}U_{+}(\cdot,0)\to U_{+}(\cdot,0)
in

L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
as
R\to\infty
,
we have
\mathcal{J}_{\mathrm{flat}}[\chi_{R}U_{+}]\to S_{\ast}
, and, when
n\geq 4
, all the
weighted moments entering the explicit expression of
\Lambda[\chi_{R}U_{+}]
converge to the
corresponding moments of
U_{+}
itself by dominated convergence, using the finiteness of
these moments for
U_{+}
(see Lemma
2.11
). In particular, the limit

\Lambda[U_{+}]:=\lim_{R\to\infty}\Lambda[\chi_{R}U_{+}]

exists and depends only on
n
and on the model optimizer
U_{+}
.

Writing
v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
for the boundary bubble generated by
U_{+}

(implemented via the cutoff convention in §
2.1
), it follows that the coefficient of

\varepsilon H_{g}(x)
in the first-order expansion of
\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]

at
\varepsilon=0
is

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}:=S_{\ast}^{-1}\,\Lambda[U_{+}],

so that, for
n\geq 4
,

\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]=S_{\ast}\Big(1+\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon\Big)+o(\varepsilon)\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\downarrow 0.

For dimensions
n\geq 4
, the first-order coefficient admits the explicit moment formula

(2.18)

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}=\Big(\tfrac{2}{n-1}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\tan}-\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}\Big)+\tfrac{n-2}{2}\,\Theta,\qquad n\geq 4,

where
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}
,
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\tan}
and
\Theta
are as in
§
2.1
. If, in addition,
U_{+}
is harmonic with

U_{+}(\cdot,0)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
(which holds for the Escobar optimizer when
n\geq 4
),
Lemma
2.11
yields the simplified expression

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}=\frac{(n-2)^{2}}{2(n-1)}\,\Theta,\qquad n\geq 4.

Dimensional note (
n=3
).
When
n=3
, the
L^{2}
trace of
U_{+}
diverges and the raw
moments defining
(\mathfrak{g}^{(1)},\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\tan},\Theta)
are no longer finite.
In this case we do not use (
2.18
); instead,
\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0

is defined via the hemisphere model as in Remark
2.12
, and the first-order
expansion above is understood with that
\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}
.

Remark 2.14

(Sign convention)
.

We orient
\nu
as the
inner
unit normal, so the second fundamental form
\II
and
mean curvature
H_{g}=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\II
are computed with respect to the inward
pointing normal. In particular, for the Euclidean unit ball equipped with this convention,
boundary Fermi coordinates give
g_{ab}(t)=(1-t)^{2}\bar{g}_{ab}(0)
, so comparison with the
general expansion
g_{ab}(t)=\bar{g}_{ab}(0)-2t\,\II_{ab}+O(t^{2})
yields

\II_{ab}=\bar{g}_{ab}(0),\qquad H_{g}>0,

and strictly convex Euclidean domains have
positive
boundary mean curvature in our
convention.

In the first-order correction to the quadratic form (cf. (
2.9
)), the
gradient part contributes

\varepsilon\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\Big(2\,\mathrm{II}^{ab}\partial_{y_{a}}w\,\partial_{y_{b}}w-H_{g}\,|\nabla w|^{2}\Big)\,dy,

and, for tangentially radial
w
, its sign is governed by

\frac{2}{n-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\nabla_{\tan}w|^{2}\,dy-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\nabla w|^{2}\,dy.

The boundary term in
\mathcal{Q}_{g}
enters with the positive coefficient
2(n-1)
, so its
contribution to the first-order expansion is

2(n-1)H_{g}\,\varepsilon\,\|w(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2},

which is
positive
when
H_{g}>0
(e.g. for strictly convex domains in our convention)
and negative when
H_{g}<0
(locally concave boundary).

2.3.
Model stability on the hemisphere and transfer

Constraint slice on the hemisphere.

Let
F_{\ast}
denote the Cayley pullback to
(S^{n}_{+},g_{\mathrm{round}})
of a fixed half-space optimizer
U_{+}
for the covariant Escobar problem (normalized on the boundary). We work on

(2.19)

\mathcal{S}:=\Big\{u\in H^{1}(S^{n}_{+}):\ \|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial S^{n}_{+})}=\|F_{\ast}\|_{L^{q}(\partial S^{n}_{+})}\Big\},

and denote by
E:=T_{F_{\ast}}\mathcal{M}_{S}
the tangent space to the optimizer manifold on
S^{n}_{+}
generated by boundary-preserving conformal motions (transported from
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
via the Cayley transform).

Lemma 2.15

(Modulation near the optimizer manifold on
S^{n}_{+}
)
.

There exist
\delta_{*}>0
and a
C^{1}
map assigning to every
u\in\mathcal{S}
with
\mathrm{dist}_{H^{1}}(u,\mathcal{M}_{S})\leq\delta_{*}
a unique
v^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{M}_{S}
such that

\displaystyle u-v^{\sharp}\perp T_{v^{\sharp}}\mathcal{M}_{S}\ \text{ in the graph inner product }\ \langle\phi,\psi\rangle_{E,\mathrm{round}}:=

\displaystyle\int_{S^{n}_{+}}\Big(\nabla\phi\!\cdot\!\nabla\psi+\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}R_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}\,\phi\psi\Big)\,dV

\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{n-2}{2}\int_{\partial S^{n}_{+}}H_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}\,\phi\psi\,d\sigma.

Moreover, the parameter map is
C^{1}
and
\|u-v^{\sharp}\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\mathrm{dist}_{H^{1}}(u,\mathcal{M}_{S})
.

Proof.

Fix a boundary Fermi collar and a boundary chart
x=x(\xi)
with
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
near a given
x_{0}\in\partial M
.
For parameters
p:=(a,\xi,\varepsilon)
with
a>0
and
\varepsilon>0
small, set

\Psi(p)\ :=\ a\,U_{+,x(\xi),\varepsilon}\ \in H^{1}(M).

Define the constraint map
F:H^{1}(M)\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

F(u,p)\ :=\ \begin{pmatrix}F_{\mathrm{sl}}(u,p)\\[2.84526pt]
F_{\varepsilon}(u,p)\\[2.84526pt]
F_{1}(u,p)\\
\vdots\\
F_{n-1}(u,p)\end{pmatrix}\ :=\ \begin{pmatrix}\displaystyle\int_{\partial M}\Psi(p)^{q-1}\big(u-\Psi(p)\big)\,d\sigma_{g}\\[8.53581pt]
\displaystyle\big\langle u-\Psi(p),\,\partial_{\varepsilon}\Psi(p)\big\rangle_{E,g}\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle\big\langle u-\Psi(p),\,\partial_{\xi^{1}}\Psi(p)\big\rangle_{E,g}\\
\vdots\\
\displaystyle\big\langle u-\Psi(p),\,\partial_{\xi^{n-1}}\Psi(p)\big\rangle_{E,g}\end{pmatrix}.

Solving
F(u,p)=0
is exactly the system in the statement, with
v^{\sharp}=\Psi(p)
and
r=u-\Psi(p)
.

Step 1 (Nondegeneracy of the Jacobian in
p
).

Fix a base point
p_{0}=(a_{0},\xi_{0},\varepsilon_{0})
(with
\varepsilon_{0}
small) and set
u_{0}:=\Psi(p_{0})
.
Then
F(u_{0},p_{0})=0
. Differentiating
F
in the parameter variables at
(u_{0},p_{0})
and using
u_{0}-\Psi(p_{0})=0
gives

\displaystyle\partial_{p_{j}}F_{\mathrm{sl}}(u_{0},p_{0})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\partial M}u_{0}^{\,q-1}\,\partial_{p_{j}}\Psi(p_{0})\,d\sigma_{g},

\displaystyle\partial_{p_{j}}F_{\beta}(u_{0},p_{0})

\displaystyle=-\big\langle\partial_{p_{\beta}}\Psi(p_{0}),\,\partial_{p_{j}}\Psi(p_{0})\big\rangle_{E,g}\qquad(\beta\in\{\varepsilon,\xi^{1},\dots,\xi^{n-1}\}),

i.e. the
(n\times n)
Jacobian
D_{p}F(u_{0},p_{0})
is a Gram-type matrix built from the vectors

\partial_{a}\Psi(p_{0})=U_{+,x(\xi_{0}),\varepsilon_{0}},\qquad\partial_{\varepsilon}\Psi(p_{0}),\qquad\partial_{\xi^{\alpha}}\Psi(p_{0})\quad(\alpha=1,\dots,n-1),

together with the slice constraint row.
In the Euclidean half-space model, these vectors are linearly independent and the corresponding Gram matrix is invertible.
Since we work in a fixed collar and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E,g}\simeq\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(M)}^{2}
under (Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
),
the manifold case is a small perturbation of the Euclidean one after Fermi rescaling for
\varepsilon_{0}
sufficiently small.
Hence
D_{p}F(u_{0},p_{0})
remains invertible, with inverse bounded uniformly for all base points in the collar and all

\varepsilon_{0}
sufficiently small.

Step 2 (Existence, uniqueness, and
C^{1}
dependence).

Since
u\mapsto F(u,p)
is
C^{1}
on sign-preserving neighborhoods and
p\mapsto\Psi(p)
is
C^{1}
,
the map
(u,p)\mapsto F(u,p)
is
C^{1}
.
By Step 1 and the implicit function theorem, there exist
\delta_{*}>0
and a unique
C^{1}
map

u\ \longmapsto\ p(u)=(a^{\sharp}(u),\xi^{\sharp}(u),\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u))

defined for all
u
with
\|u-u_{0}\|_{H^{1}(M)}<\delta_{*}
, such that
F(u,p(u))=0
.
Writing
x^{\sharp}(u):=x(\xi^{\sharp}(u))
and
v^{\sharp}:=\Psi(p(u))
gives the asserted decomposition and orthogonality.

Step 3 (Quantitative estimate in terms of distance to the bubble manifold).

Let
\mathcal{M}:=\{aU_{+,x,\varepsilon}:a>0,\ x\in\partial M,\ \varepsilon>0\}
.
By uniqueness,
v^{\sharp}
is the (local) projection of
u
onto
\mathcal{M}
in the gauge given by the orthogonality
conditions. The uniform invertibility of
D_{p}F
yields a uniform Lipschitz bound

|a^{\sharp}-a|+d_{\partial}(x^{\sharp},x)+|\varepsilon^{\sharp}-\varepsilon|\ \lesssim\ \|u-aU_{+,x,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}(M)}

whenever
aU_{+,x,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{M}
is sufficiently close to
u
.
Taking the infimum over
(a,x,\varepsilon)
and using the triangle inequality gives

\|r\|_{H^{1}(M)}=\|u-v^{\sharp}\|_{H^{1}(M)}\ \lesssim\ \mathrm{dist}_{H^{1}(M)}(u,\mathcal{M}),

which is the claimed estimate.
∎

Lemma 2.16

(Modulation near
\mathcal{B}_{\partial}
in the covariant graph gauge)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
. Work in a fixed Fermi collar. We equip
H^{1}(M)
with the covariant graph bilinear form

\langle\phi,\psi\rangle_{E,g}:=\int_{M}\Big(\nabla\phi\!\cdot\!\nabla\psi+\tfrac{1}{a_{n}}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,\phi\psi\Big)\,dV_{g}+\tfrac{n-2}{2}\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,\phi\psi\,d\sigma_{g},

which is an inner product equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}}
by
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
. In a fixed Fermi collar, for
u\in H^{1}(M)
sufficiently close to the boundary bubble manifold

\mathcal{B}_{\partial}=\big\{U_{+,x,\varepsilon}:\ x\in\partial M,\ \varepsilon>0\big\},

there exists a unique triple of parameters
(a^{\sharp},x^{\sharp},\varepsilon^{\sharp})
with
a^{\sharp}>0
such that, with
v^{\sharp}:=a^{\sharp}\,U_{+,x^{\sharp},\varepsilon^{\sharp}}
and
r:=u-v^{\sharp}
,

\langle r,\partial_{\varepsilon}v^{\sharp}\rangle_{E,g}=0,\qquad\langle r,\partial_{x^{\alpha}}v^{\sharp}\rangle_{E,g}=0\quad(\alpha=1,\dots,n-1),

and the boundary constraint
\int_{\partial M}(v^{\sharp})^{q-1}r\,d\sigma_{g}=0
holds (hence the amplitude direction is fixed by the slice). The parameter map
u\mapsto(a^{\sharp},x^{\sharp},\varepsilon^{\sharp})
is
C^{1}
and

\|r\|_{H^{1}(M)}\lesssim\mathrm{dist}_{H^{1}}(u,\{a\,U_{+,x,\varepsilon}:a>0,\ x\in\partial M,\ \varepsilon>0\})
.

Lemma 2.17

(Constrained second variation at
F_{\ast}
)
.

Let
q=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
and let
F_{\ast}
be the Cayley pullback of a half-space optimizer, normalized by
\|F_{\ast}\|_{L^{q}(\partial S^{n}_{+})}=1
. Then

L_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}^{\circ}F_{\ast}=0\ \text{ in }S^{n}_{+},\qquad B_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}^{\circ}F_{\ast}=\lambda_{\ast}\,F_{\ast}^{\,q-1}\ \text{ on }\partial S^{n}_{+},

with
\lambda_{\ast}=\mathcal{N}^{\circ}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}(F_{\ast})=\mathcal{J}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}[F_{\ast}]=S_{\ast}
. For
\phi\in T_{F_{\ast}}\mathcal{S}=\{\phi:\int_{\partial S^{n}_{+}}F_{\ast}^{\,q-1}\phi=0\}
, the constrained second variation is

(2.20)

Q[\phi]=\mathcal{N}^{\circ}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}(\phi,\phi)\;-\;(q-1)\lambda_{\ast}\int_{\partial S^{n}_{+}}F_{\ast}^{\,q-2}\phi^{2}\,d\sigma.

On
(S^{n}_{+},g_{\mathrm{round}})
we have
H_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}\equiv 0
, so the boundary mean-curvature term vanishes in
\mathcal{N}^{\circ}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}
. Moreover,
\ker Q=E
and there exists
c_{\ast}>0
such that
Q[\phi]\geq c_{\ast}\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(S^{n}_{+})}^{2}
for all
\phi\in T_{F_{\ast}}\mathcal{S}\cap E^{\perp}
.

Proposition 2.18

(Weighted Robin (Jacobi) spectral gap on
S^{n}_{+}
)
.

With
Q
as in (
2.20
), there exists
\gamma_{\ast}=\gamma_{\ast}(n)>0
such that

Q[\phi]\ \geq\ \gamma_{\ast}\,\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(S^{n}_{+})}^{2}\qquad\text{for all }\ \phi\in T_{F_{\ast}}\mathcal{S}\ \text{ with }\ \phi\perp E\text{ in the pairing }\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E}:=\mathcal{N}^{\circ}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}(\cdot,\cdot).

Equivalently, the Jacobi system

L_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}^{\circ}\phi=0\ \text{ in }S^{n}_{+},\qquad B_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}^{\circ}\phi=(q-1)\lambda_{\ast}\,F_{\ast}^{\,q-2}\phi\ \text{ on }\partial S^{n}_{+},

has strictly positive first eigenvalue on
T_{F_{\ast}}\mathcal{S}\cap E^{\perp}
(orthogonality in
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E}
). By conformal invariance, the gap is uniform along
\mathcal{M}_{S}
.

Proof.

If no gap held, a normalized sequence
\phi_{k}\in T_{F_{\ast}}\mathcal{S}\cap E^{\perp}
with
Q[\phi_{k}]\to 0
would converge (up to subsequences) to a nontrivial Jacobi field in the kernel; pushing to
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
via the Cayley map and using the known nondegeneracy of the Escobar bubble shows the kernel equals
E
, contradiction.
∎

Theorem 2.19

(Local Escobar stability on
(S^{n}_{+},g_{\mathrm{round}})
)
.

There exist
\delta_{0}>0
and
c_{*}>0
(depending only on
n
) such that: if
u\in H^{1}(S^{n}_{+})
satisfies

\mathrm{Def}^{\mathrm{Esc}}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}(u)\leq\delta_{0}
, then for the best-fit
v^{\sharp}\in\mathcal{M}_{S}
given by Lemma
2.15
(so that
u-v^{\sharp}\perp T_{v^{\sharp}}\mathcal{M}_{S}
in
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E}
),

\mathrm{Def}^{\mathrm{Esc}}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}(u)\ \geq\ c_{*}\,\|u-v^{\sharp}\|_{E,g_{\mathrm{round}}}^{2}\ \simeq\ \|u-v^{\sharp}\|_{H^{1}(S^{n}_{+})}^{2}.

Proof.

By the homogeneity of
\mathcal{J}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}
in
u
, we may rescale
u
so that
u\in\mathcal{S}
and work on the constraint slice (
2.19
). By Lemma
2.15
we may write
u=v^{\sharp}+w
with
w\perp T_{v^{\sharp}}\mathcal{M}_{S}
in the covariant graph pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E}=\mathcal{N}^{\circ}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
. A Taylor expansion of
\mathcal{J}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}
on the slice
\mathcal{S}
at
v^{\sharp}
gives

\mathrm{Def}^{\mathrm{Esc}}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}(u)=\tfrac{1}{2}\,Q[w]\ +\ O(\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{3}),

where
Q
is the Hessian quadratic form (
2.20
) (transported along
\mathcal{M}_{S}
). The cubic term is controlled by smoothness of
\mathcal{J}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}
on
\mathcal{S}
and bounded geometry of
S^{n}_{+}
. By Proposition
2.18
and Lemma
2.20
(a),

Q[w]\ \gtrsim\ \|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

By the positivity of
R_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}
and
H_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}\equiv 0
, the graph norm induced by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E}
is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(S^{n}_{+})}
. For
\delta_{0}
small the cubic remainders are absorbed, yielding the claim.
∎

Lemma 2.20

(Graph norm equivalence and conformal transfer)
.

Let
\|\cdot\|_{E,g}
denote the Escobar graph norm built from
(L_{g}^{\circ},B_{g}^{\circ})
via the weak bilinear form

\|u\|_{E,g}^{2}:=\mathcal{N}^{\circ}_{g}(u,u).

(a)

(
Equivalence on the sphere
) There exist
0<c\leq C<\infty
such that, for all
w\in H^{1}(S^{n}_{+})
,

c\,\|w\|_{H^{1}(S^{n}_{+})}^{2}\ \leq\ \|w\|_{E,g_{\mathrm{round}}}^{2}\ \leq\ C\,\|w\|_{H^{1}(S^{n}_{+})}^{2}.

(b)

(
Conformal transfer
) If
\widehat{g}=\phi^{\frac{4}{n-2}}g
and
u=\phi\,v
(equivalently
v=\phi^{-1}u
), then
\|v\|_{E,\widehat{g}}=\|u\|_{E,g}
and
\mathrm{Def}^{\mathrm{Esc}}_{\widehat{g}}(v)=\mathrm{Def}^{\mathrm{Esc}}_{g}(u)
.
In particular, for the Cayley transform
\mathcal{C}:S^{n}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
with its conformal factor,

\|F-V^{\sharp}\|_{E,g_{\mathrm{round}}}=\|f-v^{\sharp}\|_{E,\mathrm{flat}},\qquad\mathrm{Def}^{\mathrm{Esc}}_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}(F)=\mathrm{Def}^{\mathrm{Esc}}_{\mathrm{flat}}(f).

(c)

(
Explicit form and equivalence
) By the Green identity (
2.22
), the graph norm is explicitly

(2.21)

\|u\|_{E,g}^{2}=\int_{M}|\nabla u|^{2}\,dV_{g}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}\int_{M}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,u^{2}\,dV_{g}+\frac{n-2}{2}\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,u^{2}\,d\sigma_{g}.

In particular, on
(S^{n}_{+},g_{\mathrm{round}})
where
H_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}\equiv 0
, this simplifies further.
Consequently, under
(BG
2+
)
and the coercivity assumption
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
,
\|\cdot\|_{E,g}
and
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(M)}
are equivalent.

Proof.

We first state the Green identity for the canonical pair
(L_{g}^{\circ},B_{g}^{\circ})
:

(2.22)

\int_{M}u\,L_{g}^{\circ}v\,dV_{g}+\int_{\partial M}u\,B_{g}^{\circ}v\,d\sigma_{g}=\int_{M}\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\rangle\,dV_{g}+\tfrac{n-2}{4(n-1)}\int_{M}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,uv\,dV_{g}+\tfrac{n-2}{2}\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,uv\,d\sigma_{g}.

(a) Plug
u=v=w
into (
2.22
) with
g=g_{\mathrm{round}}
. Use
R_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}>0
and
