) Assume
(BG
2+
)
and

\max_{\partial M}H_{g}>0
. Then
S_{\mathrm{tr}}(M,g)>S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
, hence attainment as in
(b)
.
Moreover, among small-scale boundary bubbles
v_{x,\varepsilon}
the first-order correction is maximized at
points where
H_{g}
attains its maximum on
\partial M
.

Proof.

(a) Testing
T_{M}
on boundary bubbles supported in a shrinking Fermi ball (after enforcing the mean-zero gauge) and letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0

yields values arbitrarily close to the half-space sharp constant, hence the lower bound.

(b) This is Theorem
2.52
.

(c) (c) Fix
x
with
H_{g}(x)>0
and let
v_{x,\varepsilon}
be the boundary bubble from Lemma
2.49
.
To enforce the homogeneous gauge, set

\widetilde{v}_{x,\varepsilon}:=v_{x,\varepsilon}-\fint_{M}v_{x,\varepsilon}\,dV_{g}\ \in\ H^{1}_{\#}(M).

Then
\int_{M}|\nabla\widetilde{v}_{x,\varepsilon}|^{2}=\int_{M}|\nabla v_{x,\varepsilon}|^{2}
, while

\|\widetilde{v}_{x,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}^{q}=\|v_{x,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}^{q}+o(\varepsilon)
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0

(since
\fint_{M}v_{x,\varepsilon}=O(\varepsilon^{(n+2)/2})
and
q=2^{*}_{\partial}
).
Hence

T_{M}(\widetilde{v}_{x,\varepsilon})=T_{M}(v_{x,\varepsilon})+o(\varepsilon).

By Lemma
2.49
, for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small we have

T_{M}(\widetilde{v}_{x,\varepsilon})>S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
, so
S_{\mathrm{tr}}(M,g)>S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
.
Attainment then follows from (b).
∎

Theorem 2.52

(Regime A: strict super-Euclidean constant
\Rightarrow
attainment)
.

Work in the homogeneous gauge
H^{1}_{\#}(M)
. If

S_{\mathrm{tr}}(M,g)\ >\ S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}),

then every maximizing sequence for
T_{M}
is precompact in
H^{1}(M)
and the supremum is attained.

Proof.

Let
(u_{k})
be maximizing in
H^{1}_{\#}(M)
and normalize
\|\nabla u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(M)}=1
, so

T_{M}(u_{k})=\|u_{k}|_{\partial M}\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\partial M)}^{2^{*}_{\partial}}
, where

2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
. By concentration-compactness for the critical trace embedding,
either
(u_{k})
is precompact in
H^{1}(M)
(hence converges strongly, and the limit attains the supremum),
or else loss of compactness occurs by boundary concentration.

In the latter case there exist boundary points
x_{k}\in\partial M
and scales
\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0

so that in boundary Fermi coordinates
\Phi_{x_{k}}
, define the rescaled functions using a growing cutoff.
Fix
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{2}^{+})
with
\chi\equiv 1
on
B_{1}^{+}
and set
\chi_{R}(y):=\chi(y/R)
.
Let

R_{k}:=\frac{r_{0}}{4\varepsilon_{k}}\ \longrightarrow\ \infty,\qquad f_{k}(y)\ :=\ \varepsilon_{k}^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\;u_{k}\big(\Phi_{x_{k}}(\varepsilon_{k}y)\big)\,\chi_{R_{k}}(y),\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}

are bounded in
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
and do not vanish. Passing to a subsequence, one obtains

f_{k}\to f_{\infty}
strongly in
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
with
f_{\infty}
a half-space extremal for the sharp
trace inequality, hence

\|f_{\infty}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{2^{*}_{\partial}}\;=\;S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}).

Pulling back yields
T_{M}(u_{k})\to S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
, contradicting

S_{\mathrm{tr}}(M,g)>S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
. Therefore bubbling cannot occur and
(u_{k})
is precompact,
so the supremum is attained.
∎

Theorem 2.53

(Regime B: nonpositive first/second order
\Rightarrow
fixed-scale gap for boundary bubbles)
.

Assume
n\geq 4
and
(BG
3+
)
. Suppose
\max_{\partial M}H_{g}\leq 0
, and if
\max_{\partial M}H_{g}=0

assume
\max_{\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})}\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{g}<0
. Then there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and,
for every
\varepsilon_{\ast}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, a constant
\eta=\eta(\varepsilon_{\ast})>0
such that
for all boundary bubbles
v_{x,\varepsilon}
with
\varepsilon\in[\varepsilon_{\ast},\varepsilon_{0}]
,

T_{M}(v_{x,\varepsilon})\ \leq\ S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\,(1-\eta),\qquad\text{uniformly in }x\in\partial M.

In particular, any maximizing sequence that loses compactness by bubbling must have bubbling scales

\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
, and its concentration points must lie in
\arg\max_{\partial M}H_{g}
.

Proof.

If
\max_{\partial M}H_{g}<0
, then by Lemma
2.49
there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and

\eta_{1}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
and all
x\in\partial M
,

T_{M}(v_{x,\varepsilon})\ \leq\ S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\,(1-\eta_{1}\,\varepsilon).

Hence for
\varepsilon\in[\varepsilon_{\ast},\varepsilon_{0}]
one has the uniform bound

T_{M}(v_{x,\varepsilon})\leq S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\,(1-\eta_{1}\,\varepsilon_{\ast})
.

If
\max_{\partial M}H_{g}=0
, then for
x
away from
\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})
one has
H_{g}(x)<0
and we revert
to the previous estimate. For
x
in a neighborhood of
\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})
, the second-order expansion
in Remark
2.50
applies, and the hypothesis

\max_{\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})}\mathfrak{R}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{g}<0
yields (after shrinking
\varepsilon_{0}
if needed)
a uniform estimate of the form

T_{M}(v_{x,\varepsilon})\ \leq\ S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\,(1-\eta_{2}\,\varepsilon^{2})

near
\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})
. Therefore for
\varepsilon\in[\varepsilon_{\ast},\varepsilon_{0}]
,

T_{M}(v_{x,\varepsilon})\leq S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\,(1-\eta_{2}\,\varepsilon_{\ast}^{2})
there as well.
Combining the regions gives the claimed uniform fixed-scale gap.

Finally, if a maximizing sequence bubbles with scales bounded below by some
\varepsilon_{\ast}>0
, the
fixed-scale gap contradicts maximality. Thus bubbling forces
\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
; moreover, the
first-order term in Lemma
2.49
implies the centers must asymptotically maximize

H_{g}
.
∎

Theorem 2.54

(Regime C: positive first-order
\Rightarrow
strict super-Euclidean and attainment)
.

Assume
n\geq 4
,
(BG
2+
)
, and
\max_{\partial M}H_{g}>0
. Work in the homogeneous gauge
H^{1}_{\#}(M)
.
Then

S_{\mathrm{tr}}(M,g)\ >\ S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}),

and hence, by Theorem
2.52
,
T_{M}
attains its supremum on
H^{1}_{\#}(M)
and every maximizing
sequence is precompact in
H^{1}(M)
. Moreover, among small-scale boundary bubbles, the first-order
correction is optimized at points where
H_{g}
attains its maximum on
\partial M
.

Proof.

Pick
x_{0}\in\arg\max_{\partial M}H_{g}
and choose
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. Let

v_{x_{0},\varepsilon}=\mathcal{T}_{x_{0},\varepsilon}U_{+}
be the boundary bubble used in
Lemma
2.49
. Enforce the homogeneous gauge by setting

\tilde{v}_{x_{0},\varepsilon}:=v_{x_{0},\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{\operatorname{Vol}(M)}\int_{M}v_{x_{0},\varepsilon}\,dV_{g}\in H^{1}_{\#}(M).

Since the correction is constant,
\int_{M}|\nabla\tilde{v}_{x_{0},\varepsilon}|^{2}=\int_{M}|\nabla v_{x_{0},\varepsilon}|^{2}
.
Moreover
\int_{M}v_{x_{0},\varepsilon}\,dV_{g}=O(\varepsilon^{\frac{n+2}{2}})
, so the boundary trace changes by

o(\varepsilon)
in
L^{2^{*}_{\partial}}(\partial M)
and does not affect the first-order term. Therefore
Lemma
2.49
yields

T_{M}(\tilde{v}_{x_{0},\varepsilon})=S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\Big(1+\rho_{n}\,H_{g}(x_{0})\,\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon)\Big),\qquad\rho_{n}>0.

Since
H_{g}(x_{0})>0
, the right-hand side is strictly larger than
S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
for all sufficiently
small
\varepsilon
, hence
S_{\mathrm{tr}}(M,g)>S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
. Attainment and precompactness then
follow from Theorem
2.52
. The last sentence follows from the sign of the first-order term in
Lemma
2.49
.
∎

Remark 2.55

(On rigidity at the Euclidean level (plain trace))
.

The rigidity statement "attainment at the model constant forces a round cap / totally umbilic boundary"
belongs to the
Euclidean-level attainment
scenario

S_{\mathrm{tr}}(M,g)=S_{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
together with existence of a maximizer.
We do not use such a rigidity input in the plain-trace addendum; the only rigidity theorem used in the
paper is the conformally covariant Escobar rigidity at
S_{\ast}
in the main thread.

3.
Escobar II: Second-order expansion, renormalized mass, and consequences

Remark 3.1

(Scope of this section)
.

This section develops the
second-order, one-bubble
theory for the covariant Escobar problem:

O(\varepsilon^{2})
Fermi expansions, the renormalized mass
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
, and the precise
second-order expansion for a single boundary bubble. The multi-bubble analysis (Lyapunov-Schmidt
reduction, reduced functionals, interaction kernels, and existence/multiplicity at the threshold)
is deferred to Section
4
. We retain the canonical normalization (
2.6
). All second-order expansions and coefficients below are derived under (BG
3+
) and in the covariant weak pairing. Throughout this section we assume
n\geq 4
.
For cutoff–independent coefficients at order
\varepsilon^{2}
(in particular
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
) we will
additionally assume
n\geq 5
, and for the third–order coefficient
\Theta_{g}
we assume
n\geq 6
.
(The borderline cases
n=4
at second order and
n=5
at third order require logarithmic
renormalizations/cutoff bookkeeping which we do not pursue here.)

3.1.
Fermi expansions up to second order, boundary Jacobians, and moments

Throughout this subsection we assume (BG
3+
) and use semi-geodesic (Fermi) coordinates
relative to the
inner
unit normal on a fixed collar. In particular,

g_{\alpha n}\equiv 0,\qquad g_{nn}\equiv 1\quad\text{on the collar},

and all expansions below are uniform in
x
with constants depending only on the bounded-geometry data.

In Fermi coordinates about
x\in\partial M
, with tangential indices

\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
and normal index
n
, the metric and Jacobian admit the
second-order expansions (uniform in
x
on compact charts):

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}(y)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\beta}-2\,\mathrm{II}_{\alpha\beta}(x)\,y_{n}-\tfrac{1}{3}\,\bar{R}_{\alpha\gamma\beta\delta}(x)\,y_{\gamma}y_{\delta}-2\,(\nabla_{\!{}^{\top}})_{\gamma}\mathrm{II}_{\alpha\beta}(x)\,y_{\gamma}y_{n}

(3.1)

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt+\big((\mathrm{II}^{2})_{\alpha\beta}(x)-R_{\alpha n\beta n}(x)\big)\,y_{n}^{2}+O(|y|^{3}),

\displaystyle g_{\alpha n}(y)

\displaystyle\equiv 0,\qquad g_{nn}(y)\equiv 1,

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}(y)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\beta}+2\,\mathrm{II}^{\alpha\beta}(x)\,y_{n}+\tfrac{1}{3}\,\bar{R}^{\alpha}{}_{\gamma}{}^{\beta}{}_{\delta}(x)\,y_{\gamma}y_{\delta}+2\,(\nabla_{\!{}^{\top}})_{\gamma}\mathrm{II}^{\alpha\beta}(x)\,y_{\gamma}y_{n}

(3.2)

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt+\big(3(\mathrm{II}^{2})^{\alpha\beta}(x)+R^{\alpha}{}_{n}{}^{\beta}{}_{n}(x)\big)\,y_{n}^{2}+O(|y|^{3}),

\displaystyle g^{\alpha n}(y)

\displaystyle\equiv 0,\qquad g^{nn}(y)\equiv 1,

\displaystyle\sqrt{|g|}(y)

\displaystyle=1-H_{g}(x)\,y_{n}-(\nabla_{\!{}^{\top}})_{\gamma}H_{g}(x)\,y_{\gamma}y_{n}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Big(H_{g}(x)^{2}-|\mathrm{II}(x)|^{2}-\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)\Big)\,y_{n}^{2}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\operatorname{Ric}_{\bar{g}}(x)_{\gamma\delta}\,y_{\gamma}y_{\delta}+O(|y|^{3}).

(Here
\bar{R}
and
\operatorname{Ric}_{\bar{g}}
denote, respectively, the Riemann and Ricci curvature of the boundary metric
\bar{g}=g|_{T\partial M}
; indices are raised and lowered with
\bar{g}
, and
(\mathrm{II}^{2})_{\alpha\beta}:=\mathrm{II}_{\alpha}{}^{\gamma}\mathrm{II}_{\beta\gamma}
.)

On the slice
y_{n}=0
(i.e. on
\partial M
), in tangential geodesic normal coordinates at
x
,

(3.4)

d\sigma_{g}\big|_{y_{n}=0}=\sqrt{\det(g_{\alpha\beta}(y^{\prime},0))}\,dy^{\prime}\ =\ \big(1-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\operatorname{Ric}_{\bar{g}}(x)_{\gamma\delta}\,y_{\gamma}y_{\delta}+O(|y^{\prime}|^{3})\big)\,dy^{\prime}.

First and second moments of
U_{+}
.

Let
U_{+}
be the normalized (harmonic) half-space optimizer for the covariant Escobar functional,
with
\|\nabla U_{+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}=1
, and let
\chi_{R}
be a smooth cutoff with
\chi_{R}\equiv 1
on
B_{R}

and
\operatorname{supp}\,\chi_{R}\subset B_{2R}
. Define the truncated moments

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{J}(R)

\displaystyle:=\int_{B^{+}_{2R}}|\nabla(\chi_{R}U_{+})|^{2}\,dy,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{J}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy=1,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}(R)

\displaystyle:=\int_{B^{+}_{2R}}y_{n}\,|\nabla(\chi_{R}U_{+})|^{2}\,dy,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{tan}}(R)

\displaystyle:=\int_{B^{+}_{2R}}y_{n}\,|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}(\chi_{R}U_{+})|^{2}\,dy,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{tan}}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}\,|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}U_{+}|^{2}\,dy,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Theta(R)

\displaystyle:=\int_{B_{2R}\cap\{y_{n}=0\}}|(\chi_{R}U_{+})(y^{\prime},0)|^{2}\,dy^{\prime},

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|U_{+}(y^{\prime},0)|^{2}\,dy^{\prime},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}(R)

\displaystyle:=\int_{B_{2R}\cap\{y_{n}=0\}}|(\chi_{R}U_{+})(y^{\prime},0)|^{q}\,dy^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|U_{+}(y^{\prime},0)|^{q}\,dy^{\prime},\,\,\,q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\tfrac{2(n-1)}{n-2}.

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}(R)

\displaystyle:=\int_{B^{+}_{2R}}y_{n}^{2}\,|\nabla(\chi_{R}U_{+})|^{2}\,dy,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}^{2}\,|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{tan}}(R)

\displaystyle:=\int_{B^{+}_{2R}}y_{n}^{2}\,|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}(\chi_{R}U_{+})|^{2}\,dy,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{tan}}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}^{2}\,|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}U_{+}|^{2}\,dy.

Lemma 3.2

(Half-space bubble: tangential and total
y_{n}^{2}
-moments)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
and let
U_{+}
be the (centered, tangentially radial) harmonic half-space optimizer used throughout
Section
3
. Then the second moments defined in
(
3.10
)–(
3.11
) satisfy

\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{tan}}\ =\ \frac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}.

Equivalently, the normal part carries the other half:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}^{2}\,|\partial_{n}U_{+}|^{2}\,dy\ =\ \frac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}.

Proof.

By classification of half-space Escobar optimizers (and our centering convention), one may take

U_{+}(y^{\prime},y_{n})\ =\ c_{n}\,\big(|y^{\prime}|^{2}+(1+y_{n})^{2}\big)^{-\frac{n-2}{2}},\qquad y_{n}>0,

for some dimensional constant
c_{n}>0
(the value cancels in the ratio below).
Write
r:=|y^{\prime}|
and
a:=1+y_{n}
. Then
U_{+}=c_{n}(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-(n-2)/2}
and a direct computation gives

|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}U_{+}|^{2}=(n-2)^{2}c_{n}^{2}\,\frac{r^{2}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{n}},\qquad|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}=(n-2)^{2}c_{n}^{2}\,\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{n}}.

For each fixed
a>0
, integrate over
y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
in polar coordinates:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}U_{+}|^{2}\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\omega_{n-2}(n-2)^{2}c_{n}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{n}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{n}}\,dr,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\omega_{n-2}(n-2)^{2}c_{n}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{n-2}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{n-1}}\,dr,

since
(r^{2}+a^{2})/(r^{2}+a^{2})^{n}=(r^{2}+a^{2})^{-(n-1)}
.
Now change variables
s=r^{2}
in the first integral:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{n}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{n}}\,dr=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}{(s+a^{2})^{n}}\,ds.

Integrate by parts with
u=s^{\frac{n-1}{2}}
and
dv=(s+a^{2})^{-n}\,ds
so that

du=\frac{n-1}{2}s^{\frac{n-3}{2}}ds
and
v=-\frac{1}{n-1}(s+a^{2})^{-(n-1)}
.
The boundary term vanishes (since
n\geq 5
gives integrability at
\infty
), hence

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{\frac{n-1}{2}}}{(s+a^{2})^{n}}\,ds=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{\frac{n-3}{2}}}{(s+a^{2})^{n-1}}\,ds.

Reverting to
r
yields

\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{n}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{n}}\,dr=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{r^{n-2}}{(r^{2}+a^{2})^{n-1}}\,dr.

Thus for every
a>0
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}U_{+}|^{2}\,dy^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy^{\prime}.

Multiplying by
y_{n}^{2}=(a-1)^{2}
and integrating
y_{n}\in(0,\infty)
gives

\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{tan}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}
.
∎

Dimensional note.
In
n=3
the
L^{2}
-based trace moment
\Theta
, and likewise the global moments
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)},\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{tan}}
, are infinite. In that case we do not use these global moments to define coefficients such as
\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}
, which are instead defined via the spherical model as in Remark
2.12
. Likewise, the
y_{n}^{2}
-moments
\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{tan}}
are finite iff
n\geq 5
; in
n=4
they exhibit the expected logarithmic divergence.

Lemma 3.3

(No linear trace correction; leading
O(\varepsilon)
numerator under (BG
2+
))
.

Fix
x\in\partial M
and
R\gg 1
. Define

(3.12)

U_{+,x,\varepsilon}(z):=\varepsilon^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}\,U_{+}\!\left(\frac{\Phi_{x}^{-1}(z)}{\varepsilon}\right)\,\chi_{R}\!\left(\frac{\Phi_{x}^{-1}(z)}{\varepsilon}\right)\quad\text{for }z\in\Phi_{x}(\varepsilon B_{2R}^{+}),\qquad 0\ \text{outside}.

and set
u_{x,\varepsilon}:=U_{+,x,\varepsilon}
, where
U_{+}
is the
harmonic
half-space optimizer
for the covariant Escobar problem normalized by

\|\nabla U_{+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}=1
.
By choosing the centered representative on the optimizer manifold, we may and do assume that
U_{+}
is
tangentially radial
about the origin. In particular, the
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
-contribution in the first-order gradient correction averages out (cf. the isotropic reduction in (
2.9
)). Here
q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
, and the cutoffs
\chi_{R}
are taken from an admissible
tangentially radial
class:
we fix a single radial cutoff
\chi=\chi(|y|)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\chi\equiv 1
on
B_{1}
and

\operatorname{supp}\chi\subset B_{2}
, and set
\chi_{R}(y):=\chi(y/R)
. In particular,
\chi_{R}U_{+}
remains
tangentially radial.
Since
\chi_{R}
is obtained by scaling a fixed profile,
\|\nabla\chi_{R}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim R^{-1}
and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}|U_{+}|^{2}\,|\nabla\chi_{R}|^{2}\;\lesssim\;R^{-2}\int_{B_{2R}^{+}\setminus B_{R}^{+}}|U_{+}|^{2}\;=\;o(1)\quad(R\to\infty),

so
\mathfrak{J}(R)=\int|\nabla(\chi_{R}U_{+})|^{2}\to\int|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}=1
.
Then, as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
with
R
fixed,
uniformly for
x
in compact subsets of
\partial M
,

(3.13)

\displaystyle\|u_{x,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}^{q}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{T}(R)+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.14)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\langle\nabla u_{x,\varepsilon},\nabla u_{x,\varepsilon}\rangle_{g}\,dV_{g}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{J}(R)\ +\ \varepsilon\,H_{g}(x)\Big(\tfrac{2}{n-1}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{tan}}(R)-\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}(R)\Big)\ +\ O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,u_{x,\varepsilon}^{2}\,d\sigma_{g}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\,H_{g}(x)\,\Theta(R)\ +\ O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Consequently, for the Escobar
quotient
(
1.2
) one has

(3.16)

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{x,\varepsilon}]\ =\ S_{\ast}(R)\Big(1+\rho_{n,R}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})\Big),

where

(3.17)

S_{\ast}(R)\ :=\ \frac{\mathfrak{J}(R)}{\mathfrak{T}(R)^{2/q}},

and the
first-order conformal coefficient

(3.18)

\rho_{n,R}^{\mathrm{conf}}\;=\;\frac{\Big(\tfrac{2}{n-1}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{tan}}(R)-\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}(R)\Big)+\tfrac{n-2}{2}\,\Theta(R)}{\mathfrak{J}(R)}.

Letting
R\to\infty
, we define
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}:=\lim_{R\to\infty}\rho_{n,R}^{\mathrm{conf}}
. For
n\geq 4
, the moments converge individually (since
\mathfrak{J}(R)\to 1
) and

(3.19)

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\;=\;\Big(\tfrac{2}{n-1}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{tan}}-\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}\Big)+\frac{n-2}{2}\,\Theta.

(In dimension
n=3
we do not pass
R\to\infty
in this section; the coefficient

\rho_{3}^{\mathrm{conf}}
is defined via the hemisphere model as in Remark
2.12
.) In particular, for
n\geq 4
, letting
R\to\infty
in the admissible cutoff scheme
(equivalently, along a diagonal
R=R(\varepsilon)\to\infty
with
\varepsilon R(\varepsilon)\to 0
),
the corresponding cutoff–independent boundary bubble

v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}\quad\text{(understood via the above cutoff/limit convention)}

satisfies

\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]=S_{\ast}\big(1+\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon\big)+o(\varepsilon),\qquad S_{\ast}=C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(S^{n}_{+}),\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow 0).

Lemma 3.4

(Second-order covariant transfer with cutoff-independent limit under (BG
3+
))
.

Assume
n\geq 4
and
(BG
3+
)
. Fix
R\gg 1
and let
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
(the cutoff-independent limits stated below are asserted under the additional assumption
n\geq 5
); afterwards we pass
R\to\infty
as indicated below. For each fixed
R
,
uniformly for
x
in compact subsets of
\partial M
,

(3.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{x,\varepsilon}]=S_{\ast}(R)\Big(1+\rho_{n,R}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}\big(\kappa_{1,R}\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)+\kappa_{2,R}\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x)+\mathcal{Q}_{R}(\mathring{\mathrm{II}}(x))\big)+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{3})\Big),

where
\kappa_{1,R},\kappa_{2,R}
are dimensional numbers and
\mathcal{Q}_{R}
is a quadratic form in

\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
, all depending on
R
through weighted moments of
U_{+}
clipped by
\chi_{R}
.
Moreover, if
n\geq 5
, then as
R\to\infty
,

(3.21)

\kappa_{j,R}\ \longrightarrow\ \kappa_{j}\quad(j=1,2),\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{R}\ \longrightarrow\ \mathcal{Q},

and the limits depend only on
n
(not on the cutoff profile). Equivalently,

(3.22)

\frac{\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{x,\varepsilon}]}{S_{\ast}(R)}=1+\rho_{n,R}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}\big(\kappa_{1,R}\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)+\kappa_{2,R}\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x)+\mathcal{Q}_{R}(\mathring{\mathrm{II}}(x))\big)+O_{R}(\varepsilon^{3}).

Proof.

Use the second-order Fermi expansions (
3.1
)–(
3.3
) and the boundary
surface element (
3.4
). The
O(\varepsilon)
term is given by Lemma
3.3
.
At order
\varepsilon^{2}
, contracting the
y_{n}^{2}
and
|y^{\prime}|^{2}
coefficients with the tangential/normal
quadratic forms built from
U_{+}
produces linear combinations of
\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)
,
\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}
,
and a quadratic form in
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
. Derivative terms at order
\varepsilon^{2}
(the
y_{\gamma}y_{n}
terms involving
(\nabla_{\!{}^{\top}})\mathrm{II}
and
(\nabla_{\!{}^{\top}})H_{g}
in (
3.1
) and (
3.3
)) are odd in
y^{\prime}
and vanish by tangential radiality of
U_{+}
; such effects first contribute at order
O(\varepsilon^{3})
(cf. the
\Delta_{\partial}H_{g}
channel in (
3.30
)).
Linear
\mathrm{II}
-terms cancel because, with
U_{+}
tangentially radial (centered representative), the
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
-contribution averages to zero in the angular variable on each
\{y_{n}=\mathrm{const}\}
slice. Writing
u_{x,\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}(\chi_{R}U_{+})\!\left(\Phi_{x}^{-1}(\cdot)/\varepsilon\right)
,
each coefficient is a (cutoff-dependent) weighted moment of
\chi_{R}U_{+}
. This yields (
3.20
)
with coefficients
\kappa_{j,R}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{R}
expressed through such truncated moments.

If
n\geq 5
, the weighted moments appearing at order
\varepsilon^{2}
are integrable at infinity, and the cutoff can be removed by dominated convergence, so
\kappa_{j,R}\to\kappa_{j}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{R}\to\mathcal{Q}
as
R\to\infty
. The denominator has no
O(\varepsilon)
correction and only an
O_{R}(\varepsilon^{2})
one (by
(
3.13
)); linearizing the quotient produces (
3.20
), and taking
R\to\infty
after
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
yields (
3.22
).
∎

Definition 3.5

(Renormalized mass)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
and
(BG
3+
)
. Let
U_{+}
be the normalized (harmonic) half-space optimizer for the covariant Escobar functional, and let

v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
be the corresponding boundary bubble at
x\in\partial M
and scale
\varepsilon
(with the usual admissible cutoff/limit convention from §
3.1
).

The second-order one-bubble expansion (Lemma
3.4
) yields, uniformly for
x
on compact boundary collars,

\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]=S_{\ast}\Big(1+\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon\ +\ \mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon^{2}\Big)\ +\ o(\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad\varepsilon\downarrow 0.

If
n\geq 6
, the remainder improves to
O(\varepsilon^{3})
. We
define
the
renormalized mass

\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)
to be the coefficient of
\varepsilon^{2}
in this expansion.
Equivalently, for an admissible cutoff family
\chi_{R}
and
u_{x,\varepsilon}=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R}U_{+})
,

\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x):=\lim_{R\to\infty}\ \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\ \frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{x,\varepsilon}]}{S_{\ast}(R)}-1-\rho^{\mathrm{conf}}_{n,R}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon\right),

where the order of limits is as in §
3.1
.

Moreover, by Lemma
3.4
, there exist universal constants
\kappa_{1}(n),\kappa_{2}(n)
and a universal quadratic form

\mathcal{Q}
on trace-free symmetric 2-tensors on
T_{x}\partial M
such that

\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\ =\ \kappa_{1}(n)\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)\ +\ \kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x)\ +\ \mathcal{Q}(\mathring{\mathrm{II}}(x)).

Moreover, due to tangential radiality, the quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}
is isotropic, hence

\mathcal{Q}(\mathring{\mathrm{II}})\ =\ \kappa_{3}(n)\,|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2},

and for
n\geq 5
one has
\kappa_{3}(n)<0
; see Proposition
3.7

and Lemma
3.2
.

3.2.
Renormalized Mass and the One-Bubble Expansion

Theorem 3.6

(One–bubble expansion with cutoff–independent coefficient)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
, and
(BG
3+
)
.
Let
v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
denote the cutoff-independent boundary bubble defined
via the iterated cutoff convention of §
3.1
(i.e. first
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
with
R
fixed, then
R\to\infty
).
Equivalently, one may realize this iterated limit along a diagonal
R=R(\varepsilon)\to\infty
with
\varepsilon R(\varepsilon)\to 0

chosen so that the truncation errors (including
S_{\ast}(R)-S_{\ast}
and the
O_{R}(\varepsilon^{3})
remainders in Lemma
3.4
)
are
o(\varepsilon^{2})
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
Then, uniformly for
x
on compact boundary collars,

\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]=S_{\ast}\Big(1+\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon+\varepsilon^{2}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\Big)\ +\ o(\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad\varepsilon\downarrow 0,

where
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is the renormalized mass from Definition
3.5
. If
n\geq 6
, the remainder improves to
O(\varepsilon^{3})
. Moreover, due to tangential radiality of
U_{+}
, the quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}
in Definition
3.5
is isotropic:

\mathcal{Q}(\mathring{\mathrm{II}})=\kappa_{3}(n)\,|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
with

\kappa_{3}(n)\ <\ 0\qquad(n\geq 5),

cf. Proposition
3.7
and Lemma
3.2
.

Proof.

Fix
R
and apply Lemma
3.4
to
\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R}U_{+})
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, then let
R\to\infty

as in Definition
3.5
. Equivalently, one may implement the same iterated limit along a diagonal
R=R(\varepsilon)
for which all truncation
errors are
o(\varepsilon^{2})
.
For
n\geq 5
the
O(\varepsilon^{2})
coefficients admit cutoff-independent limits as
R\to\infty
,
yielding the stated expansion for
v_{x,\varepsilon}
with an
o(\varepsilon^{2})
remainder (and
O(\varepsilon^{3})
if
n\geq 6
).
Isotropy of
\mathcal{Q}
follows from tangential radiality of
U_{+}
.
∎

Proposition 3.7

(Structure and signs of the
O(\varepsilon^{2})
coefficient without closed forms)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
and
(BG
3+
)
. Let
U_{+}
be the harmonic half-space optimizer, normalized by
\|\nabla U_{+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})}=1
, and let
q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
. The renormalized mass
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)
(Definition
3.5
) decomposes as a linear combination of
\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)
,
\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x)
, and a quadratic form in
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}(x)
. Moreover:

•

\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)
is independent of
H_{g}(x)^{2}
.

•

\kappa_{3}(n)<0
for
n\geq 5
; in particular the traceless second-fundamental-form channel
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
enters with a strictly negative coefficient.

•

\kappa_{2}(n)>0
; the boundary scalar curvature channel
\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}
contributes with positive sign.

•

\kappa_{1}(n)
is a difference of positive moment terms and has no universal sign
a priori
.

Proof of Proposition
3.7
.

Fix
x\in\partial M
and work in a Fermi chart
\Phi_{x}
with the second-order expansions
(
3.1
)–(
3.3
) and the boundary surface element (
3.4
).
Let
u_{x,\varepsilon}=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R}U_{+})
.

Independence from
H^{2}
and the
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
channel (correct sign).

In the Dirichlet integrand
g^{ab}\partial_{a}u\,\partial_{b}u\,\sqrt{|g|}
, the only
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
-terms
at order
\varepsilon^{2}
come from the
y_{n}^{2}
-coefficients in
g^{\alpha\beta}
and
\sqrt{|g|}
.
Indeed, expanding (
3.2
)–(
3.3
) and retaining only the
y_{n}^{2}
pieces gives

g^{\alpha\beta}(y)\sqrt{|g|}(y)=\delta^{\alpha\beta}+\Big(3(\mathrm{II}^{2})^{\alpha\beta}-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathrm{II}|^{2}\,\delta^{\alpha\beta}\Big)\,y_{n}^{2}+\ (\text{terms involving }H,\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu),\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M})\ +\ O(|y|^{3}),

so the traceless channel is extracted by writing
\mathrm{II}=\tfrac{H}{n-1}\bar{g}+\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
.
The cross term from
(2y_{n}\mathrm{II}^{\alpha\beta})(1-Hy_{n})
is linear in
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
and averages to
0

by tangential radiality of
U_{+}
; hence it does not contribute to
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
.
After tangential angular averaging one uses

(\mathring{\mathrm{II}}^{2})^{\alpha\beta}\,\partial_{\alpha}U_{+}\,\partial_{\beta}U_{+}\ \leadsto\ \frac{|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}}{n-1}\,|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}U_{+}|^{2},

so the
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
coefficient in the numerator is proportional to

\frac{3}{n-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}^{2}\,|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}U_{+}|^{2}\,dy\ -\ \frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}^{2}\,|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy.

Since the
\varepsilon^{2}
correction in the denominator depends only on the intrinsic boundary curvature (hence is absorbed into the

\kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}
channel) and produces no additional quadratic form in
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
beyond that channel,
the residual
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
contribution defining
\kappa_{3}(n)
comes from the numerator term displayed above.
By Lemma
3.2
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}^{2}\,|\nabla_{\!\mathrm{tan}}U_{+}|^{2}\,dy=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}y_{n}^{2}\,|\nabla U_{+}|^{2}\,dy=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}.

Therefore, for
n\geq 5
,

\kappa_{3}(n)=\left(\frac{3}{n-1}\cdot\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=\frac{4-n}{2(n-1)}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}\ <\ 0.

This yields the asserted strict negativity of the
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
channel in
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
for
n\geq 5
.

The
\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}
channel.

The term
\tfrac{1}{3}R_{\alpha\gamma\beta\delta}\,y_{\gamma}y_{\delta}
in
g^{\alpha\beta}
contributes
positively to the bulk Dirichlet energy after tangential averaging, yielding a multiple of

\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x)\int|y^{\prime}|^{2}|\nabla(\chi_{R}U_{+})|^{2}
.
The boundary measure correction (
3.4
) contributes

-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g|\partial M}\,{}_{\gamma\delta}y_{\gamma}y_{\delta}
to the
L^{q}
norm; upon
linearizing the quotient, this also enters with a positive prefactor (
-2/q
times the negative sign).
Hence both sources contribute with the same (positive) sign, proving
\kappa_{2}(n)>0
.

The
\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)
channel.

At order
\varepsilon^{2}
there are competing contributions of opposite sign (e.g., the
R_{\alpha n\beta n}\,y_{n}^{2}
term in
g^{\alpha\beta}
vs the Jacobian term
-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)\,y_{n}^{2}
).
Thus
\kappa_{1}(n)
is a difference of positive moment terms and has no universal sign a priori.
∎

3.3.
Sampling observability without projections

Set

E(x,\varepsilon):=\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]-S_{\ast},

where
v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
denotes the cutoff-independent boundary bubble,
understood via an admissible diagonal cutoff scheme
R=R(\varepsilon)\to\infty
with
\varepsilon R(\varepsilon)\to 0

(cf. §
3.1
). For
\varepsilon
sufficiently small the diagonal may be chosen so that

v_{x,k\varepsilon}
is well-defined for
k=1,2,3
within the fixed collar.
Assuming
n\geq 7
, (BG
4+
), and locally constant
H_{g}
, the renormalized expansion holds up to
O(\varepsilon^{4})
, uniformly on compact collars (see Lemma
3.27
and §
3.1
):

E(x,\varepsilon)=S_{\ast}\Big(\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon+\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon^{2}+\Theta_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon^{3}\Big)+O(\varepsilon^{4}),

with
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0
and where
\mathfrak{R}_{g},\Theta_{g}
are the cutoff-independent coefficients from the one-bubble expansion (Theorem
3.6
and Lemma
3.27
). Here
S_{\ast}=C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(S^{n}_{+})
.

Theorem 3.8

(Three-scale, projection-free de-biasing)
.

Assume
n\geq 7
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
, and
(BG
4+
)
. Assume
H_{g}
is locally constant. Fix
x
in a Fermi collar and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small. Define

A_{k}(x;\varepsilon):=\frac{E(x,k\varepsilon)}{k\varepsilon},\qquad k=1,2,3,

and
\Delta_{1}:=A_{2}-A_{1}
,
\Delta_{2}:=A_{3}-A_{2}
. Then the estimators

\widehat{\Theta}_{\varepsilon}(x):=\frac{\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1}}{2\,S_{\ast}\,\varepsilon^{2}},\qquad\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\varepsilon}(x):=\frac{\Delta_{1}}{S_{\ast}\,\varepsilon}-3\,\varepsilon\,\widehat{\Theta}_{\varepsilon}(x),

\widehat{H}_{\varepsilon}(x):=\frac{1}{\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}}\Bigg(\frac{A_{1}(x;\varepsilon)}{S_{\ast}}-\varepsilon\,\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\varepsilon}(x)-\varepsilon^{2}\,\widehat{\Theta}_{\varepsilon}(x)\Bigg)

satisfy the uniform de-biasing bounds

\big|\widehat{H}_{\varepsilon}(x)-H_{g}(x)\big|\ \leq\ C\,\varepsilon^{3},\qquad\big|\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\varepsilon}(x)-\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\big|\ \leq\ C\,\varepsilon^{2},\qquad\big|\widehat{\Theta}_{\varepsilon}(x)-\Theta_{g}(x)\big|\ \leq\ C\,\varepsilon,

with
C=C(M,g,n)
independent of
x
in the collar. All uses of
E(x,\varepsilon)
are for the Escobar quotient
\mathcal{J}_{g}
; the
O(\varepsilon^{3})
remainders in
A_{k}(x;\varepsilon)
are uniform in
k\in\{1,2,3\}
and
x
on compact collars, and
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0
.

Proof sketch.

For the quotient
\mathcal{J}_{g}
, write

A_{k}=S_{\ast}\big(\rho H+(k\varepsilon)\mathfrak{R}+(k\varepsilon)^{2}\Theta\big)+O(\varepsilon^{3}),

uniformly for
k\in\{1,2,3\}
, where
\rho=\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
,
H=H_{g}(x)
,
\mathfrak{R}=\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)
and
\Theta=\Theta_{g}(x)
. (The
O(\varepsilon^{3})
remainder in
A_{k}
corresponds to the
O(\varepsilon^{4})
remainder in
E(x,k\varepsilon)
.) Then

\Delta_{1}=S_{\ast}\big(\varepsilon\mathfrak{R}+3\varepsilon^{2}\Theta\big)+O(\varepsilon^{3}),\qquad\Delta_{2}=S_{\ast}\big(\varepsilon\mathfrak{R}+5\varepsilon^{2}\Theta\big)+O(\varepsilon^{3}).

Hence

\widehat{\Theta}_{\varepsilon}=\frac{\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{1}}{2S_{\ast}\varepsilon^{2}}=\Theta+O(\varepsilon).

For the renormalized mass,

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\varepsilon}=\frac{\Delta_{1}}{S_{\ast}\varepsilon}-3\varepsilon\widehat{\Theta}_{\varepsilon}=\big(\mathfrak{R}+3\Theta\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big)-3\varepsilon\big(\Theta+O(\varepsilon)\big)=\mathfrak{R}+O(\varepsilon^{2}).

Finally,

\displaystyle\widehat{H}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}\Big(\frac{A_{1}}{S_{\ast}}-\varepsilon\,\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon^{2}\widehat{\Theta}_{\varepsilon}\Big)

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}\Big(\big(\rho H+\varepsilon\mathfrak{R}+\varepsilon^{2}\Theta+O(\varepsilon^{3})\big)-\varepsilon\big(\mathfrak{R}+O(\varepsilon^{2})\big)-\varepsilon^{2}\big(\Theta+O(\varepsilon)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=H+O(\varepsilon^{3}),

uniformly in
x
.
∎

Remark 3.9

(Explicit constants and robustness)
.

All constants are explicit:
S_{\ast}=C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(S^{n}_{+})
and
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
is given in Lemma
2.42
(see also (
2.18
)). The estimators use only the energies
E(x,k\varepsilon)
of the raw test bubbles
v_{x,k\varepsilon}
for the quotient
\mathcal{J}_{g}
; no modulation or projection is required. A two-scale variant recovers
H_{g}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
with slightly weaker rates.

Theorem 3.10

(Model-free boundary observability for Escobar)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
. Let
(M,g)
satisfy
(BG
3+
)
and assume
H_{g}
is locally constant. Let

v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
and set

E(x,\varepsilon):=\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]-S_{\ast}.

Then for any
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
,

\widehat{H}_{g}(x):=\frac{E(x,\varepsilon)}{S_{\ast}\,\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,\varepsilon}\quad\text{satisfies}\quad\big|\widehat{H}_{g}(x)-H_{g}(x)\big|\ \leq\ C\,\varepsilon,

with
C=C(M,g,n)
uniform on compact collars. Here
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0
(Lemma
2.42
). Moreover, under the assumptions of Theorem
3.8
, the three-scale de-biasing recovers
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
and
\Theta_{g}
with the stated rates.

Cutoff/limits.

Fix an admissible cutoff family
\{\chi_{R}\}
(smooth,
\chi_{R}\equiv 1
on
B_{R}
,
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{R}\subset B_{2R}
).
For each small
\varepsilon
we
realize
the cutoff–independent bubble by a diagonal truncation

v_{x,\varepsilon}\ :=\ \mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R(\varepsilon)}U_{+}),\qquad R(\varepsilon)\to\infty,\ \ \varepsilon R(\varepsilon)\to 0,

chosen so that all truncation errors are absorbed into the stated remainders (uniformly for
x
on compact collars and for the finitely many scales used below; e.g.
\varepsilon,2\varepsilon
in Theorem
3.12
).
The cutoff–independent coefficients (such as
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}},\mathfrak{R}_{g},\Theta_{g}
) are defined by the
iterated
limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
at fixed
R
, then
R\to\infty
as in Lemma
3.4
and Lemma
3.27
.

3.4.
Modulated projection and quantitative measurement

Definition 3.11

(Modulated projection to a prescribed scale)
.

Let
u
lie in a small
H^{1}
-neighbourhood of the boundary bubble manifold
\mathcal{B}_{\partial}
.
By Lemma
2.16
, there exist unique parameters

(a^{\sharp}(u),x^{\sharp}(u),\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u))\in(0,\infty)\times\partial M\times(0,\varepsilon_{0}),\qquad w^{\sharp}(u)\in H^{1}(M),

such that

u\;=\;a^{\sharp}(u)\,U_{+,x^{\sharp}(u),\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u)}\;+\;w^{\sharp}(u),

and
w^{\sharp}(u)
satisfies the modulation orthogonality conditions in the covariant graph pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E,g}
together with the boundary slice constraint

\int_{\partial M}\big(a^{\sharp}(u)\,U_{+,x^{\sharp}(u),\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u)}\big)^{q-1}\,w^{\sharp}(u)\,d\sigma_{g}\;=\;0,\qquad q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}.

(We will record only
(x^{\sharp}(u),\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u))
below; the amplitude
a^{\sharp}(u)
is irrelevant for

\mathcal{J}_{g}
by homogeneity.)

For any prescribed scale
\eta\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
we define the
modulated projection

\mathcal{P}(u;\eta)\ :=\ v_{x^{\sharp}(u),\eta}.

Here
v_{x,\eta}
is the cutoff-independent boundary bubble, understood with the cutoff/limits convention fixed in this subsection
(e.g. realized by an admissible diagonal truncation
v_{x,\eta}=\mathcal{T}_{x,\eta}(\chi_{R(\eta)}U_{+})
with

R(\eta)\to\infty
and
\eta R(\eta)\to 0
, chosen so that the truncation error is absorbed into the stated remainders at the
finitely many scales used).

Theorem 3.12

(Quantitative inverse/measurement principle)
.

Assume
n\geq 7
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
,
(BG
4+
)
, and that
H_{g}
is locally constant. Assume also that
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
.
Let
u
be a Palais-Smale near-extremal
1
1

1

By a PS near-extremal at level
S_{\ast}
we mean
u\in\mathcal{S}
with

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u]=S_{\ast}+o(1)
and
\|\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(u)\|_{(H^{1})^{\ast}}=o(1)
.
for the Escobar
quotient
at level
S_{\ast}=C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(S^{n}_{+})
,
and assume
u
lies in the boundary channel so that Lemma
2.16
applies.
Let
(x^{\sharp}(u),\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u))
be its modulation parameters. Set

\Delta(v):=\frac{\mathcal{J}_{g}[v]-S_{\ast}}{S_{\ast}},\,\,M(u):=\frac{\Delta\big(\mathcal{P}(u;\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u))\big)}{\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u)},\,\,N(u):=\frac{\Delta\big(\mathcal{P}(u;2\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u))\big)-2\,\Delta\big(\mathcal{P}(u;\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u))\big)}{(\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u))^{2}}.

Then there exists
c_{0}>0
(depending only on bounded-geometry data) such that if
\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u)\leq c_{0}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\ \rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}\big(x^{\sharp}(u)\big)\ -\ \big(M(u)-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u)\,N(u)\big)\ \Big|

\displaystyle\ \leq\ C\,(\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u))^{2},

\displaystyle\Big|\ \mathfrak{R}_{g}\big(x^{\sharp}(u)\big)\ -\ \tfrac{1}{2}\,N(u)\ \Big|

\displaystyle\ \leq\ C\,\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u),

with
C=C(M,g,n)
. The admissible cutoff class and order of limits are as in the cutoff/limits note following Theorem
3.10
.

Proof.

Write
x^{\sharp}=x^{\sharp}(u)
and
\varepsilon^{\sharp}=\varepsilon^{\sharp}(u)
. By the one-bubble
third-order expansion under (BG
4+
) and constant
H_{g}
(Theorem
3.6
and Lemma
3.27
), applied at scales
\eta\in\{\varepsilon^{\sharp},2\varepsilon^{\sharp}\}
,

\Delta\big(\mathcal{P}(u;\eta)\big)=\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x^{\sharp})\,\eta\ +\ \mathfrak{R}_{g}(x^{\sharp})\,\eta^{2}\ +\ \Theta_{g}(x^{\sharp})\,\eta^{3}\ +\ O(\eta^{4}).

Hence

M(u)=\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x^{\sharp})\ +\ \mathfrak{R}_{g}(x^{\sharp})\,\varepsilon^{\sharp}\ +\ \Theta_{g}(x^{\sharp})\,(\varepsilon^{\sharp})^{2}\ +\ O\big((\varepsilon^{\sharp})^{3}\big),

and

N(u)=2\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x^{\sharp})\ +\ 6\,\Theta_{g}(x^{\sharp})\,\varepsilon^{\sharp}\ +\ O\big((\varepsilon^{\sharp})^{2}\big).

Therefore

\frac{N(u)}{2}=\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x^{\sharp})+3\Theta_{g}(x^{\sharp})\varepsilon^{\sharp}+O\big((\varepsilon^{\sharp})^{2}\big),

and

M(u)-\frac{\varepsilon^{\sharp}}{2}N(u)=\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x^{\sharp})\ -\ 2\,\Theta_{g}(x^{\sharp})\,(\varepsilon^{\sharp})^{2}\ +\ O\big((\varepsilon^{\sharp})^{3}\big),

which implies the stated bounds.
∎

Remark 3.13

(Why the projection uses only the bubble)
.

The operator
\mathcal{P}(u;\eta)
keeps the modulated center
x^{\sharp}(u)
and drops the
remainder. This avoids singular error terms of the form

\varepsilon^{-2}\|w^{\sharp}(u)\|^{2}
that arise if one attempts to transport the full remainder
across scales.

Remark 3.14

(Explicit constants and robustness)
.

All constants are explicit:
S_{\ast}=C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(S^{n}_{+})
and
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
is given by (
2.52
) (equivalently (
2.18
)).
For the single-scale and three-scale estimators (Theorems
3.10
and
3.8
), the estimators use only the quotient energies

E(x,k\varepsilon)=\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,k\varepsilon}]-S_{\ast}
of the cutoff-independent boundary bubbles

v_{x,k\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,k\varepsilon}U_{+}
(equivalently, the limit as
R\to\infty
of
\mathcal{T}_{x,k\varepsilon}(\chi_{R}U_{+})
in the admissible cutoff class); no modulation or projection is required.

A two-scale variant recovers
H_{g}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
with slightly weaker rates.

3.5.
Escobar-Willmore package: mass decomposition, closure, and consequences

Throughout this section we work in the
covariant
Escobar setting.
We recall that, for the tangentially radial half-space optimizer
U_{+}
,
the first-order expansion along the boundary bubble
v_{x,\varepsilon}=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
reads

\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]=S_{\ast}\Bigl(1+\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon\Bigr)+O(\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0.

Cutoff/limits.
As in §
3.1
, bubbles are built with admissible cutoffs
\chi_{R}
(smooth,
\chi_{R}\equiv 1
on
B_{R}
,
\operatorname{supp}\,\chi_{R}\subset B_{2R}
), and coefficients are understood as cutoff-independent limits obtained by first sending
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
at fixed
R
, then
R\to\infty
(or along a diagonal with
\varepsilon R(\varepsilon)\to 0
).

3.5.1.
Canonical splitting of the boundary mass
\mathfrak{R}_{g}

Let
\Sigma=\partial M
, let
\bar{g}:=g|_{T\Sigma}
be the induced boundary metric, let
\mathrm{II}
be the second fundamental form,
H=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\mathrm{II}
the mean curvature (with respect
to the inner unit normal
\nu
), and
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}:=\mathrm{II}-\tfrac{H}{n-1}\,\bar{g}
the trace-free part (measuring deviation from umbilicity).

Theorem 3.15

(Escobar-Willmore decomposition of
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
and
(BG
3+
)
. There exist explicit dimension-dependent constants

\kappa_{3}(n)\neq 0
,
\kappa_{1}(n)
, and
\kappa_{2}(n)
(given by radial moments of the half-space optimizer, matching Definition
3.5
and Proposition
3.7
)
such that, for every
x\in\Sigma
,

(3.23)

\quad\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)=\kappa_{3}(n)\,|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}(x)|^{2}\;+\;\kappa_{1}(n)\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)\;+\;\kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x)\,.\quad

In particular, for
n\geq 5
one has the strict universal sign
\kappa_{3}(n)<0
.

Proof sketch.

This is a restatement of the decomposition derived in Proposition
3.7
, which follows directly from the definition of
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
as the coefficient of
\varepsilon^{2}
in the Fermi expansion. The negativity of
\kappa_{3}(n)
and positivity of
\kappa_{2}(n)
is established in the proof of Proposition
3.7
by analyzing the contributing moments and the cancellation of
H^{2}
terms.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

In the present second-order framework (where
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is defined via cutoff-independent limits of second-order moments), the statement and the sign
\kappa_{3}(n)<0
are asserted for
n\geq 5
.

Corollary 3.17

(Isotropic recovery of
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
from three–scale energies)
.

Assume
n\geq 7
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
,
(BG
4+
)
, and that
H_{g}
is locally constant in a boundary collar.
Let
v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
be the cutoff-independent canonical (tangentially radial) boundary test bubble at
x\in\partial M

(equivalently, built along an admissible diagonal cutoff scheme
R=R(\varepsilon)\to\infty
with
\varepsilon R(\varepsilon)\to 0
), and set

E(x,\varepsilon):=\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]-S_{\ast}
.
Define the three–scale estimators
A_{k}(x;\varepsilon)=E(x,k\varepsilon)/(k\varepsilon)
,
k=1,2,3
, and the de-biased estimate
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\varepsilon}(x)
of the renormalized mass as in Theorem
3.8
.
Then, with the decomposition from Theorem
3.15
,

\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\ =\ \kappa_{3}(n)\,|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}(x)|^{2}\ +\ \kappa_{1}(n)\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)\ +\ \kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x),

the isotropic estimator

(3.24)

\widehat{\,|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}\,}_{\varepsilon}(x)\ :=\ \frac{1}{\kappa_{3}(n)}\Big(\,\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\varepsilon}(x)\ -\ \kappa_{1}(n)\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)\ -\ \kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x)\,\Big)

satisfies the uniform error bound

\big|\ \widehat{\,|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}\,}_{\varepsilon}(x)\ -\ |\mathring{\mathrm{II}}(x)|^{2}\ \big|\ \leq\ C\,\varepsilon^{2},

for all
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
, with
C=C(M,g,n)
independent of
x
in the collar. In particular, the
scalar
anisotropy channel
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
is quantitatively observable using only isotropic probes.

Cutoff/limits.
All coefficients are understood as cutoff-independent limits obtained as in §
3.1
; the constants
\kappa_{j}(n)
appearing above are the cutoff-independent limits from Definition
3.5
and Proposition
3.7
. In this corollary we only use these second-order objects for
n\geq 5
.

Proof sketch.

By Theorem
3.8
, the three–scale de-biasing yields

\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)+O(\varepsilon^{2})

uniformly on collars (the
H
and
\Theta
terms are eliminated at the level of
A_{k}
).
By the Escobar–Willmore decomposition (Theorem
3.15
) and
\kappa_{3}(n)\neq 0
(in fact
\kappa_{3}(n)<0
for
n\geq 5
),

|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}(x)|^{2}=\frac{1}{\kappa_{3}(n)}\Big(\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)-\kappa_{1}(n)\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)-\kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x)\Big).

Inserting
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\varepsilon}(x)
in place of
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)
gives the estimator
(
3.24
); the stated
O(\varepsilon^{2})
error follows.
∎

Remark 3.18

(What isotropic probes can and cannot see)
.

The tangentially radial profile averages out the
linear
anisotropy, so no componentwise information about
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
survives at first order.
At
O(\varepsilon^{2})
only the scalar invariant
|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}
enters (with universal coefficient
\kappa_{3}(n)<0
for
n\geq 5
), which is precisely what (
3.24
) recovers.
Full tensor recovery of
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}
would require anisotropic (quadrupolar) harmonic probes, which we do not employ here.

3.5.2.
Willmore-stabilized closure at the hemisphere threshold

We record a quantitative barrier in the zero-mean-curvature stratum.
Recall
C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
denotes the hemisphere threshold.

Corollary 3.19

(Positive-mass exclusion of one-bubble blow-up in the
H\equiv 0
stratum)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
,
(BG
3+
)
, and
C^{\ast}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
.
Suppose there is a boundary collar
U\subset\Sigma
with
H_{g}\equiv 0
on
U
and

\inf_{x\in U}\ \mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\ \geq\ r_{0}\ >\ 0.

Then there exists
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(r_{0},M,g)>0
and
c=c(r_{0})>0
such that:

(a) Quadratic barrier for one-bubble probes.

For all
x\in U
and all
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
,

\mathcal{J}_{g}\big(\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}\big)\ \geq\ S_{\ast}\Big(1+c\,\varepsilon^{2}\Big).

(b) Exclusion of reduced one-bubble critical points.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}:=t(x,\varepsilon)\,U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{S}
denote the slice–normalized
one–bubble ansatz (the
k=1
case of Lemma
4.4
), and let

\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon):=\mathcal{J}_{g}\!\big(\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}+w_{x,\varepsilon}\big)

denote the
k=1
reduced functional (Definition
4.6
).
Then
\mathcal{J}_{1}
has no critical point
with
x\in U
and
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
.

In particular, by Lemma
3.20
below, no sequence of

critical points
of the Escobar quotient on the constraint slice (i.e. solutions of the
Escobar Euler–Lagrange system) with energy level tending to
S_{\ast}
can exhibit
one-bubble

blow-up with centers in
U
.

Geometric sufficient condition.

Using the decomposition

\mathfrak{R}_{g}=\kappa_{3}(n)|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}+\kappa_{1}(n)\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)+\kappa_{2}(n)\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M},\qquad\kappa_{3}(n)<0,

a sufficient condition for
\inf_{U}\mathfrak{R}_{g}\geq r_{0}>0
is

\kappa_{1}(n)\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)+\kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}\ \geq\ r_{0}\ +\ |\kappa_{3}(n)|\,|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2}\quad\text{on }U.

In particular, if
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}\equiv 0
on
U
and the left-hand side is
\geq r_{0}>0
, then
\mathfrak{R}_{g}\geq r_{0}
on
U
and the above exclusion applies.

Proof.

(a) Barrier.
Since
H_{g}\equiv 0
on
U
, the one-bubble expansion (Theorem
3.6
) gives

\mathcal{J}_{g}\big(\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}\big)=S_{\ast}\Big(1+\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon^{2}\Big)+o(\varepsilon^{2}),

uniformly for
x\in U
. Write the remainder as
r(x,\varepsilon)
so that

r(x,\varepsilon)=o(\varepsilon^{2})
uniformly for
x\in U
. If
\inf_{U}\mathfrak{R}_{g}\geq r_{0}>0
,
then there exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that for all
x\in U
and
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1}
,

r(x,\varepsilon)\ \geq\ -\tfrac{1}{2}r_{0}\,\varepsilon^{2}.

Hence

\mathcal{J}_{g}\big(\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}\big)\ \geq\ S_{\ast}\Big(1+\tfrac{1}{2}r_{0}\,\varepsilon^{2}\Big)\qquad(x\in U),

which is the claimed barrier with
c=\tfrac{1}{2}r_{0}
.

(b) No reduced critical points.

Set

F_{1}(x,\varepsilon):=\left(\frac{\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)}{S_{\ast}}\right)^{n-1}.

Since
t\mapsto t^{n-1}
is strictly increasing on
(0,\infty)
,
(x,\varepsilon)
is a critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{1}

iff it is a critical point of
F_{1}
.

For
k=1
, the reduced expansion (the
k=1
case of Theorem
3.21
, noting that the interaction sum is absent)
gives, uniformly for
x\in U
,

F_{1}(x,\varepsilon)=1+(n-1)\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2}).

Differentiating in
\varepsilon
yields

\partial_{\varepsilon}F_{1}(x,\varepsilon)=2(n-1)\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon).

If
\inf_{U}\mathfrak{R}_{g}\geq r_{0}>0
, then shrinking
\varepsilon_{1}
if necessary we obtain

\partial_{\varepsilon}F_{1}(x,\varepsilon)\ \geq\ (n-1)r_{0}\,\varepsilon\ >\ 0\qquad\text{for all }x\in U,\ 0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{1},

so
\partial_{\varepsilon}F_{1}\neq 0
and hence
(x,\varepsilon)
cannot be a critical point of
F_{1}
(or of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
)
in this region. The "geometric sufficient condition" follows immediately from the decomposition of
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
.
∎

Lemma 3.20

(One-bubble criticality implies reduced criticality)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
and set
q=2^{*}_{\partial}
.
Let
u\in\mathcal{S}:=\{v\in H^{1}(M):\|v\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\}
be a constrained critical point of

\mathcal{J}_{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
(equivalently, an Escobar Euler–Lagrange solution normalized by

\|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
).

Assume further that
u
lies in the one–bubble regime so that the Lyapunov–Schmidt map for
k=1
applies
(Lemma
4.4
with
k=1
): namely, there exist parameters
(x,\varepsilon)
(with
\varepsilon
small) such that

u\ =\ \mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}\ +\ w_{x,\varepsilon},

where
\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}=t(x,\varepsilon)\,U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{S}
is the slice–normalized one–bubble
ansatz and
w_{x,\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(M)
is the corresponding Lyapunov–Schmidt correction.

Then
(x,\varepsilon)
is a critical point of the reduced one–bubble functional
\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)\coloneqq\mathcal{J}_{g}\big(\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}+w_{x,\varepsilon}\big)
, i.e.

\nabla_{x,\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)=0.

Proof.

Set
\Phi(x,\varepsilon):=\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}+w_{x,\varepsilon}
, so that

\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)=\mathcal{J}_{g}(\Phi(x,\varepsilon))
and
u=\Phi(x,\varepsilon)
.

Since
u\in\mathcal{S}
is a constrained critical point of the
quotient

\mathcal{J}_{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
,
and
\mathcal{J}_{g}
is homogeneous of degree
0
under scalings
u\mapsto tu
, it follows that
u
is in fact an
(unconstrained) critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{g}
on
H^{1}(M)\setminus\{0\}
, i.e.

D\mathcal{J}_{g}[u][\psi]=0\qquad\forall\,\psi\in H^{1}(M).

(Equivalently: on
\mathcal{S}
one has
\mathcal{J}_{g}=\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}
, and the Euler–Lagrange/Lagrange multiplier
formulation gives
D\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}[u]=\lambda\,D(\|\cdot\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}^{2})[u]
with
\lambda=\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}(u)=\mathcal{J}_{g}(u)
by testing against
u
; hence
D\mathcal{J}_{g}[u]=0
.)

By Lemma
4.4
, the Lyapunov–Schmidt map
(x,\varepsilon)\mapsto w_{x,\varepsilon}
is
C^{1}
, hence

\Phi
is
C^{1}
in
(x,\varepsilon)
. Therefore, for each parameter direction

p\in\{\varepsilon,x^{1},\dots,x^{n-1}\}
the chain rule yields

\partial_{p}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)=D\mathcal{J}_{g}[u]\big[\partial_{p}\Phi(x,\varepsilon)\big]=0.

Thus
\nabla_{x,\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)=0
.
∎

3.6.
Center-only reduction with Willmore drift (multi-bubble regime)

At the Escobar threshold, the reduced multi-bubble
quotient
from §
4

acquires a
Willmore drift
at each center.

Theorem 3.21

(Reduced potential with Willmore drift)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
,
(BG
3+
)
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
, and that we are in the
refined multi-bubble regime

of Theorem
4.8
(ii)
near the bubble centers (in particular:
H_{g}
is locally constant in fixed
boundary collars around the centers and
H_{g}(x_{i})=0
for each center so that the
\varepsilon_{i}^{2}
coefficient is
cutoff–independent and the refined Lyapunov–Schmidt estimate applies), together with the usual separation hypothesis
(
4.7
).
Then the reduced
k
-bubble Escobar functional satisfies

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})}{S_{\ast}}\right)^{n-1}

\displaystyle=k+(n-1)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big(\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x_{i})\,\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})\big)

\displaystyle\quad+(n-1)\sum_{i\neq j}c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})\;+\;o\Big(\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\Big),

where
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
satisfies the curvature decomposition (
3.23
). In particular, when

H_{g}\equiv 0
the center selection is driven at leading order by minimizing

\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})
(which contains the universal traceless-curvature term

\kappa_{3}(n)\sum_{i}|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}(x_{i})|^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}
with
\kappa_{3}(n)<0
for
n\geq 5
) together with the boundary interaction kernel; in the one-bubble case
k=1
on the
H_{g}\equiv 0
stratum, small-scale criticality in
\varepsilon

requires
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)=0
(cf. Corollary
3.19
).

Equivalence of criticality.
Since
t\mapsto t^{n-1}
has strictly positive derivative on
(0,\infty)
,
critical points of
\mathcal{J}_{k}
coincide with critical points of
\big(\mathcal{J}_{k}/S_{\ast}\big)^{n-1}
.

Proof sketch.

Insert (
3.23
) into the
O(\varepsilon_{i}^{2})
coefficient of the single-bubble terms and apply the standard Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction from §
4
for the interactions; modulation orthogonality decouples the angular second variation at leading order. The assumptions ensure the coefficients are the cutoff-independent limits defined earlier.
∎

3.7.
Positive-mass consequences, rigidity,
H=0
stratum

Theorem 3.22

(Boundary renormalized mass: sign control under pinching; rigidity at the threshold)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
. Let
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
be the renormalized mass of Definition
3.5
.

(a) Pointwise sign control under geometric pinching.

If, in a boundary collar,
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}\equiv 0
and the curvature pinching

(3.25)

\kappa_{1}(n)\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)\ +\ \kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}\ \geq\ 0

holds pointwise, then
\mathfrak{R}_{g}\geq 0
there. If, in addition,
\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)\geq 0
,
\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}\geq 0
, and
\kappa_{1}(n)\geq 0
, then

\mathfrak{R}_{g}=0
implies
\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}=0
in the collar; if moreover
\kappa_{1}(n)>0
, then
\mathfrak{R}_{g}=0
iff

\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)=\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}=0
in the collar.

(b) Rigidity at the hemisphere value with attainment under umbilicity and vanishing mass.

Suppose
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
and the Escobar
quotient

\mathcal{J}_{g}
admits a smooth extremal
u>0
. If
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}\equiv 0
on
\partial M
and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}\equiv 0
on
\partial M
, and either

(3.26)

\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)\geq 0,\quad\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}\geq 0,\quad\kappa_{1}(n)\geq 0,

or directly
\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)\equiv 0\equiv\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}
, then
(M,g)
is conformally a round spherical cap.

Proof.

(a) By Definition
3.5
and Proposition
3.7
,

\mathfrak{R}_{g}=\kappa_{1}(n)\,\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)+\kappa_{2}(n)\,\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}+\kappa_{3}(n)\,|\mathring{\mathrm{II}}|^{2},

with
\kappa_{2}(n)>0
and
\kappa_{3}(n)<0
. If
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}\equiv 0
and (
3.25
) holds, the right-hand side is nonnegative; the "
iff
" statement follows under the additional nonnegativity assumptions.

(b) Let
u>0
be an extremal for
\mathcal{J}_{g}
at level
S_{\ast}
and set
\widehat{g}=u^{\frac{4}{n-2}}g
. Conformal covariance yields
\mathrm{Scal}_{\widehat{g}}\equiv 0
and constant boundary mean curvature for
\widehat{g}
. If (
3.26
) holds, then
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}\equiv 0
and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}\equiv 0
together with part (a) imply
\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}=0
; if moreover
\kappa_{1}(n)>0
, then also
\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)=0
. In the alternative,
\operatorname{Ric}_{g}(\nu,\nu)\equiv 0\equiv\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}
is assumed. Umbilicity is conformally invariant, so
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}_{\widehat{g}}\equiv 0
on
\partial M
. Thus
(M,\widehat{g})
is scalar-flat with totally umbilic boundary, constant boundary mean curvature, and achieves the hemisphere constant. Escobar’s rigidity (
[
27
]
) then gives that
(M,\widehat{g})
is isometric to a round spherical cap; hence
(M,g)
is conformally a round spherical cap.
∎

Theorem 3.23

(Top stratum
H_{g}\equiv 0
: second-order law and threshold obstruction/selection)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
, and
(BG
3+
)
, and suppose
H_{g}\equiv 0
in a collar of
\partial M
.
Then, uniformly in
x\in\partial M
,

(3.27)

\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]=S_{\ast}\Big(1+\varepsilon^{2}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\Big)\ +\ o(\varepsilon^{2})\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow 0),

where
v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
is the cutoff–independent boundary bubble and

\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is the renormalized mass (Definition
3.5
).

Consequently:

(a)

If there exists
x_{0}\in\partial M
with
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{0})<0
, then
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)<S_{\ast}
.

(b)

Assume
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
and let
(u_{\ell})\subset\mathcal{S}
be a sequence of
constrained critical
points
(i.e. normalized Euler–Lagrange solutions) with
\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to S_{\ast}
.
If
(u_{\ell})
blows up with a single boundary bubble in the collar (so that, after Lyapunov–Schmidt reparametrization,

u_{\ell}=\mathcal{U}_{x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell}}+w_{x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell}}
with
\varepsilon_{\ell}\downarrow 0
),
then the blow-up center
p=\lim_{\ell}x_{\ell}
satisfies the
threshold selection laws

(3.28)

\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nabla_{\partial}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0,

where
\nabla_{\partial}
denotes the boundary gradient.
In particular, if
\inf_{x\in U}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)>0
on a collar neighborhood
U
, then no such one-bubble blow-up can occur
with centers in
U
(cf. Corollary
3.19
).

(c)

In the borderline case where
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
vanishes at some points, the second-order theory alone does not decide
whether bubbling at the threshold occurs: the existence/obstruction mechanism at points with

\mathfrak{R}_{g}=0
is governed by the next nonvanishing coefficient (e.g. the third-order coefficient
\Theta_{g}
when
n\geq 6
),
see §
3.3
and Theorem
3.29
.

Proof.

The expansion (
3.27
) is Theorem
3.6
with
H_{g}\equiv 0
.

(a) Fix
x_{0}
with
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{0})<0
. By (
3.27
), for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small
we have
\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x_{0},\varepsilon}]<S_{\ast}
, hence
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)\leq\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x_{0},\varepsilon}]<S_{\ast}
.

(b) Let
(u_{\ell})
be constrained critical points with
\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to S_{\ast}
and assume one-bubble blow-up in the
collar. By Lemma
3.20
, the Lyapunov–Schmidt parameters
(x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell})
are
critical for the reduced one-bubble functional
\mathcal{J}_{1}
:

\partial_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell})=0,\qquad\nabla_{x}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell})=0.

In the
H_{g}\equiv 0
collar, the refined one-bubble reduced expansion (the
k=1
case of Theorem
3.21
)
holds in a
C^{1}
sense:

\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)=S_{\ast}\Big(1+\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon^{2}\Big)+o_{C^{1}}(\varepsilon^{2}),

uniformly on the collar, where
o_{C^{1}}(\varepsilon^{2})
denotes a remainder whose value and first derivatives in

(x,\varepsilon)
are
o(\varepsilon^{2})
uniformly. In particular,

\partial_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)=2S_{\ast}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon+o(\varepsilon),\qquad\nabla_{x}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)=S_{\ast}\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\nabla_{\partial}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)+o(\varepsilon^{2}).

Evaluating at
(x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell})
and using stationarity gives
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell})\to 0
and

\nabla_{\partial}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell})\to 0
, hence (
3.28
) at
p=\lim_{\ell}x_{\ell}
.

(c) This is immediate from the fact that when
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0
the first nonvanishing term in the scale derivative
of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
is higher order (e.g. the
\Theta_{g}
–term in
n\geq 6
), so the second-order law cannot decide existence.
∎

3.8.
Convex Euclidean domains & stable collars

Theorem 3.24

(Convex Euclidean domains: compactness of solutions)
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded
C^{3}
domain,
n\geq 4
, whose boundary has strictly positive mean curvature
H_{g}
with respect to the
inner
unit normal:

H_{g}(x)\ \geq\ h_{0}>0\qquad\text{for all }x\in\partial\Omega.

Let
u_{\ell}>0
solve the critical covariant Escobar boundary problem and be normalized by
\int_{\partial\Omega}u_{\ell}^{2^{*}_{\partial}}\,d\sigma=1
, where
2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
. Then
(u_{\ell})
is precompact in
H^{1}(\Omega)
; in particular, no
boundary
bubbling occurs.

Proof.

We use the
inner
normal convention, so
H_{g}\geq h_{0}>0
on
\partial\Omega
.
Assume by contradiction that boundary blow-up occurs. In the reduced
k
-bubble scheme for the Escobar
quotient
,
the reduced criticality system forces, in particular, the scale equations

\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{J}_{k}}{S_{\ast}}\right)^{n-1}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})\ =\ 0\qquad(i=1,\dots,k).

By the leading-order scale expansion (first-order one-bubble law) together with the scale–separation hypotheses (so that interaction contributions to the scale derivative are
o(1)
as
\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\downarrow 0
),

\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathcal{J}_{k}}{S_{\ast}}\right)^{n-1}=(n-1)\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\ +\ o(1),

uniformly for the centers
x_{\ell,i}
. Since
H_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\geq h_{0}
and
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0
, the right-hand side is strictly positive for
\ell
large, contradicting the scale criticality. Therefore boundary bubbling cannot occur, and
(u_{\ell})
is precompact in
H^{1}(\Omega)
.
∎

Corollary 3.25

(Stability under small
C^{3}
perturbations of a convex collar)
.

Let
(M^{n},g)
be a compact manifold with boundary. Suppose there exists a boundary collar

\mathcal{U}\simeq\partial M\times[0,r_{0})
and a
C^{3}
diffeomorphism onto its image in a convex
Euclidean domain such that, in these Fermi coordinates,

\|g-\delta\|_{C^{3}(\mathcal{U})}\ \leq\ \eta\quad\text{and}\quad H_{\delta}\ \geq\ h_{0}>0\text{ on }\partial M,

with
\eta>0
sufficiently small. Then, for the
inner
normal of
g
, we have

H_{g}\ \geq\ h_{0}/2\ >\ 0\quad\text{on }\partial M.

Any sequence of positive solutions of the covariant Escobar problem

L_{g}^{\circ}u_{\ell}=0\ \text{ in }M,\qquad B_{g}^{\circ}u_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}\,u_{\ell}^{2^{*}_{\partial}-1}\ \text{ on }\partial M,

normalized by
\int_{\partial M}u_{\ell}^{2^{*}_{\partial}}\,d\sigma_{g}=1
, is precompact in
H^{1}(M)
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Theorem
3.24
. The first-order coefficient

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}
retains a uniform positive sign on the collar for
\eta
small, so the reduced
scale derivative for
\big(\mathcal{J}_{k}/S_{\ast}\big)^{n-1}
is strictly positive at small scales, which precludes
boundary blow-up.
∎

Remark 3.26

(No structural use of second order)
.

Under strict mean-convexity with respect to the inner normal (
H_{g}>0
), the first-order term

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}\,\varepsilon
in the one-bubble expansion already forces the reduced
scale derivative to be positive at small scales; no second-order information is needed to rule out boundary bubbling.

3.9.
Third order expansion

Assume
n\geq 6
and the higher-order Fermi control

(BG
4+
)

the boundary collar admits Fermi coordinates with
C^{4}
–bounded jets of
g
.

Lemma 3.27

(Third-order Escobar one-bubble expansion: structure)
.

Assume
n\geq 6
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
4+
)
. Let
U_{+}
be the normalized half-space optimizer (covariant setting) and let

v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}

denote the cutoff–independent boundary bubble, understood via the admissible cutoff/limit convention built from (
3.12
) (see the Cutoff/limits note below). Then, uniformly in
x
on compact subsets of the collar,

(3.29)

\mathcal{J}_{g}[v_{x,\varepsilon}]\ =\ S_{\ast}\left(1+\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon\ +\ \varepsilon^{2}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\ +\ \varepsilon^{3}\,\Theta_{g}(x)\right)\ +\ o(\varepsilon^{3}),\qquad\varepsilon\downarrow 0,

where
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
are as in §
2
–
3
, and

(3.30)

\Theta_{g}(x)\ =\ \alpha_{1}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)\ +\ \alpha_{2}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x)\ +\ \alpha_{3}(n)\,\langle\nabla_{\nu}\mathring{\II},\mathring{\II}\rangle(x)\ +\ \alpha_{4}(n)\,\Delta_{\partial}H_{g}(x),

with
\nu
the inner unit normal,
\Delta_{\partial}
the boundary Laplacian, and
\alpha_{j}(n)
universal dimensional constants. If
n\geq 7
, the remainder improves to
O(\varepsilon^{4})
.

Cutoff/limits.
The expansion is obtained by testing the truncated bubbles

u_{x,\varepsilon,R}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R}U_{+}),

sending
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
at fixed
R
, and then
R\to\infty
(see §
3.1
).
Equivalently, one may proceed along a diagonal
R=R(\varepsilon)\to\infty
with
\varepsilon R(\varepsilon)\to 0
.
The cutoff–independent bubble
v_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}U_{+}
is understood via this limit convention.

Proposition 3.28

(Model simplification: locally conformally flat, umbilic, and constant
H
)
.

Assume the setting of Lemma
3.27
(in particular
n\geq 6
and
(BG
4+
)
), and assume that, in a boundary collar,
g
is locally conformally flat,
\mathring{\mathrm{II}}\equiv 0
, and
H_{g}
is (locally) constant on
\partial M
. Then the third-order coefficient simplifies to

(3.31)

\Theta_{g}(x)\ =\ \alpha_{1}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu)(x)\ +\ \alpha_{2}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(x),

i.e. the
\langle\nabla_{\nu}\mathring{\mathrm{II}},\mathring{\mathrm{II}}\rangle
and
\Delta_{\partial}H_{g}
channels vanish.

Theorem 3.29

(Closure at the Escobar threshold: compactness, rigidity, bubbling)
.

Assume
n\geq 6
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
4+
)
, and let
C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(M,g)
denote the sharp Escobar constant.

(i) Subcritical compactness via a negative coefficient.

If there exists
x\in\partial M
such that the first non-vanishing coefficient in the sequence

\{\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x),\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x),\,\Theta_{g}(x)\}
is strictly negative, then

C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(M,g)<S_{\ast}
and every minimizing/Palais–Smale (PS) sequence at level
C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(M,g)

is precompact in
H^{1}(M)
(cf. Theorem
2.46
).

(ii) Threshold degeneracy and third-order sign.

Assume
C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
and let
p\in\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})
. If

H_{g}(p)=0,\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0,\qquad\Theta_{g}(p)>0,

then the existence of a PS sequence bubbling at
p
is not ruled out by this local expansion.
Conversely, if
\Theta_{g}(p)<0
under
H_{g}(p)=\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0
, then necessarily

C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(M,g)<S_{\ast}
by
(i)
.

(iii) Local rigidity at vanishing invariants (attainment).

If
C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
,
H_{g}\equiv\mathfrak{R}_{g}\equiv\Theta_{g}\equiv 0
in a boundary collar of

p\in\partial M
, and there exists a smooth extremal
u
at level
S_{\ast}
with boundary barycenter in that collar, then,
assuming in addition either
\mathring{\II}\equiv 0
on the collar or the curvature pinching from (
3.26
) together with
\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu)\equiv\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}\equiv 0
there, the conformal metric
\widehat{g}=u^{\frac{4}{n-2}}g
is locally isometric to
(S^{n}_{+},g_{\mathrm{round}})
near
p
.

(iv) Selection at the threshold.

Under
C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
, any
Palais–Smale (in particular, any critical-point)
blow-up sequence at level
S_{\ast}
concentrates at points

x\in\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})
where the first nonvanishing coefficient among
\{\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g},\ \mathfrak{R}_{g},\ \Theta_{g}\}
is nonnegative.
If, in addition,
all three invariants vanish on a collar and an extremal exists, then the rigid case
(iii)
occurs.

Lemma 3.30

(Extremal blow-up profiles at level
S_{\ast}
are one-signed)
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
. Let
(u_{k})\subset\mathcal{S}:=\{u\in H^{1}(M):\|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\}

be a Palais–Smale sequence for
\mathcal{J}_{g}
at the hemisphere level:

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{k}]\to S_{\ast},\qquad\|\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(u_{k})\|_{H^{1}(M)}\to 0,

and assume
(u_{k})
blows up. Then any extracted boundary blow-up profile in Fermi rescalings is, up to sign and the
boundary-preserving symmetries of
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
(tangential translations and dilations), the standard half-space optimizer
U_{+}
.
In particular, after possibly replacing
u_{k}
by
-u_{k}
for large
k
, the blow-up lies in the positive boundary-bubble channel.

Proof.

By the Palais–Smale condition on
\mathcal{S}
, there exist multipliers
\lambda_{k}
such that

L_{g}^{\circ}u_{k}\to 0\ \text{in }H^{-1}(M),\qquad B_{g}^{\circ}u_{k}-\lambda_{k}\,|u_{k}|^{q-2}u_{k}\to 0\ \text{in }H^{-1/2}(\partial M).

Testing the boundary residual against
u_{k}
and using
\|u_{k}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
yields

\lambda_{k}=\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{k}]+o(1)\to S_{\ast}
.

Assuming blow-up, concentration–compactness on
\partial M
produces at least one atom. After choosing a blow-up center
and rescaling in Fermi coordinates, one obtains a nontrivial half-space limit

U\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
satisfying the flat Euler–Lagrange system with parameter
\lambda_{\infty}=S_{\ast}
:

-\Delta U=0\ \text{in }\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\qquad-\partial_{y_{n}}U=S_{\ast}\,|U|^{q-2}U\ \text{on }\partial\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}.

Let
m:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|U(\cdot,0)|^{q}\,dy^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)
. Testing the boundary condition against
U
gives the energy identity

(3.32)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}|\nabla U|^{2}\,dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}U(\cdot,0)\,(-\partial_{y_{n}}U)(\cdot,0)\,dy^{\prime}=S_{\ast}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|U(\cdot,0)|^{q}\,dy^{\prime}=S_{\ast}\,m.

On the other hand, the sharp half-space trace inequality (whose best constant equals
S_{\ast}
by conformal invariance)
states

(3.33)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}|\nabla W|^{2}\,dy\ \geq\ S_{\ast}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}|W(\cdot,0)|^{q}\,dy^{\prime}\Big)^{2/q}\qquad\forall\,W\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}).

Applying (
3.33
) to
W=U
and combining with (
3.32
) yields

S_{\ast}\,m\ \geq\ S_{\ast}\,m^{2/q}.

Since
1-\frac{2}{q}=\frac{1}{n-1}>0
, this implies
m\geq 1
. On the other hand,
m
is exactly the boundary
L^{q}
–mass of the
atom extracted from the slice-normalized sequence
\|u_{k}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
, hence necessarily
m\leq 1
.
Therefore
m=1
, and equality holds in (
3.33
) for
U
.

By the known characterization of equality cases in the sharp trace inequality on
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}

(see, e.g., Escobar’s classification of optimizers for the sharp trace inequality),

U
must coincide, up to tangential translation and dilation and an overall sign, with the standard optimizer
U_{+}
.
In particular,
U
has a fixed sign; replacing
U
by
-U
if necessary gives the positive profile.
Transporting back to
(M,g)
shows that blow-up at level
S_{\ast}
is necessarily in the (signed)
U_{+}
boundary-bubble channel,
and after a global sign change one may work in the positive channel.
∎

Corollary 3.31

(Model sign criterion in the LCF–umbilic–constant-
H
class)
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
3.28
, one has

\Theta_{g}(p)=\alpha_{1}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu)(p)\ +\ \alpha_{2}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(p).

In particular, the sign of
\Theta_{g}(p)
is the sign of this linear combination.
Hence, at a truly degenerate
p
with
H_{g}(p)=\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p)=0
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu)(p)+\alpha_{2}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(p)<0\

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\ C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(M,g)<S_{\ast}\ \text{ and PS compactness (by {(i)}),}

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu)(p)+\alpha_{2}(n)\,\nabla_{\nu}\mathrm{Scal}_{g|\partial M}(p)>0\

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\ \text{bubbling may occur at the threshold.}

If, in addition,
H_{g}\equiv 0
and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}\equiv 0
on that collar (so that
\Theta_{g}\equiv 0
there when
\nabla_{\nu}\Ric_{g}(\nu,\nu)\equiv 0
), and an extremal exists at
S_{\ast}
, then the rigid case of Theorem
3.29
(iii)
applies and
(M,g)
is locally conformally hemispherical there.

4.
Escobar III: Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction, interactions, and threshold classification

Functional conventions.

We work primarily with the Escobar quotient

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u]:=\frac{\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}(u)}{\|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}^{2}},\qquad q=2_{\partial}^{\ast}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2},

where
\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}(u)=\langle u,u\rangle_{E,g}
is the graph numerator. When restricting to the
constraint slice

\mathcal{S}:=\{u\in H^{1}(M):\ \|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\},

one has
\mathcal{J}_{g}=\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}
on
\mathcal{S}
. In the multi-bubble regime it is also convenient
to consider the monotone reparametrization

F_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}):=\Big(\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})/S_{\ast}\Big)^{n-1},

for which the leading-order baseline becomes additive:
F_{k}=k+(\text{lower order terms})
.

Derivative/gradient conventions.

Throughout Section
4
we regard the Escobar quotient
\mathcal{J}_{g}
as a functional on the
constraint slice

\mathcal{S}:=\big\{u\in H^{1}(M):\ \|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\big\},\qquad q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2},

so that
\mathcal{J}_{g}=\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}
on
\mathcal{S}
.
We keep
\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}(u):=\langle u,u\rangle_{E,g}
(no factor
\tfrac{1}{2}
).

The tangent space at
u\in\mathcal{S}
is

T_{u}\mathcal{S}=\Big\{\phi\in H^{1}(M):\ \int_{\partial M}|u|^{q-2}u\,\phi\,d\sigma_{g}=0\Big\}.

(In the sign-preserving regime
u|_{\partial M}>0
this reduces to
\int_{\partial M}u^{q-1}\phi=0
.)
Thus the constrained first variation (i.e. the differential of
\mathcal{J}_{g}

restricted to

\mathcal{S}
) is

(4.1)

D(\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}})[u][\phi]=2\langle u,\phi\rangle_{E,g}\qquad(\phi\in T_{u}\mathcal{S}).

When it is convenient to view the constrained differential as a functional on the ambient space
H^{1}(M)
,
we use the standard Lagrange-multiplier representation: there exists a unique
\lambda(u)\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(4.2)

\phi\ \longmapsto\ 2\langle u,\phi\rangle_{E,g}-\lambda(u)\int_{\partial M}|u|^{q-2}u\,\phi\,d\sigma_{g}

agrees with
D(\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}})[u]
on
T_{u}\mathcal{S}
(equivalently, it vanishes on the normal direction to

\mathcal{S}
at
u
). We will freely pass between (
4.1
) and (
4.2
).

We identify
(H^{1}(M))^{\ast}
with
H^{1}(M)
via the Riesz map

\mathcal{R}_{E,g}:H^{1}(M)\to(H^{1}(M))^{\ast},\qquad\mathcal{R}_{E,g}(v)(\phi):=\langle v,\phi\rangle_{E,g},

and define the constrained gradient by

\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(u)\ :=\ \mathcal{R}_{E,g}^{-1}\Big(D(\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}})[u]\Big)\in H^{1}(M),

where we take
\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(u)
to be the unique element of
T_{u}\mathcal{S}
representing the
differential in the
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E,g}
pairing. Equivalently,

\langle\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(u),\phi\rangle_{E,g}=D(\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}})[u][\phi]
for all

\phi\in T_{u}\mathcal{S}
.
