Whenever we write
\Pi\,D\mathcal{J}_{g}(\cdot)
we mean equivalently
\Pi\,\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(\cdot)

under this identification.

Regime summary.

We distinguish the first-order regime, the refined regime (local constancy of
H_{g}
), the separated/no-tower regime, and, in dimension
n=5
, the scale-comparable regime required for scale elimination.

Remark 4.1

(Cutoff convention in the multi–bubble regime)
.

The Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction and interaction estimates in Section
4
require

uniform spatial localization
of each bubble at scale
\varepsilon_{i}
so that separation implies small overlap.
Accordingly, throughout Section
4
we fix once and for all a large cutoff radius

R_{0}\gg 1\quad\text{and a fixed admissible cutoff }\chi_{R_{0}}\ \text{ with }\chi_{R_{0}}\equiv 1\text{ on }B_{R_{0}},\ \supp\chi_{R_{0}}\subset B_{2R_{0}},

and we set, for
x\in\partial M
and
\varepsilon>0
small,

(4.3)

U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\ :=\ \mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}(\chi_{R_{0}}U_{+}),

where
\mathcal{T}_{x,\varepsilon}
is the covariant transfer map in Fermi coordinates and
U_{+}
is the fixed
tangentially radial half-space optimizer.

All separation/overlap arguments are carried out with this
fixed
cutoff. In particular,
(
4.7
) will always be imposed with
\Lambda\geq 10R_{0}
, so that the supports of distinct bubbles
lie in essentially disjoint boundary collars and the overlap estimates are quantitative.

Relationship with the cutoff–independent coefficients.

The cutoff–independent quantities from Section
3
(in particular
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}
and

\mathfrak{R}_{g}
) are defined via the two–limit convention (first
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
at fixed
R
, then
R\to\infty
,
or along a diagonal
R(\varepsilon)\to\infty
with
\varepsilon R(\varepsilon)\to 0
).
Whenever such quantities appear in the reduced expansions below, they are understood at the level of the

universal constants
: one may regard
\rho_{n,R_{0}}^{\mathrm{conf}}
and the
R_{0}
–truncated second-order
coefficients as approximations of their cutoff–independent limits as
R_{0}\to\infty
.
The Lyapunov–Schmidt construction itself is carried out for a fixed large
R_{0}
, with separation imposed relative to
this
R_{0}
.

Remark 4.2

(Sign conventions and scope of the reduction package)
.

The slice
\mathcal{S}=\{\|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\}
and its tangent space are defined without any sign restriction, via the
constraint differential
\phi\mapsto\int_{\partial M}|u|^{q-2}u\,\phi
.
Nevertheless, the Lyapunov–Schmidt/modulation and dynamical tools in §
4
are developed in a

sign-preserving
neighborhood and use the positive model bubble manifold generated by the half-space optimizer
U_{+}
.
By contrast, compactness
below
the hemisphere value is sign-free: a Palais–Smale sequence at any level

\Lambda<S_{\ast}
is
H^{1}
-bounded by coercivity and cannot bubble because any extracted half-space
critical
profile with
multiplier
\Lambda
would contradict the sharp trace constant (Theorem
2.46
).
At the minimizing threshold
S_{\ast}
in the
single-bubble
channel, any blow-up profile necessarily saturates the sharp
trace inequality and is therefore
\pm U_{+}
up to symmetries, so after a global sign flip one may work in the positive sector
(Lemma
3.30
).
A fully sign-free multi-bubble theory would require introducing signed sectors
\boldsymbol{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\}^{k}
in the ansatz

\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}U_{+,x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}}
; we do not pursue that extension here.

4.1.
Lyapunov–Schmidt correction and reduced functional

Lemma 4.3

(Refined one–bubble residual is kernel–pure at order
\varepsilon
)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
. Fix a cutoff radius
R_{0}
and define

U_{+,x,\varepsilon}
as in (
4.3
). Let

\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}:=t(x,\varepsilon)\,U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{S},\qquad t(x,\varepsilon):=\|U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}^{-1},

and set

T_{\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}}\mathcal{S}=\Big\{\psi\in H^{1}(M):\ \int_{\partial M}\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}^{\,q-1}\psi\,d\sigma_{g}=0\Big\}.

Define the slice–tangent modulation directions

Z_{0}:=\varepsilon\,\partial_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon},\qquad Z_{\alpha}:=\varepsilon\,\partial_{(x)^{\alpha}}\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}\quad(\alpha=1,\dots,n-1),

and the corresponding kernel space

\mathcal{K}_{x,\varepsilon}:=\mathrm{span}\{Z_{0},Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n-1}\}\ \subset\ T_{\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}}\mathcal{S}.

Let
\Pi_{x,\varepsilon}
denote the
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E,g}
–orthogonal projection onto

\mathcal{K}_{x,\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cap T_{\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}}\mathcal{S}.

Then, uniformly for
x
on compact boundary collars and
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, there exist coefficients

a_{\beta}(x,\varepsilon)\in\mathbb{R}
(
\beta=0,\dots,n-1
) such that

(4.4)

\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon})=\sum_{\beta=0}^{n-1}a_{\beta}(x,\varepsilon)\,Z_{\beta}(x,\varepsilon)\ +\ \mathcal{R}_{x,\varepsilon},

where the remainder satisfies

(4.5)

\|\mathcal{R}_{x,\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}(M)}\ \leq\ C\,\varepsilon^{2}.

In particular, if
H_{g}(x)=0
, then the
projected residual
onto the slice–tangent kernel complement satisfies

(4.6)

\big\|\Pi_{x,\varepsilon}\,\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon})\big\|_{H^{1}(M)}\ \leq\ C\,\varepsilon^{2}.

Proof sketch.

On the constraint slice
\mathcal{S}
one has
\mathcal{J}_{g}=\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}
, hence

D(\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}})[\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}][\psi]=2\langle\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon},\psi\rangle_{E,g}\qquad(\psi\in T_{\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}}\mathcal{S}).

The first-order one-bubble expansion in Section
3
gives, uniformly on collars,

\mathcal{J}_{g}[\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}]=S_{\ast}\Big(1+\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon\Big)+O(\varepsilon^{2}),

with no linear correction in the trace normalization.
Differentiating in the modulation directions and using that
Z_{\beta}\in T_{\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}}\mathcal{S}
yields that
all order-
\varepsilon
contributions to the constrained differential are realized along
\mathrm{span}\{Z_{\beta}\}
, and the
remainder is
O(\varepsilon^{2})
in the
H^{1}
–dual norm. Identifying
(H^{1})^{\ast}
with
H^{1}
via the Riesz map associated
with
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E,g}
gives (
4.4
)–(
4.5
). When

H_{g}(x)=0
, the order-
\varepsilon
coefficient vanishes, and projecting onto

\mathcal{K}_{x,\varepsilon}^{\perp}\cap T_{\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}}\mathcal{S}
yields
(
4.6
).
∎

Lemma 4.4

(Intrinsic Lyapunov–Schmidt with quantitative residual)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
and set

q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
.
Fix once and for all the (multi–bubble) cutoff convention of
Remark
4.1
; in particular, each

U_{+,x,\varepsilon}
is a
fixed–cutoff
Fermi pushforward of the half–space optimizer.

There exist
\Lambda\gg 1
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
(depending on
(M,g,n)
,
k
, and the fixed cutoff radius) such that
for any admissible
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
with

(4.7)

\frac{d_{g}(x_{i},x_{j})}{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}}\ \geq\ \Lambda\qquad(i\neq j),

and
\varepsilon_{i}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, define the
slice-normalized multi-bubble ansatz

\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:=t(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\sum_{i=1}^{k}U_{+,x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}},\qquad t(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}):=\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}U_{+,x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}}\Big\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}^{-1},

so that
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in\mathcal{S}:=\{u\in H^{1}(M):\|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\}
.

Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E,g}
denote the Escobar graph inner product, and define the constrained gradient on the slice by

\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(u)\in T_{u}\mathcal{S}\quad\text{as the unique element satisfying}\quad\langle\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(u),\phi\rangle_{E,g}=D(\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}})[u][\phi]\ \ \forall\,\phi\in T_{u}\mathcal{S}.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\subset T_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\mathcal{S}

be the (slice–tangent) modulation kernel generated by the parameter derivatives of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

(with respect to
(x_{i},\varepsilon_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,k
), and let
\Pi_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
be the

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E,g}
–orthogonal projection onto

X:=\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}^{\perp}\cap T_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\mathcal{S}.

Then there is a unique

w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in X

solving the projected constrained–gradient equation

(4.8)

\Pi_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\,\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}\big(\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big)=0,

(where
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in\mathcal{S}
since both summands
lie in
\mathcal{S}
and
w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in T_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\mathcal{S}

is constructed via a slice chart).

Moreover one has the
general
bound

(4.9)

\|w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|_{H^{1}(M)}\ \leq\ C(M,g,k)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon_{i}\ +\ \sum_{i\neq j}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\Big(\Lambda^{-1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}}{d_{g}(x_{i},x_{j})}\Big)\right).

Refined regime.

Under the additional hypothesis

(4.10)

for each
i
, one has
H_{g}(x_{i})=0
and
H_{g}
is locally constant in a fixed boundary collar of
x_{i}
(uniformly in
i
),

one has the
refined
bound

(4.11)

\|w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|_{H^{1}(M)}\ \leq\ C(M,g,k)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\ +\ \sum_{i\neq j}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\Big(\Lambda^{-1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}}{d_{g}(x_{i},x_{j})}\Big)\right).

Uniform spectral gap on the complement.

For fixed
k
, the constrained Hessian quadratic form on the slice at
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}

has a uniform spectral gap on
X
:

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}[v]\ \geq\ c_{0}\,\|v\|_{H^{1}(M)}^{2}\quad\forall\,v\in X,

where
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{U}}
is the usual constrained Jacobi form
(defined explicitly in (
4.13
) below) and
c_{0}>0
depends only on
(M,g,n)
and
k

but is independent of
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
.

Proof.

Write
U_{i}:=U_{+,x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}}
and define the slice-normalized ansatz

\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:=t\sum_{i=1}^{k}U_{i},\qquad t=t(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}):=\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}U_{i}\Big\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}^{-1},

so that
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{S}:=\{u\in H^{1}(M):\|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\}
, where

q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
.
Let
U_{+}
be the fixed tangentially radial (harmonic) half-space optimizer.

By the fixed–cutoff Fermi construction (Remark
4.1
),

\|U_{i}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=\|U_{+}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}+O(\varepsilon_{i})\quad\text{uniformly in }x_{i},

hence for
\varepsilon_{0}
small there are constants
0<c_{\ast}\leq C_{\ast}<\infty
such that

c_{\ast}\leq\|U_{i}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}\leq C_{\ast}
for all
i
.
Since
U_{i}\geq 0
, it follows that

(4.12)

0<c(k)\ \leq\ t(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\ \leq\ C(k)<\infty

uniformly for admissible configurations with
\max_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\leq\varepsilon_{0}
.

Equip
H^{1}(M)
with the Escobar graph inner product

\langle u,v\rangle_{E,g}:=\int_{M}\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\rangle\,dV_{g}+\frac{n-2}{4(n-1)}\int_{M}\mathrm{Scal}_{g}\,uv\,dV_{g}+\frac{n-2}{2}\int_{\partial M}H_{g}\,uv\,d\sigma_{g},

whose norm is equivalent to
\|\,\cdot\,\|_{H^{1}(M)}
by Lemma
2.20
(c) under
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
) and (BG
2+
).

Step 1 (Kernel, projection, tangency, and Jacobi form).

For the
i
-th bubble, consider derivatives with respect to
\varepsilon_{i}
and the tangential position of

x_{i}\in\partial M
. For a local orthonormal frame
\{E_{\alpha}(x_{i})\}_{\alpha=1}^{n-1}
of
T_{x_{i}}\partial M

let
\gamma_{\alpha}(t)=\exp_{x_{i}}(tE_{\alpha}(x_{i}))
and define

\partial_{(x_{i})^{\alpha}}U_{+,x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}}:=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}U_{+,\gamma_{\alpha}(t),\varepsilon_{i}}.

Set the slice–tangent modulation kernel space

\mathcal{K}_{i}:=\mathrm{span}\Big\{\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\ \partial_{(x_{i})^{\alpha}}\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:\alpha=1,\dots,n-1\Big\},\qquad\mathcal{K}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{K}_{i},

and let
\Pi:=\Pi_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
be the graph-orthogonal projection onto

X:=\mathcal{K}^{\perp}\cap T_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{S}
.

We work with the constrained gradient on
\mathcal{S}
as above. Critical points satisfy the Escobar Euler–Lagrange system

L_{g}^{\circ}u=0\ \text{ in }M,\qquad B_{g}^{\circ}u=\lambda(u)\,u^{q-1}\ \text{ on }\partial M,

for the associated multiplier
\lambda(u)
.

The constrained Hessian quadratic form (Jacobi form) on
T_{\mathcal{U}}\mathcal{S}
is

(4.13)

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{U}}[v]:=\langle v,v\rangle_{E,g}-(q-1)\,\lambda(\mathcal{U})\int_{\partial M}\mathcal{U}^{\,q-2}v^{2}\,d\sigma_{g}.

Step 2 (Uniform spectral gap on
X
).

The claim is obtained by decoupling the quadratic form using a partition of unity adapted to the bubble collars
and the complement, and applying the single-bubble coercivity (Theorem
2.23
) to each profile, with
interaction errors controlled by separation (
4.7
). This yields a uniform
c_{0}>0
for fixed
k
.

Step 3 (Residual bound).

Expand the constrained gradient at
\mathcal{U}
:

\Pi\,\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(\mathcal{U}+w)=\Pi\,\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(\mathcal{U})\ +\ \mathcal{L}w\ +\ \Pi\,\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U}}(w),

where
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U}}(w)
is quadratic in
w
in
H^{1}
and
\mathcal{L}:X\to X
is the linearization of

\Pi\,\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}
at
\mathcal{U}
restricted to
X
.

Define the projected residual

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{U}}\ :=\ \Pi\,\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(\mathcal{U})\ \in X.

Then (
4.8
) is equivalent (in
X
) to

\mathcal{L}w\ =\ -\,\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{U}}\ -\ \Pi\,\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U}}(w).

One has the
general
bound

\|\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{U}}\|_{H^{1}(M)}\ \leq\ C(M,g,k)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon_{i}\ +\ \sum_{i\neq j}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\Big(\Lambda^{-1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}}{d_{g}(x_{i},x_{j})}\Big)\right),

and under the refined hypothesis (
4.10
) the single-bubble contributions improve to
O(\varepsilon_{i}^{2})

thanks to Lemma
4.3
.

Step 4 (Nonlinear remainder and fixed point).

A contraction mapping (or IFT) argument on
X
, using the uniform inverse bound for
\mathcal{L}
from the spectral gap and the
quadratic estimate
\|\Pi\,\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{U}}(w)\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2}
, yields a unique solution

w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in X
with the bounds (
4.9
) and (
4.11
).
∎

Corollary 4.5

(Smooth dependence of the Lyapunov–Schmidt map)
.

Under the hypotheses of Lemma
4.4
, for fixed
k
the map

(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\ \longmapsto\ w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in H^{1}(M)

is
C^{1}
on the admissible configuration set (and
C^{2}
in the scale variables
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
on compact subsets
of the admissible set). Moreover, its derivatives satisfy uniform bounds of the form (
cf.

4.37
)

\|\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\nabla_{x_{i}}w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\|_{H^{1}}\ \leq\ C\,\mathsf{Drift}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}),

with
C
depending only on
(M,g,n)
and
k
(and monotonically on
\Lambda
).
Consequently, the reduced functional
\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
is
C^{1}
in

(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
(and
C^{2}
in
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
on compact admissible sets), and
differentiation under the reduction is legitimate.

Proof sketch.

Lemma
4.4
solves the projected equation on the Banach space
X
by a contraction mapping (equivalently by the IFT)
for the map

\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},w):=\Pi_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\,\mathsf{grad}_{\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{J}_{g}(\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+w)\in X.

The map
\mathcal{F}
is
C^{1}
in all variables on the admissible set (and
C^{2}
in
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
) by smoothness of

\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}}
and smooth dependence of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
.
The linearization in
w
is the uniformly invertible operator
\mathcal{L}:X\to X
. The claimed bounds follow by differentiating
the fixed point/IFT identity and using the uniform inverse bound for
\mathcal{L}
together with the residual estimates.
∎

Definition 4.6

(Reduced functional)
.

Let
q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
. For
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
in the admissible set, define the
Lyapunov–Schmidt
graph map
(normalized onto the slice)

\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}):=\frac{\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}{\big\|\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}}\ \in\ \mathcal{S},\qquad\mathcal{S}:=\{u\in H^{1}(M):\|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\}.

The
reduced functional
is

(4.14)

\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}):=\mathcal{J}_{g}\big(\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\big).

Equivalently, since
\mathcal{J}_{g}
is scale invariant,

\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\mathcal{J}_{g}\big(\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big).

Here
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in\mathcal{S}
is the slice-normalized multi-bubble ansatz
from Lemma
4.4
, i.e.

\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}:=t(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\sum_{i=1}^{k}U_{+,x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}},\qquad t(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}):=\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}U_{+,x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}}\Big\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}^{-1},

and
w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
is the Lyapunov–Schmidt correction provided by Lemma
4.4
.

Lemma 4.7

(Envelope identity: constrained criticality implies reduced criticality)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Lemma
4.4
for some fixed
k
and work on the slice
\mathcal{S}
.
Let
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
be admissible and let

\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}):=\frac{\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}{\big\|\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}+w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}}\in\mathcal{S}

be the (slice-valued) Lyapunov–Schmidt graph map. Then:

(a)

\Phi
is
C^{1}
in
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
and

\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\mathcal{J}_{g}(\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}))

is
C^{1}
.

(b)

If
u\in\mathcal{S}
is a constrained critical point of
\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}}
and

u=\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
for some admissible
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
, then

(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
is a critical point of the reduced functional
\mathcal{J}_{k}
, i.e.

\nabla_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\,\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=0.

Proof.

Part (a) follows from Corollary
4.5
together with smoothness of the normalization map

f\mapsto f/\|f\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}
on a neighborhood where
\|f\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}
is bounded away from
0

(which holds on the admissible set for
\varepsilon_{0}
small).

For (b), fix an admissible
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
and set
u:=\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\in\mathcal{S}
.
Since
u
is a constrained critical point on
\mathcal{S}
,

D(\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}})[u][\psi]=0\qquad\forall\,\psi\in T_{u}\mathcal{S}.

For any parameter direction
\partial_{p}
with
p\in\{\varepsilon_{i},(x_{i})^{\alpha}\}
, the derivative

\partial_{p}\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
lies in
T_{u}\mathcal{S}
because

\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\in\mathcal{S}
for all parameters. Hence

0=D(\mathcal{J}_{g}|_{\mathcal{S}})[u]\big[\partial_{p}\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\big]=\partial_{p}\big(\mathcal{J}_{g}(\Phi(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}))\big)=\partial_{p}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}),

which is exactly
\nabla_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{J}_{k}=0
.
∎

4.2.
Multi–bubble expansion and interaction kernel

Theorem 4.8

(Multi-bubble expansion with quantitative remainders)
.

Let
q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
. Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
, and the
separation condition (
4.7
). Then, as
\max_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\downarrow 0
, the following hold.

(i) First-order expansion (always).

There exist dimensional constants
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0
and
c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0

(depending only on
n
and the chosen half-space optimizer) and a remainder

\mathcal{R}_{k}^{(1)}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
such that

(4.15)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})}{S_{\ast}}

\displaystyle=k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\;+\;k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x_{i})\,\varepsilon_{i}\;+\;\sum_{i\neq j}c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\;\mathcal{R}_{k}^{(1)}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}),

where
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
is the renormalized boundary-to-boundary Green kernel for
(L^{\circ}_{g},B^{\circ}_{g})
, and

(4.16)

|\mathcal{R}_{k}^{(1)}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})|\ \leq\ C(M,g,k)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\ +\ \sum_{i\neq j}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\Big(\Lambda^{-1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}}{d_{g}(x_{i},x_{j})}\Big)\right).

(ii) Second-order refinement (under local constancy of
H_{g}
in the bubble collars).

Assume in addition
n\geq 5
and the refinement hypothesis

(4.17)

for each
i
, one has
H_{g}(x_{i})=0
and
H_{g}
is locally constant in a fixed boundary collar of
x_{i}
(uniformly in
i
),

so that the refined Lyapunov–Schmidt estimate (
4.11
) applies and the second-order
self-expansion can be inserted at each bubble center. Then there is a (possibly different) remainder

\mathcal{R}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
such that

(4.18)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})}{S_{\ast}}

\displaystyle=k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\;+\;k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big(\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,H_{g}(x_{i})\,\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})\big)\;+\;\sum_{i\neq j}c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\;\mathcal{R}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}),

and the remainder satisfies

(4.19)

|\mathcal{R}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})|\ \leq\ C(M,g,k)\left(\omega\!\left(\max_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\ +\ \sum_{i\neq j}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\Big(\Lambda^{-1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}}{d_{g}(x_{i},x_{j})}\Big)\right),

where
\omega(t)\to 0
as
t\downarrow 0
.
In particular, for fixed separation parameter
\Lambda
, as
\max_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\downarrow 0
,

\mathcal{R}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\ =\ o\!\Big(\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\Big)\ +\ O\!\Big(\Lambda^{-1}\sum_{i\neq j}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\Big).

Proof sketch.

Write
\mathcal{U}=t\sum_{i}U_{i}
with
U_{i}:=U_{+,x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}}
and
t=t(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
as in
Lemma
4.4
, and let
w=w_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
be the Lyapunov–Schmidt correction.
Since
\mathcal{J}_{g}
is smooth on the positive cone and scale invariant,
a Taylor expansion (in a slice chart) gives

\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})=\mathcal{J}_{g}(\mathcal{U})+O(\|w\|_{H^{1}(M)}^{2}).

By Lemma
4.4
we have (
4.9
), hence
O(\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2})=O(\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{2})

(up to the interaction scale), which is compatible with (
4.16
). Under
(
4.17
) we instead use (
4.11
), so
O(\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2})=O(\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{4})
,
which is negligible compared to the one-bubble truncation error at order
o(\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{2})

(and in particular
O(\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{3})
when
n\geq 6
), and compared to the interaction scale.

For
\mathcal{J}_{g}(\mathcal{U})
, apply Brezis–Lieb on
\partial M
and use the one-bubble expansion:

•

in case (i), use the first-order one-bubble law (Lemma
3.3
), which yields the

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x_{i})\varepsilon_{i}
terms and an
O(\varepsilon_{i}^{2})
remainder;

•

in case (ii), use the second-order one-bubble expansion (Theorem
3.6
),
which yields additionally
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})\varepsilon_{i}^{2}
and a remainder
o(\varepsilon_{i}^{2})

(resp.
O(\varepsilon_{i}^{3})
if
n\geq 6
).

The numerator
\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}(\mathcal{U})
splits into diagonal pieces and interaction pieces

\langle U_{i},U_{j}\rangle_{E,g}
, giving the Green-kernel contributions

c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})
with errors controlled by the
overlap factor in (
4.16
)–(
4.19
). Collecting the diagonal and interaction
contributions and inserting the control of
O(\|w\|_{H^{1}}^{2})
yields
(
4.15
)–(
4.16
) and, under (
4.17
),
(
4.18
)–(
4.19
).
∎

Remark 4.9

(Dimension split and scaling)
.

Write
\alpha=\frac{n-2}{2}
. In the multi–bubble expansions
(Theorem
4.8
(i)–(ii)), the pairwise interaction contribution has the form

(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\alpha}\,\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j}).

On collision–free configuration sets
\{\mathbf{x}:\min_{i\neq j}d_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})\geq\delta\}
the kernel

\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
(and its first derivatives) are
O_{\delta}(1)
, so interactions scale like

O_{\delta}\!\big((\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\alpha}\big)
; near the diagonal,
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
blows up with a
positive singular part (Definition
4.10
), yielding the usual collision barrier.

First-order (always).

In the general expansion (
4.15
), the self contribution appears explicitly only at order

\varepsilon
through
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x_{i})\varepsilon_{i}
; all
O(\varepsilon_{i}^{2})
self-effects are absorbed
into the remainder
\mathcal{R}_{k}^{(1)}
.

Second-order (refined regime).

In the refined expansion (
4.18
) (Theorem
4.8
(ii)), valid when
n\geq 5

(and with a genuine
O(\varepsilon_{i}^{3})
self remainder only when
n\geq 6
), self contributions scale like

\varepsilon
(via
H_{g}
) and
\varepsilon^{2}
(via
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
).

Consequently, on
\delta
–separated configurations with comparable scales
\varepsilon_{i}\sim\varepsilon
:

•

in
n=4
(
\alpha=1
) interactions are
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, the same order as the (log–renormalized) second–order self term,
and hence compete directly with it for center selection;

•

in
n\geq 5
(
\alpha>1
) interactions are
O(\varepsilon^{2\alpha})=o(\varepsilon^{2})
and are negligible at the

\varepsilon^{2}
level;

•

in
n=3
(
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}
) interactions are
O(\varepsilon)
and therefore compete with the linear

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}\,\varepsilon
term; if the linear term vanishes (e.g.
H_{g}\equiv 0
or at centers with
H_{g}=0
),
then interactions dominate the
quadratic remainder
in (
4.15
).

Accordingly, our
\varepsilon^{2}
-level, center-only reductions are carried out only in the refined regime where

\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is available and the interaction scale is controlled on collision–free configurations.

Definition 4.10

(Interaction kernel)
.

The
interaction kernel

\mathsf{G}_{\partial}:\big(\partial M\times\partial M\big)\setminus\mathrm{diag}\to\mathbb{R}

is the renormalized boundary–to–boundary Green kernel for the self–adjoint Dirichlet-to-Neumann map
\mathcal{S}_{g}

associated with
(L_{g}^{\circ},B_{g}^{\circ})
, appearing in
(
4.15
) and (
4.18
). Under
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
2+
)

it satisfies:

(i)

Symmetry:

\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x,y)=\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(y,x)
for all
x\neq y
.

(ii)

Local positive singular part:
Near the diagonal, the leading singularity has a positive coefficient:

\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x,y)\ \sim\ \begin{cases}a_{n}\,d_{\partial}(x,y)^{\,2-n},&n\geq 4,\\
a_{3}\,d_{\partial}(x,y)^{-1},&n=3,\end{cases}\qquad a_{n},a_{3}>0,

so in particular
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x,y)\to+\infty
as
x\to y
.

(iii)

Global sign is geometry dependent:
Away from the diagonal, the renormalized kernel may change sign depending
on
(M,g)
; there is no universal pointwise positivity.

Theorem 4.11

(No bubbling at
S_{\ast}
from positive renormalized mass)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
, and
(BG
3+
)
.
Suppose

C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast},\qquad H_{g}\ \text{is Morse on }\partial M,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\min_{x\in\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})}\ \mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\ >\ 0.

Then every sequence of
positive constrained critical points

u_{\ell}\in\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{J}_{g}
on the slice

\mathcal{S}:=\Big\{u\in H^{1}(M):\ \|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\Big\},\qquad q=2^{*}_{\partial},

with
\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to S_{\ast}
is precompact in
H^{1}(M)
.
In particular,
no bubbling occurs at the hemisphere threshold
. (At level
S_{\ast}
, quantization forces
k_{\ast}=1
,
so any tower with at least two nontrivial bubbles is excluded; the present result rules out even the single bubble.)

Note.
Since
n\geq 5
, one has
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0
by Lemma
2.42
.

Proof.

This is Theorem
4.29
.∎

Definition 4.12

(Isolated and isolated–simple boundary blow-up)
.

Assume
n\geq 3
. Let
(u_{\ell})
be a sequence of positive smooth solutions of the Escobar Euler–Lagrange system
on
(M,g)
, normalized by
\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
, and such that

\sup_{\partial M}u_{\ell}\ \longrightarrow\ +\infty.

A point
p\in\partial M
is a
boundary blow-up point
if there exist
x_{\ell}\in\partial M
with

x_{\ell}\to p
and
u_{\ell}(x_{\ell})\to+\infty
; one may (and will) choose
x_{\ell}
to be local maxima of
u_{\ell}

in a small boundary ball around
p
.

We say that
p
is an
isolated
blow-up point if there exist
r_{0}>0
and
C\geq 1
such that

u_{\ell}(x)\ \leq\ C\,d_{\partial}(x,x_{\ell})^{-\frac{n-2}{2}}\qquad\forall\,x\in B^{\partial}_{r_{0}}(p)\setminus\{x_{\ell}\},\ \forall\,\ell\ \text{large},

where
d_{\partial}
is the boundary distance and
B^{\partial}_{r_{0}}(p)
is the boundary geodesic ball.

Let
\bar{u}_{\ell}(r)
denote the spherical average of
u_{\ell}
over the boundary sphere
\partial B^{\partial}_{r}(x_{\ell})
:

\bar{u}_{\ell}(r):=\frac{1}{|\partial B^{\partial}_{r}(x_{\ell})|}\int_{\partial B^{\partial}_{r}(x_{\ell})}u_{\ell}\,d\sigma_{\bar{g}}.

We say that
p
is
isolated simple
if it is isolated and the function

r\ \longmapsto\ r^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\bar{u}_{\ell}(r)

has exactly one critical point in
(0,r_{0})
for all
\ell
sufficiently large.

Theorem 4.13

(No bubble towers under isolated–simple blow-up)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
2+
)
.
Let
(u_{\ell})
be a sequence of positive smooth solutions of the Escobar Euler–Lagrange system on
(M,g)
,
normalized by
\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
, and assume the energies are bounded:

\sup_{\ell}\ \mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\ <\ \infty.

Assume that every boundary blow-up point of
(u_{\ell})
is
isolated simple
in the sense of
Definition
4.12
.

Then, after passing to a subsequence, there exist:

•

a (possibly trivial) limiting solution
u_{\infty}
of the Escobar equation on
(M,g)
,

•

an integer
J\geq 0
,

•

boundary points
x_{\ell}^{j}\to p_{j}\in\partial M
and scales
\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j}\downarrow 0
for
j=1,\dots,J
,

•

and a remainder
w_{\ell}\in H^{1}(M)
with
\|w_{\ell}\|_{H^{1}(M)}\to 0
,

such that

u_{\ell}\ =\ u_{\infty}\ +\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}U^{(j)}_{\ell}\ +\ w_{\ell},

where each
U^{(j)}_{\ell}
is a boundary bubble profile (Fermi push-forward of a half-space optimizer)
with parameters
(x_{\ell}^{j},\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j})
, and the bubbles satisfy
pure center separation
:

\frac{d_{\partial}(x_{\ell}^{i},x_{\ell}^{j})}{\varepsilon_{\ell}^{i}+\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j}}\ \longrightarrow\ \infty\qquad(i\neq j).

In particular,
no bubble tower occurs
: each blow-up point
p_{j}
contributes
exactly one
bubble scale.

Remark 4.14
.

The conclusion that no bubble tower occurs holds under the isolated-simple blow-up assumption at each concentration point, which excludes the presence of secondary concentration scales. Throughout the remainder of the paper, all reduced energy expansions and Lyapunov-Schmidt reductions are carried out only under the separated (no-tower) regime, ensured either by isolated simplicity or by explicit scale comparability assumptions.

Proof.

Follows directly from Theorem
4.19
.∎

Theorem 4.15

(Enhancement: No pure bubbling in the positive-mass regime)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
, and
(BG
3+
)
.
Suppose that
H_{g}\equiv 0
on
\partial M
and that the renormalized mass is strictly positive everywhere:

\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\ >\ 0\qquad\forall\,x\in\partial M.

(By Theorem
3.22
and the Escobar–Willmore splitting, this holds, for example,
under the stated umbilic/pinching hypotheses.)

Let
(u_{\ell})
be a sequence of positive smooth solutions of the Escobar Euler–Lagrange system, normalized by

\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
, with
\sup_{\ell}\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\leq K
for some
K<\infty
.
Assume that
(u_{\ell})
exhibits
pure bubbling
, i.e.
u_{\ell}\rightharpoonup 0
weakly in
H^{1}(M)
, and that
the bubbling occurs in the
separated/no-tower regime
(for instance, ensured if every blow-up point is
isolated simple, so that Theorem
4.13
applies).

Then such a pure bubbling sequence cannot exist.

Proof sketch.

Assume by contradiction that
(u_{\ell})
is pure bubbling in the separated/no-tower regime.
After Lyapunov–Schmidt reparametrization (Lemma
4.4
), the associated reduced parameters

(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
satisfy the finite-dimensional stationarity system, in particular

\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})=0\qquad(i=1,\dots,k).

Let
\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}:=\max_{i}\varepsilon_{\ell,i}
and choose
i_{\ell}
with

\varepsilon_{\ell,i_{\ell}}=\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}
.

In the refined regime with
H_{g}\equiv 0
, differentiating the reduced expansion yields for
\alpha=\tfrac{n-2}{2}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{S_{\ast}}\,\partial_{\varepsilon_{i_{\ell}}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})

\displaystyle=2k^{-2/q}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i_{\ell}})\,\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}

\displaystyle\;\;\hskip 5.69054pt+k^{-2/q}\,c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\alpha\sum_{j\neq i_{\ell}}\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{\ell,j}^{\alpha}\Big(\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i_{\ell}},x_{\ell,j})+\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,j},x_{\ell,i_{\ell}})\Big)\;+\;o(\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}).

(
O(\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}^{2})\quad(n\geq 6)
).

To control the interaction sum without assuming boundedness of
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
, fix
r_{\ast}>0
small and split indices

j\neq i_{\ell}
into two classes:

•

if
d_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i_{\ell}},x_{\ell,j})<r_{\ast}
, then by the local asymptotic

\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x,y)=a_{n}\,d_{\partial}(x,y)^{2-n}+O(1)
with
a_{n}>0
(as
y\to x
),
choosing
r_{\ast}
small gives
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i_{\ell}},x_{\ell,j})\geq 0
and likewise with
(i_{\ell},j)
swapped;
hence these contributions are
nonnegative
;

•

if
d_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i_{\ell}},x_{\ell,j})\geq r_{\ast}
, then
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
is bounded below by
-C(r_{\ast})
, so using
\varepsilon_{\ell,j}\leq\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}
,

\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{\ell,j}^{\alpha}\Big(\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i_{\ell}},x_{\ell,j})+\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,j},x_{\ell,i_{\ell}})\Big)\ \geq\ -C(r_{\ast})\,\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{\ell,j}^{\alpha}\ \geq\ -C(r_{\ast})\,\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}^{2\alpha-1}=-C(r_{\ast})\,\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}^{\,n-3}.

Since
n\geq 5
, we have
n-3\geq 2
, hence
\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}^{\,n-3}=o(\varepsilon_{\ell,\max})
.
Moreover,
\mathfrak{R}_{g}\geq c_{0}>0
on
\partial M
by compactness.
Therefore,

\partial_{\varepsilon_{i_{\ell}}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})\ \geq\ c\,\varepsilon_{\ell,\max}\ +\ o(\varepsilon_{\ell,\max})\ >\ 0\qquad\text{for $\ell$ large},

contradicting stationarity. Hence no such pure bubbling sequence exists.
∎

4.3.
Balanced scales and center-only reduction

Standing dimension assumption for this subsection.

Throughout this subsection we assume
n\geq 5
, so that the cutoff–independent renormalized mass

\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is defined (Definition
3.5
). The borderline case
n=4
requires logarithmic
renormalization/cutoff bookkeeping and is not treated here.

Lemma 4.16

(Scale elimination via the implicit function theorem)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
and suppose
n\geq 5
so that the cutoff–independent
renormalized mass
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is defined (Definition
3.5
). Write

q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2},\qquad\alpha=\frac{n-2}{2}>1.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})\cap\{H_{g}=0\}
be compact and assume we are in the
refined regime

near
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. the hypothesis (
4.17
) holds uniformly for all bubble centers in a
neighborhood of
\mathcal{C}
and the refined expansion (
4.18
) (with remainder (
4.19
))
applies there. Assume also that

\inf_{x\in\mathcal{C}}|\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)|\ \geq\ r_{0}\ >\ 0.

Fix
k\geq 1
and
\delta>0
, and set the
\delta
–separated configuration set

\mathfrak{C}_{k}^{\delta}(\mathcal{C}):=\Big\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{C}^{k}:\ \min_{i\neq j}d_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})\geq\delta\Big\}.

For admissible
(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
define the
scale stationarity system

\mathcal{F}_{i}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\ :=\ \partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}),\qquad i=1,\dots,k,

(to avoid confusion with the monotone reparametrization
F_{k}=(\mathcal{J}_{k}/S_{\ast})^{n-1}
introduced in the
functional conventions).

Then there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
\Lambda\gg 1
such that whenever

\mathbf{x}\in\mathfrak{C}_{k}^{\delta}(\mathcal{C})
and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]^{k}
satisfy the
separation condition (
4.7
), the
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
–Jacobian satisfies, for all
1\leq i,j\leq k
,

(4.20)

\partial_{\varepsilon_{j}}\mathcal{F}_{i}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\ =\ 2S_{\ast}\,k^{-2/q}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})\,\delta_{ij}\ +\ \mathcal{E}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}),

where the error matrix
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
obeys the uniform bounds

(4.21)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{ii}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})|

\displaystyle\ \leq\ C_{\delta}\Big(\varepsilon_{i}\ +\ \varepsilon_{i}^{\alpha-2}\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{j}^{\alpha}\Big),

(4.22)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})|

\displaystyle\ \leq\ C_{\delta}\,(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\alpha-1}\qquad(i\neq j),

for a constant
C_{\delta}
depending only on
\delta
,
(M,g,n)
and
k
(and monotonically on the fixed
\Lambda
).

Uniform invertibility in standard regimes.

•

If
n\geq 6
(so
\alpha\geq 2
), then after shrinking
\varepsilon_{0}
(depending on
\delta,r_{0},k
)
the matrix
\partial_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})
is uniformly invertible on

\mathfrak{C}_{k}^{\delta}(\mathcal{C})\times(0,\varepsilon_{0}]^{k}
under (
4.7
).

•

If
n=5
(so
\alpha=\tfrac{3}{2}
), the same uniform invertibility holds on any scale–comparable subset

\max_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\leq C_{0}\min_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
(for fixed
C_{0}\geq 1
), after possibly shrinking
\varepsilon_{0}

depending also on
C_{0}
.

Local scale elimination (IFT).

Consequently, if
(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
is admissible with

\bar{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathfrak{C}_{k}^{\delta}(\mathcal{C})
,
\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]^{k}
,

\mathcal{F}(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=0
, and

\partial_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\mathcal{F}(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})
invertible
(e.g. in the regimes above), then there exist neighborhoods

\bar{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{U}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in\mathcal{V}
and a unique
C^{1}
map

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{V}
such that

\mathcal{F}\big(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x})\big)\equiv 0\qquad\text{for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{U},\qquad\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\bar{\mathbf{x}})=\bar{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}.

Moreover, if
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x})
is the
C^{1}
scale map given by the IFT and

\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}))
, then

\nabla_{x_{i}}\widehat{\mathcal{J}}_{k}(\mathbf{x})=\partial_{x_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}))\qquad(i=1,\dots,k),

since
\partial_{\varepsilon_{j}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(\mathbf{x}))\equiv 0
.

Proof.

Work in the refined regime and use (
4.18
) (Theorem
4.8
(ii)):

\frac{\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})}{S_{\ast}}=k^{1-\frac{2}{q}}+k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\!\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big(\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x_{i})\varepsilon_{i}+\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\big)+\sum_{i\neq j}c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\alpha}\,\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})\right]+\mathcal{R}_{k}(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}),

with
|\mathcal{R}_{k}|
controlled by (
4.19
). Differentiate in
\varepsilon_{i}
to obtain

\frac{\mathcal{F}_{i}}{S_{\ast}}=k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\Big(\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x_{i})+2\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})\varepsilon_{i}\Big)+k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\alpha\,c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{i}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{j}^{\alpha}\,\Big(\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})+\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{j},x_{i})\Big)+\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{R}_{k}.

Differentiate once more in
\varepsilon_{j}
.
For
j\neq i
,

\frac{\partial_{\varepsilon_{j}}\mathcal{F}_{i}}{S_{\ast}}=k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\alpha^{2}\,c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,\varepsilon_{i}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{j}^{\alpha-1}\,\Big(\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})+\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{j},x_{i})\Big)+\partial_{\varepsilon_{j}}\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{R}_{k},

and for
j=i
,

\frac{\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{F}_{i}}{S_{\ast}}=2k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})+k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\alpha(\alpha-1)\,c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{i}^{\alpha-2}\varepsilon_{j}^{\alpha}\,\Big(\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})+\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{j},x_{i})\Big)+\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{k}.

On
\mathfrak{C}_{k}^{\delta}(\mathcal{C})
the kernel
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
is bounded (and smooth), so the explicit interaction
pieces are controlled by the right-hand sides of (
4.21
)–(
4.22
).
Moreover,
\mathcal{J}_{k}
(hence
\mathcal{R}_{k}
) is
C^{2}
in the scale variables on admissible configuration sets, and
differentiating the overlap estimates underlying (
4.19
) yields, after shrinking
\varepsilon_{0}
and fixing

\Lambda\gg 1
,

|\partial_{\varepsilon_{j}}\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{R}_{k}|\ \leq\ C_{\delta}\,(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j})^{\alpha-1}\quad(j\neq i),\qquad|\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{k}|\ \leq\ C_{\delta}\Big(\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}^{\alpha-2}\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{j}^{\alpha}\Big),

uniformly under (
4.7
) and
\min_{i\neq j}d_{\partial}(x_{i},x_{j})\geq\delta
.
Collecting terms gives (
4.20
)–(
4.22
).

For invertibility, note that the diagonal entries converge (in the regimes stated) to
2S_{\ast}k^{-2/q}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})
uniformly, while the sums of off-diagonal entries are
o(1)
as
\max_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\to 0
; Gershgorin’s theorem yields uniform invertibility for
\varepsilon_{0}
small. The final assertion is the standard implicit function theorem.∎

Remark 4.17

(Scale elimination versus scale selection)
.

Lemma
4.16
is an
implicit-function
statement: it eliminates scales
locally around a
balanced-scale solution
provided
\inf_{\mathcal{C}}|\mathfrak{R}_{g}|>0
.
It does
not
assert existence of such solutions.

For
actual bubbling
of constrained critical points, the scale stationarity equations can instead be used as a

selection/obstruction principle
. In macroscopically separated, scale-comparable regimes (so interactions are
lower order), stationarity forces
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p_{i})=0
at each limiting blow-up center; see
Theorem
4.25
. In particular, if
|\mathfrak{R}_{g}|
is bounded away from
0
on a collar, then
pure bubbling in that collar is impossible.

4.4.
Threshold classification: leading and next order and a Struwe decomposition

Theorem 4.18

(Quantization and leading-order selection)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
.
Let
(u_{\ell})
be a sequence of positive critical points of
\mathcal{J}_{g}
with
\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)
.
Since
\mathcal{J}_{g}
is scale-invariant, after rescaling we may assume
\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1

(so
u_{\ell}\in\mathcal{S}
), where
q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
.
If
(u_{\ell})
blows up, then
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
. Moreover, after passing to a subsequence there exist

1\leq k_{\ast}<\infty
, centers
x_{\ell,i}\to p_{i}\in\partial M
, scales

\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\downarrow 0
, and the decomposition

(4.23)

u_{\ell}=\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}+w_{\ell},\qquad\|w_{\ell}\|_{H^{1}(M)}\to 0,

where
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=t(\mathbf{x},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\sum_{i=1}^{k_{\ast}}U_{+,x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}}

is the slice-normalized ansatz from Lemma
4.4
.
The parameters satisfy the separation

\frac{d_{g}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})}{\varepsilon_{\ell,i}+\varepsilon_{\ell,j}}\ \longrightarrow\ \infty\qquad(i\neq j),

and the energy quantizes as

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]=k_{\ast}^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\,S_{\ast}+o(1).

(A) General leading-order balance (always).

Let
\alpha=\tfrac{n-2}{2}
. At the level of first variations in the center variables,
the stationarity of the reduced functional at the parameters

(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
in (
4.23
) implies the leading balance law

(4.24)

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,\nabla H_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\;+\;2c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{\ell,j}^{\alpha}\,\nabla_{1}\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})\;=\;O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}),\qquad i=1,\dots,k_{\ast},

where
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
is the boundary interaction kernel (Definition
4.10
)
and
c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0
depends only on
n
and the chosen optimizer.

(B) Refined next-order balance (in a second-order regime,
n\geq 5
).

Assume in addition
n\geq 5
and that the hypotheses needed for the
refined
multi-bubble expansion hold
in collars of the blow-up set (so that the
\varepsilon_{i}^{2}
self-term is defined intrinsically and
Theorem
4.8
(ii)
applies; in particular
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is defined on the relevant collars).
Then the balance law refines to

(4.25)

\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,\nabla H_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\;+\;\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\,\nabla\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\;+\;2c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{\ell,j}^{\alpha}\,\nabla_{1}\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})\;=\;O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}),\qquad i=1,\dots,k_{\ast}.

In particular:

•

If
k_{\ast}=1
(for any
n\geq 3
), then

(4.26)

\big\|\nabla H_{g}(x_{\ell,1})\big\|\leq C\,\varepsilon_{\ell,1}\ \longrightarrow\ 0,

so
p_{1}\in\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})
.

•

If
n\geq 5
and, in addition, the centers remain macroscopically separated

\inf_{\ell}\ \min_{i\neq j}d_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})\ \geq\ d_{0}>0,

and the scales are uniformly comparable (there exists
C_{0}\geq 1
with

C_{0}^{-1}\leq\varepsilon_{\ell,j}/\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\leq C_{0}
for all
i,j
and large
\ell
), then
(
4.26
) holds for each
i=1,\dots,k_{\ast}
, hence
p_{i}\in\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})
for all
i
.

Without these additional separation/comparability hypotheses, the centers satisfy
(
4.24
) (and, in the refined regime, (
4.25
)); in particular,
if the interaction sum is
o(1)
after division by
\varepsilon_{\ell,i}
, then
\nabla H_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\to 0

and
p_{i}\in\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})
.

Proof.

Location of concentration and the decomposition.

For Euler–Lagrange solutions of the Escobar problem,

L_{g}^{\circ}u_{\ell}=0\ \text{ in }M,\qquad B_{g}^{\circ}u_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}\,u_{\ell}^{q-1}\ \text{ on }\partial M,\qquad q=2^{*}_{\partial}.

Since the nonlinearity is supported on
\partial M
, any blow-up profile is governed by boundary concentration.
By boundary concentration–compactness at the critical trace exponent and the classification of half-space extremals,
after extraction we obtain the boundary profile decomposition (
4.23
) with separation and
energy quantization
\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]=k_{\ast}^{1-2/q}S_{\ast}+o(1)
.

Since always
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)\leq S_{\ast}
and
k_{\ast}^{1-2/q}\geq 1
(with equality only if
k_{\ast}=1
), blow-up forces

k_{\ast}=1
and hence
C^{*}_{\mathrm{Esc}}(M,g)=S_{\ast}
.

Balanced centers and reduced criticality.

Let
(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
denote the parameters of the bubbles in (
4.23
).
Since
u_{\ell}
solve the Euler–Lagrange system on the constraint slice and are reparametrized by the
Lyapunov–Schmidt graph map, Lemma
4.7
yields that the associated parameters

(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
are stationary for the reduced functional:

\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k_{\ast}}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})=0,\qquad\partial_{x_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k_{\ast}}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})=0\qquad(i=1,\dots,k_{\ast})

(here
\mathcal{J}_{k_{\ast}}
is the reduced functional, Definition
4.6
).
Alternatively, in an IFT regime one may first eliminate scales by solving

\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k_{\ast}}=0
(cf. Lemma
4.16
); along the resulting balanced
scale map the chain terms in
x
–differentiation vanish.

Center derivative of the reduced expansion: leading order.

Differentiate the
general
multi-bubble expansion at leading order (Theorem
4.8
(i))
with respect to
x_{i}
. The
x_{i}
–dependence enters through
H_{g}(x_{i})
and through the interaction kernel.
Using symmetry of
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
and the double-sum convention in (
4.15
), this gives

\partial_{x_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k_{\ast}}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})=S_{\ast}\,k_{\ast}^{-2/q}\Big(\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\,\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\,\nabla H_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\Big)+2S_{\ast}\,k_{\ast}^{-2/q}\,c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\sum_{j\neq i}(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\varepsilon_{\ell,j})^{\alpha}\,\nabla_{1}\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})+O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2}),

and dividing by
\varepsilon_{\ell,i}
and using stationarity yields (
4.24
).

Refined order (optional).

Under the refined second-order regime near the blow-up set (Theorem
4.8
(ii)),
the reduced expansion contains the additional self term
\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i})
(for
n\geq 5
).
Differentiating this contributes
S_{\ast}\,k_{\ast}^{-2/q}\,\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2}\nabla\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i})
to

\partial_{x_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k_{\ast}}
, hence after division by
\varepsilon_{\ell,i}
it gives the correction

\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\nabla\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i})
in (
4.25
). The remainder terms
remain
O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i})
after division by
\varepsilon_{\ell,i}
.

Consequences.

If
k_{\ast}=1
, the interaction sum is absent and (
4.26
) follows immediately.
If
n\geq 5
and the centers are macroscopically separated with uniformly comparable scales, then

\|\nabla_{1}\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})\|\leq C(d_{0})
and

\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{\ell,j}^{\alpha}\lesssim\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2\alpha-1}=\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{n-3}=O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2})
, so the interaction term is
O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2})
.
Thus (
4.24
) implies
\|\nabla H_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\|\lesssim\varepsilon_{\ell,i}
for each
i
.
∎

Theorem 4.19

(Global compactness at Escobar multiples; quantization and bubble decomposition)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
2+
)
and fix
n\geq 3
.
Let
q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
and let

\mathcal{S}:=\Big\{u\in H^{1}(M):\ \|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\Big\}.

Let
(u_{\ell})\subset\mathcal{S}
be a positive Palais–Smale sequence for
\mathcal{J}_{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
, with

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\ \longrightarrow\ k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\,S_{\ast}\;=\;k^{\frac{1}{n-1}}\,S_{\ast}\qquad\text{as }\ell\to\infty,

for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\lambda_{\ell}
be the associated multipliers (so that

B_{g}^{\circ}u_{\ell}-\lambda_{\ell}u_{\ell}^{q-1}\to 0
on
\partial M
); then necessarily

\lambda_{\ell}\ \longrightarrow\ \lambda_{\infty}:=k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\,S_{\ast}.

Then, up to a subsequence, there exist:

•

a (possibly trivial) solution
u_{\infty}\in H^{1}(M)
of

L_{g}^{\circ}u_{\infty}=0\ \text{ in }M,\qquad B_{g}^{\circ}u_{\infty}=\lambda_{\infty}\,u_{\infty}^{q-1}\ \text{ on }\partial M,

•

an integer
J\geq 0
,

•

points
x_{\ell}^{j}\in\partial M
and scales
\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j}\downarrow 0
for
j=1,\dots,J
, with the separation

d_{\partial}(x_{\ell}^{i},x_{\ell}^{j})\ \longrightarrow\ d_{ij}\in[0,\infty]\qquad(i\neq j),

•

and a remainder
w_{\ell}\in H^{1}(M)
with
\|w_{\ell}\|_{H^{1}(M)}\to 0
,

such that the Struwe-type decomposition holds:

(4.27)

u_{\ell}\ =\ u_{\infty}\ +\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}U^{(j)}_{\ell}\ +\ w_{\ell},

where each
U^{(j)}_{\ell}
is a boundary bubble profile (Fermi push-forward of a half-space optimizer) with
parameters
(x_{\ell}^{j},\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j})
.

No–tower refinement under an additional hypothesis.

If, in addition, every boundary blow–up point is isolated simple (Definition
4.12
),
then one may choose the extracted bubbles so that the pure separation condition holds:

\frac{d_{g}(x_{\ell}^{i},x_{\ell}^{j})}{\varepsilon_{\ell}^{i}+\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j}}\ \longrightarrow\ \infty\qquad(i\neq j),

i.e. no bubble tower occurs. This refinement is recorded separately in
Theorem
4.13
.

Moreover, the boundary
L^{q}
–mass and the graph numerator split as

(4.28)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial M}u_{\ell}^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial M}u_{\infty}^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}\ +\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}\int_{\partial M}|U^{(j)}_{\ell}|^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}\ +\ o(1),

(4.29)

\displaystyle\langle u_{\ell},u_{\ell}\rangle_{E,g}

\displaystyle=\langle u_{\infty},u_{\infty}\rangle_{E,g}\ +\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}\langle U^{(j)}_{\ell},U^{(j)}_{\ell}\rangle_{E,g}\ +\ o(1).

In particular, since
u_{\ell}\in\mathcal{S}
, the limit masses satisfy

1=\int_{\partial M}u_{\infty}^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}\ +\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}m_{j},\qquad m_{j}:=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\partial M}|U^{(j)}_{\ell}|^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}\in(0,1].

Each bubble profile is a half-space optimizer, hence

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\langle U^{(j)}_{\ell},U^{(j)}_{\ell}\rangle_{E,g}=S_{\ast}\,m_{j}^{2/q},\qquad\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathcal{J}_{g}[U^{(j)}_{\ell}]=S_{\ast},

and therefore

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\langle u_{\ell},u_{\ell}\rangle_{E,g}=\langle u_{\infty},u_{\infty}\rangle_{E,g}\ +\ S_{\ast}\sum_{j=1}^{J}m_{j}^{2/q}.

Finally, since
\lambda_{\ell}\to\lambda_{\infty}
and each blow-up profile solves the same half-space equation with
parameter
\lambda_{\infty}
, classification fixes the bubble mass:

m_{j}=\Big(\frac{S_{\ast}}{\lambda_{\infty}}\Big)^{n-1}=\frac{1}{k}\quad\text{for all }j.

Consequently
J\leq k
, and if
u_{\infty}\equiv 0
then
J=k
.

Remark 4.20

(Positivity of the weak limit)
.

In Theorem
4.19
we assume
u_{\ell}>0
. Since
u_{\ell}\rightharpoonup u_{\infty}
in
H^{1}(M)
and

u_{\ell}\to u_{\infty}
in
L^{r}(\partial M)
for every
1\leq r<q
, we have
u_{\infty}\geq 0
a.e. on
\partial M
, hence

u_{\infty}\geq 0
in
M
.
Moreover, either
u_{\infty}\equiv 0
, or else
u_{\infty}>0
on
\overline{M}
.
Indeed, if
u_{\infty}\not\equiv 0
, then the strong maximum principle applied to
L_{g}^{\circ}u_{\infty}=0
gives

u_{\infty}>0
in
M^{\circ}
. If
u_{\infty}(p)=0
for some
p\in\partial M
, then
p
is a boundary minimum of a nontrivial
nonnegative solution of
L_{g}^{\circ}u=0
, so Hopf’s boundary lemma yields
\partial_{\nu}u_{\infty}(p)\neq 0

(with
\nu
the outward unit normal). On the other hand, the boundary equation

B_{g}^{\circ}u_{\infty}=\lambda_{\infty}u_{\infty}^{q-1}
implies
B_{g}^{\circ}u_{\infty}(p)=0
, hence

\partial_{\nu}u_{\infty}(p)=0
since
u_{\infty}(p)=0
, a contradiction.

Proof.

We write
\ell
for the sequence index to avoid conflict with the fixed dimension
n
.

Step 1 (No interior bubbling).

If bubbling occurred at an interior point
y_{0}\in M^{\circ}
, then (using that
(u_{\ell})
is Palais–Smale on
\mathcal{S}
)
one has residuals

L_{g}^{\circ}u_{\ell}\to 0\ \text{in }H^{-1}(M),\qquad B_{g}^{\circ}u_{\ell}-\lambda_{\ell}u_{\ell}^{q-1}\to 0\ \text{in }H^{-1/2}(\partial M).

Rescaling around
y_{0}
produces a nontrivial harmonic limit in
\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, hence
0
by Liouville, a contradiction.
Thus concentration can occur only on
\partial M
.

Step 2 (Weak limit and multiplier limit).

By coercivity under (Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
) and boundedness of
(u_{\ell})
in
H^{1}(M)
, after a subsequence

u_{\ell}\rightharpoonup u_{\infty}\ \text{in }H^{1}(M),\qquad u_{\ell}\to u_{\infty}\ \text{in }L^{r}(\partial M)\ (1\leq r<q).

Testing the boundary residual against
u_{\ell}
and using
\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
yields

\lambda_{\ell}=\langle u_{\ell},u_{\ell}\rangle_{E,g}+o(1)=\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]+o(1),

hence
\lambda_{\ell}\to\lambda_{\infty}=k^{1-2/q}S_{\ast}
.
Passing to the limit in the residual system gives

L_{g}^{\circ}u_{\infty}=0\ \text{in }M,\qquad B_{g}^{\circ}u_{\infty}=\lambda_{\infty}\,u_{\infty}^{q-1}\ \text{on }\partial M.

Step 3 (Concentration–compactness on
\partial M
and extraction of bubbles).

Set
\mu_{\ell}:=u_{\ell}^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}
on
\partial M
. By concentration–compactness at the critical trace exponent,
after passing to a subsequence,

\mu_{\ell}\ \xrightharpoonup{\ast}\ u_{\infty}^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}\ +\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}m_{j}\,\delta_{p_{j}},\qquad m_{j}>0,\ p_{j}\in\partial M,

for some finite
J
.

Fix an atom
p_{j}
and choose
r>0
so small that
B_{r}^{\partial}(p_{j})
contains no other atom.
Since
u_{\ell}>0
, pick
x_{\ell}^{j}\in\overline{B_{r}^{\partial}(p_{j})}
such that

u_{\ell}(x_{\ell}^{j})=\max_{\overline{B_{r}^{\partial}(p_{j})}}u_{\ell}
.
Then
u_{\ell}(x_{\ell}^{j})\to+\infty
: otherwise
\sup_{\ell}\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r}^{\partial}(p_{j}))}<\infty
would imply

\mu_{\ell}(B_{\rho}^{\partial}(p_{j}))\ \leq\ C\,|B_{\rho}^{\partial}(p_{j})|\ \xrightarrow[\rho\downarrow 0]{}\ 0\quad\text{uniformly in }\ell,

contradicting the presence of an atom of mass
m_{j}>0
at
p_{j}
.
Define the blow-up scale

\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j}\ :=\ u_{\ell}(x_{\ell}^{j})^{-\frac{2}{n-2}}\ \downarrow\ 0.

Let
\Phi_{x_{\ell}^{j}}
be a boundary Fermi chart centered at
x_{\ell}^{j}
and define the rescaled functions

v_{\ell}^{j}(z)\ :=\ (\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,u_{\ell}\!\big(\Phi_{x_{\ell}^{j}}(\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j}z)\big),\qquad z\in B_{r/\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j}}^{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}.

Then
v_{\ell}^{j}(0)=1
and
v_{\ell}^{j}\geq 0
.
Using the Palais–Smale relations (in particular
L_{g}^{\circ}u_{\ell}\to 0
in
H^{-1}(M)
and, since
u_{\ell}>0
,

B_{g}^{\circ}u_{\ell}-\lambda_{\ell}u_{\ell}^{q-1}\to 0
in
H^{-1/2}(\partial M)
) together with (BG
2+
)
(to identify the rescaled operators with the Euclidean ones on compact sets in Fermi coordinates), standard local elliptic
estimates imply that
(v_{\ell}^{j})
is bounded in
H^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
and, after extracting a further subsequence,

v_{\ell}^{j}\ \longrightarrow\ V^{(j)}\quad\text{in }C^{1,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\ \text{and }H^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})

for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, where
V^{(j)}(0)=1
and
V^{(j)}\geq 0
.
The limit
V^{(j)}
is a nontrivial weak solution of the limiting half-space problem with parameter

\lambda_{\infty}=\lim_{\ell}\lambda_{\ell}
,

-\Delta V^{(j)}=0\ \text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\qquad B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}^{\circ}V^{(j)}=\lambda_{\infty}\,(V^{(j)})^{q-1}\ \text{ on }\partial\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},

and it has finite energy (equivalently
V^{(j)}\in\dot{H}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})
with
V^{(j)}(\cdot,0)\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
).
Since
V^{(j)}\geq 0
and
V^{(j)}\not\equiv 0
, the maximum principle and Hopf lemma give
V^{(j)}>0
on
\overline{\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}}
.
By the classification of positive finite-energy solutions of the half-space critical problem (equivalently, extremals for the
sharp trace inequality),
V^{(j)}
is a translate/dilate of the standard half-space optimizer (with normalization adjusted to
match the parameter
\lambda_{\infty}
).

Let
U_{\ell}^{(j)}
be the corresponding cut-off Fermi push-forward at
(x_{\ell}^{j},\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j})
.
Subtracting
U_{\ell}^{(j)}
removes the atom
m_{j}
at
p_{j}
; iterating over the finitely many atoms yields bubbles

U_{\ell}^{(j)}
and a remainder
w_{\ell}
with no residual concentration, hence
w_{\ell}\to 0
strongly in
H^{1}(M)
and the
decomposition (
4.27
).

Step 4 (Splitting identities).

The Brezis–Lieb lemma on
\partial M
gives (
4.28
).
Quadratic decoupling of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{E,g}
under separation gives (
4.29
).
Since each limiting profile saturates the sharp trace inequality,

\langle U^{(j)}_{\ell},U^{(j)}_{\ell}\rangle_{E,g}\ \longrightarrow\ S_{\ast}\,m_{j}^{2/q}.

Step 5 (Equal-mass conclusion from the fixed multiplier).

Since
\lambda_{\ell}\to\lambda_{\infty}
, all limiting bubbles solve the same half-space equation with parameter
\lambda_{\infty}
;
classification gives
m_{j}=(S_{\ast}/\lambda_{\infty})^{n-1}=1/k
.
∎

Remark 4.21

(Positivity convention and the boundary nonlinearity)
.

On the constraint slice

\mathcal{S}=\Big\{u\in H^{1}(M):\int_{\partial M}|u|^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}=1\Big\},\qquad q=2^{*}_{\partial},

the tangent space and the constrained Euler–Lagrange/Palais–Smale conditions are naturally expressed using the odd map

u\mapsto|u|^{q-2}u
on
\partial M
.
Throughout the Escobar part of the paper we work in sign-preserving neighborhoods and, in particular, with

positive
Palais–Smale sequences and
positive
constrained critical points (geometric conformal factors).
Accordingly we often write
u^{q-1}
in place of
|u|^{q-2}u
, and we build bubble manifolds using the positive half-space
optimizer
U_{+}
.
Sign-changing Palais–Smale sequences may exhibit additional phenomena (e.g. nodal bubbles) and are not treated here.

Remark 4.22

(Struwe decomposition at an arbitrary Palais–Smale level)
.

Theorem
4.19
is stated at the quantized levels

\lambda_{\infty}=k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}S_{\ast}
because these are exactly the levels at which
pure bubbling

(
u_{\infty}\equiv 0
) can occur on the constraint slice. If one only wants a Struwe decomposition (without fixing a
priori a quantized level), the same proof yields the following variant.

Assume
(u_{\ell})\subset\mathcal{S}
is a positive Palais–Smale sequence for
\mathcal{J}_{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
with
\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\ \longrightarrow\ \Lambda\in(0,\infty)
. Testing the boundary residual against
u_{\ell}
(and using
\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
) gives

\lambda_{\ell}=\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]+o(1)
, hence
\lambda_{\ell}\to\lambda_{\infty}:=\Lambda
.
Then Steps 0–3 in the proof of Theorem
4.19
are unchanged: after passing to a subsequence,
there exist a (possibly trivial) limit solution
u_{\infty}
, an integer
J\geq 0
, boundary bubbles
U^{(j)}_{\ell}

(
j=1,\dots,J
), and a remainder
w_{\ell}\to 0
in
H^{1}(M)
such that

u_{\ell}=u_{\infty}+\sum_{j=1}^{J}U^{(j)}_{\ell}+w_{\ell},

together with the splitting identities (
4.28
)–(
4.29
).

The only change is the final “equal–mass” computation. Writing

m_{j}:=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\partial M}|U^{(j)}_{\ell}|^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}\in(0,1],

each blow-up profile converges in Fermi rescalings to a nontrivial half-space bubble with parameter
\lambda_{\infty}=\Lambda
.
Consequently (exactly as in Step 4 of Theorem
4.19
) one has, for each
j
,

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\langle U^{(j)}_{\ell},U^{(j)}_{\ell}\rangle_{E,g}=\Lambda\,m_{j},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\langle U^{(j)}_{\ell},U^{(j)}_{\ell}\rangle_{E,g}=S_{\ast}\,m_{j}^{2/q},

since the limiting half-space profile is an optimizer for the sharp trace inequality.
Combining these identities yields

m_{j}^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}=\frac{S_{\ast}}{\Lambda}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad m_{j}=\Big(\frac{S_{\ast}}{\Lambda}\Big)^{n-1}\qquad\text{for all }j,

so all bubbles have the same boundary
L^{q}
–mass determined solely by
\Lambda
.
In particular, the mass splitting becomes

1=\int_{\partial M}|u_{\infty}|^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}\ +\ J\Big(\frac{S_{\ast}}{\Lambda}\Big)^{n-1},\qquad\text{hence}\qquad J\ \leq\ \Big(\frac{\Lambda}{S_{\ast}}\Big)^{n-1}.

In the
pure bubbling
case
u_{\infty}\equiv 0
this forces

\Lambda=J^{1/(n-1)}S_{\ast}
(quantization), and then
m_{j}=1/J
.
The no–tower refinement under isolated–simple blow-up is unchanged. The Struwe-type decomposition used here follows a well-established strategy; see
[
45
]
for a detailed and careful treatment on compact Riemannian manifolds (with or without boundary) for polyharmonic operators.

Corollary 4.23

(Threshold case
k=1
)
.

If
\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to S_{\ast}
and
(u_{\ell})
is positive Palais–Smale on
\mathcal{S}
, then either
u_{\ell}\to u_{\infty}
in
H^{1}(M)
, or

u_{\ell}\ =\ U_{+,x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell}}\ +\ o_{H^{1}}(1),

i.e. one boundary bubble.

Corollary 4.24

(Level
k^{1/(n-1)}S_{\ast}
and number of bubbles)
.

If
\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to k^{1/(n-1)}S_{\ast}
and
(u_{\ell})
is positive Palais–Smale on
\mathcal{S}
, then in (
4.27
)
the number of extracted boundary bubbles satisfies
J\in\{0,1,\dots,k\}
.
In particular,
J=k
whenever
u_{\infty}\equiv 0
.

Theorem 4.25

(Next-order selection and obstruction by the renormalized mass)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
and the hypotheses of Theorem
4.19
.
Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and set
q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
and
\alpha=\frac{n-2}{2}>1
.
Let

\mathcal{S}:=\Big\{u\in H^{1}(M):\ \|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\Big\}.

Assume we are in the
refined regime
in a fixed boundary collar neighborhood of a compact set

\mathcal{C}\subset\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})\cap\{H_{g}=0\},

namely:
H_{g}\equiv 0
on that collar and the (cutoff–independent) renormalized mass
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is defined there
(Definition
3.5
).

Let
(u_{\ell})\subset\mathcal{S}
be a sequence of
positive constrained critical points
of
\mathcal{J}_{g}
on
\mathcal{S}

(equivalently: smooth positive Escobar Euler–Lagrange solutions normalized by
\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
) such that

\mathcal{J}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\ \longrightarrow\ k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\,S_{\ast}\qquad\text{and}\qquad u_{\infty}\equiv 0

in the Struwe decomposition of Theorem
4.19
, so that
J=k
boundary bubbles are extracted.
Let
(x_{\ell,i},\varepsilon_{\ell,i})
be the associated Lyapunov–Schmidt parameters
(so
u_{\ell}=\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}+w_{\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}
with

\mathbf{x}_{\ell}=(x_{\ell,1},\dots,x_{\ell,k})
and
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell}=(\varepsilon_{\ell,1},\dots,\varepsilon_{\ell,k})
).

Assume the centers remain macroscopically separated and the scales are uniformly comparable:

(4.30)

\inf_{\ell}\ \min_{i\neq j}d_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})\ \geq\ d_{0}>0,\qquad C_{0}^{-1}\ \leq\ \frac{\varepsilon_{\ell,j}}{\varepsilon_{\ell,i}}\ \leq\ C_{0}\qquad(i,j,\ \ell\ \text{large}),

and that

x_{\ell,i}\ \longrightarrow\ p_{i}\in\mathcal{C}\qquad(i=1,\dots,k).

Then the limiting centers satisfy the
selection laws

(4.31)

\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p_{i})=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nabla\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p_{i})=0\qquad\text{for every }i=1,\dots,k,

where
\nabla
denotes the boundary gradient on
\partial M
.

Equivalently, the limiting ordered configuration
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{k})
lies in

\big(\mathrm{Crit}(\mathfrak{R}_{g})\cap\{\mathfrak{R}_{g}=0\}\big)^{k},

and therefore (writing
\mathcal{W}_{k}(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{i})
)

\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\mathcal{W}_{k}(\mathbf{p})=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{W}_{k}(\mathbf{p})=0.

Obstruction (nonexistence) as a corollary.

In particular, if
\inf_{x\in\mathcal{C}}|\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)|>0
, then no such pure bubbling sequence of
constrained critical points can concentrate in that collar.

Proof.

Since
(u_{\ell})
are constrained critical points on
\mathcal{S}
and
u_{\infty}\equiv 0
, after Lyapunov–Schmidt
reparametrization (Lemma
4.4
with this
k
) the associated reduced parameters

(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
are stationary for the reduced functional
\mathcal{J}_{k}

(Lemma
4.7
):

(4.32)

\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})=0,\qquad\partial_{x_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})=0\qquad(i=1,\dots,k).

Moreover, by the refined hypothesis near the bubble collars, Theorem
4.8
(ii) applies
to
\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})
.

Step 1 (
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p_{i})=0
from the scale equations).

In the refined regime with
H_{g}\equiv 0
on the relevant collar, differentiating the expansion
(
4.18
) gives, uniformly under (
4.30
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{S_{\ast}}\,\partial_{\varepsilon_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})

\displaystyle=k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\Bigg[2\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\,\varepsilon_{\ell,i}+\alpha\,c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{\ell,j}^{\alpha}\,\Big(\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})+\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,j},x_{\ell,i})\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69054pt+\ o(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}),

where the
o(\varepsilon_{\ell,i})
term comes from differentiating the quantitative remainder
(
4.19
) (note that
\omega(\max\varepsilon_{\ell,i})\to 0
).

On the macroscopically separated set (
4.30
) the kernel
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
is bounded,
and by scale comparability the interaction sum satisfies

\sum_{j\neq i}\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{\alpha-1}\varepsilon_{\ell,j}^{\alpha}\ =\ O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2\alpha-1})\ =\ O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{\,n-3})\ =\ o(\varepsilon_{\ell,i})\qquad(n\geq 5).

Thus (
4.32
) implies

0=2\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\,\varepsilon_{\ell,i}+\ o(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}).

Dividing by
\varepsilon_{\ell,i}
and letting
\ell\to\infty
yields
\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p_{i})=0
for every
i
.

Step 2 (
\nabla\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p_{i})=0
from the center equations).

Differentiate (
4.18
) in
x_{i}
(still in the refined regime with
H_{g}\equiv 0
in the collar) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{S_{\ast}}\,\partial_{x_{i}}\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell})

\displaystyle=k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\Bigg[\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2}\,\nabla\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i})+2\,c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}\sum_{j\neq i}(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\varepsilon_{\ell,j})^{\alpha}\,\nabla_{1}\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69054pt+\ o(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2}),

where the factor
2
comes from the double-sum convention in (
4.18
) together with the symmetry of

\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
, and the
o(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2})
term again comes from differentiating the remainder
(
4.19
).
By (
4.30
) the derivatives of
\mathsf{G}_{\partial}
are bounded, and by scale comparability

\sum_{j\neq i}(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\varepsilon_{\ell,j})^{\alpha}\ =\ O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2\alpha})\ =\ O(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{\,n-2})\ =\ o(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2})\qquad(n\geq 5).

Using (
4.32
) and dividing by
\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2}
yields

0=\nabla\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i})+o(1),

hence
\nabla\mathfrak{R}_{g}(p_{i})=0
for every
i
.
This proves (
4.31
) and the stated reformulations.
∎

Remark 4.26

(Why
n=4
is not treated here)
.

In
n=4
the
\varepsilon^{2}
self-term requires logarithmic renormalization and the interaction scale competes
with the next-order self-information; a clean next-order selection then involves additional bookkeeping and an
interaction term in the center-only potential.

Theorem 4.27

(Global compactness at Escobar multiples with quantitative reduced expansion)
.

Assume
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
and
(BG
3+
)
and suppose
n\geq 5
.
Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
, set
q=2^{*}_{\partial}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-2}
, and consider the constraint slice

\mathcal{S}:=\Big\{u\in H^{1}(M):\ \|u\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1\Big\}.

Let
(u_{\ell})\subset\mathcal{S}
be a positive Palais–Smale sequence for
\mathcal{E}_{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
with

\mathcal{E}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\ \longrightarrow\ k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\,S_{\ast}\qquad(\ell\to\infty),

and let
\lambda_{\ell}
be the associated multipliers. Then

\lambda_{\ell}\ \longrightarrow\ \lambda_{\infty}:=k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\,S_{\ast},

and the Struwe decomposition of Theorem
4.19
holds: after passing to a subsequence there exist
a (possibly trivial) solution
u_{\infty}
, an integer
J\in\{0,1,\dots,k\}
, boundary centers
x_{\ell}^{j}\in\partial M
,
scales
\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j}\downarrow 0

(j=1,\dots,J)
, and
w_{\ell}\to 0
in
H^{1}(M)
such that

u_{\ell}\ =\ u_{\infty}\ +\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}U^{(j)}_{\ell}\ +\ w_{\ell},

where each
U^{(j)}_{\ell}
is a boundary bubble profile.
Moreover, every extracted bubble has equal boundary
L^{q}
–mass

m_{j}:=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{\partial M}|U^{(j)}_{\ell}|^{q}\,d\sigma_{g}=\Big(\frac{S_{\ast}}{\lambda_{\infty}}\Big)^{n-1}=\frac{1}{k}\qquad(j=1,\dots,J),

so
J\leq k
, with
J=k
whenever
u_{\infty}\equiv 0
.
(If, in addition, bubbling occurs in the separated/no–tower regime, one may choose the extracted parameters so that

\frac{d_{g}(x_{\ell}^{i},x_{\ell}^{j})}{\varepsilon_{\ell}^{i}+\varepsilon_{\ell}^{j}}\to\infty
for
i\neq j
.)

Assume now that we are in the
pure bubbling
case
u_{\infty}\equiv 0
(hence
J=k
).
Assume in addition that
(u_{\ell})
consists of
positive constrained critical points
on
\mathcal{S}

(equivalently: smooth positive Escobar Euler–Lagrange solutions normalized by
\|u_{\ell}\|_{L^{q}(\partial M)}=1
),
that the
refined hypothesis
(
4.17
) holds in fixed collars around the bubble centers
(so that Theorem
4.8
(ii)
applies there and
\mathfrak{R}_{g}
is defined on those collars),
and that bubbling occurs in the separated/no–tower regime so that (after passing to a further subsequence if needed)
the Lyapunov–Schmidt parametrization (Lemma
4.4
with this
k
) applies.

Then, after reparametrizing the decomposition by the Lyapunov–Schmidt map (Lemma
4.4
with this
k
),
there exist parameters

(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell}):=\big((x_{\ell,1},\dots,x_{\ell,k}),(\varepsilon_{\ell,1},\dots,\varepsilon_{\ell,k})\big)

satisfying the separation condition (
4.7
) for some fixed
\Lambda\gg 1
(and all large
\ell
) such that

u_{\ell}=\mathcal{U}_{\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}+w_{\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad\mathcal{E}_{g}[u_{\ell}]=\mathcal{J}_{k}(\mathbf{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\ell}).

Consequently, by Theorem
4.8
(ii)
,

(4.33)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{E}_{g}[u_{\ell}]}{S_{\ast}}

\displaystyle=k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}\;+\;k^{-\frac{2}{q}}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big(\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\,\varepsilon_{\ell,i}+\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2}\,\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x_{\ell,i})\Big)\;+\;\sum_{i\neq j}c_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\varepsilon_{\ell,j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\,\mathsf{G}_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\;O(\Xi_{\ell}),

where the quantitative remainder can be taken as

\Xi_{\ell}:=\omega\!\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\varepsilon_{\ell,i}^{2}\;+\;\sum_{i\neq j}(\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\varepsilon_{\ell,j})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\Big(\Lambda^{-1}+\frac{\varepsilon_{\ell,i}+\varepsilon_{\ell,j}}{d_{g}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})}\Big),

and the implicit constant in
O(\Xi_{\ell})
depends only on
(M,g,n)
and
k
(and monotonically on
\Lambda
), but is
uniform in
\ell
.

In particular, since
n\geq 5
, on any macroscopically separated family

\inf_{\ell}\min_{i\neq j}d_{\partial}(x_{\ell,i},x_{\ell,j})>0
with comparable scales
\varepsilon_{\ell,i}\sim\varepsilon_{\ell}
,
the interaction sum in (
4.33
) is
O(\varepsilon_{\ell}^{n-2})=o(\varepsilon_{\ell}^{2})
and may be
absorbed into
O(\Xi_{\ell})
at the
\varepsilon^{2}
–level.

Remark 4.28

(3D)
.

For
n=3
, the Struwe decomposition and quantization at the levels
k^{\,1-\frac{2}{q}}S_{\ast}

still hold (Theorem
4.19
). However, next-order selection beyond the leading order is
interaction-dominated and requires a coupled analysis.

Theorem 4.29

(Threshold compactness from positive renormalized mass)
.

Assume
n\geq 5
,
(Y
{}^{+}_{\partial}
)
, and
(BG
3+
)
. Suppose

C_{\mathrm{Esc}}^{*}(M,g)=S_{\ast}\quad\text{and}\quad\min_{x\in\mathrm{Crit}(H_{g})}\ \mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\ >\ 0,

with
H_{g}
Morse on
\partial M
. Then every sequence of positive constrained critical points

u_{\ell}\in\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{J}_{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
(equivalently of
\mathcal{E}_{g}
on
\mathcal{S}
) with

\mathcal{E}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to S_{\ast}

is precompact in
H^{1}(M)
; in particular, no bubbling occurs at the threshold.

Note.
Since
n\geq 5
, the coefficient
\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}>0
by Lemma
2.42
.

Proof.

Let
(u_{\ell})
be positive constrained critical points on the slice
\mathcal{S}

with
\mathcal{E}_{g}[u_{\ell}]\to S_{\ast}
. Suppose by contradiction that
(u_{\ell})
blows up.

Step 1 (One-bubble decomposition at the threshold).

By the global compactness theorem (Theorem
4.19
with
k=1
), blow-up implies there exist
centers
x_{\ell}\in\partial M
, scales
\varepsilon_{\ell}\downarrow 0
, and
w_{\ell}\to 0
in
H^{1}(M)
such that

u_{\ell}=\mathcal{U}_{x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell}}+w_{\ell},\qquad\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}:=t(x,\varepsilon)\,U_{+,x,\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{S}.

After rechoosing
(x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell})
via the one-bubble Lyapunov–Schmidt parametrization
(Lemma
4.4
with
k=1
), we may assume
w_{\ell}=w_{x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell}}
is the corresponding correction.

Step 2 (Asymptotic balanced scale equation).

On the constraint slice
\mathcal{S}
one has
\mathcal{E}_{g}=\mathcal{J}_{g}=\mathcal{N}_{g}^{\circ}
.
Since
u_{\ell}
are constrained critical points, the associated one-bubble reduced functional

\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon):=\mathcal{J}_{g}\big(\mathcal{U}_{x,\varepsilon}+w_{x,\varepsilon}\big)

is stationary at
(x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell})
(Lemma
4.7
with
k=1
); in particular,

\partial_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}_{1}(x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell})=0.

Using the one-bubble second-order expansion (Theorem
3.6
),

\frac{\mathcal{J}_{1}(x,\varepsilon)}{S_{\ast}}=1+\rho_{n}^{\mathrm{conf}}H_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon+\mathfrak{R}_{g}(x)\,\varepsilon^{2}+o(\varepsilon^{2})\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow 0),

(if
n\geq 6
, the remainder improves to
O(\varepsilon^{3})
) we differentiate in
\varepsilon
at
(x_{\ell},\varepsilon_{\ell})
to obtain

(4.34)
